Navier-Stokes-Cahn-Hilliard equations on evolving surfaces Thanks: Mathematics Institute, Zeeman Building, University of Warwick, Coventry CV4 7AL, UK, tom.sales@warwick.ac.uk
Charles M. Elliott

Abstract
We derive a system of equations which can be seen as an evolving surface version of the diffuse interface “Model H” of Hohenberg and Halperin. We then consider the well-posedness for the corresponding (tangential) system when one prescribes the evolution of the surface. This well-posedness theory is considered for smooth potentials with polynomial growth, and a thermodynamically relevant singular potential - with some restrictions on initial data in the latter case.



1
Introduction

The Navier-Stokes-Cahn-Hilliard system on a sufficiently smooth, closed, oriented, evolving hypersurface,
(\Gamma(t))_{t\in[0,T]}\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
, is given by

\displaystyle\rho\partial^{\bullet}\mathbf{u}=-\nabla_{\Gamma}p+pH\boldsymbol{\nu}+\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot(2\eta(\varphi)\mathbb{E}(\mathbf{u}))-\varepsilon\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot(\nabla_{\Gamma}\varphi\otimes\nabla_{\Gamma}\varphi)+\mathbf{F},

(1.1)

\displaystyle\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot\mathbf{u}=0,

(1.2)

\displaystyle\partial^{\bullet}\varphi=\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot(M(\varphi)\nabla_{\Gamma}\mu),

(1.3)

\displaystyle\mu=-\varepsilon\Delta_{\Gamma}\varphi+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}F^{\prime}(\varphi),

(1.4)

where

\mathbb{E}(\mathbf{u})=\frac{1}{2}\left(\nabla_{\Gamma}\mathbf{u}+(\nabla_{\Gamma}\mathbf{u})^{T}\right)

is the rate of strain tensor.
We assume a matched density and so we denote the constant density by
\rho
, the velocity of the surface is denoted by
\mathbf{u}
which will often be decomposed as
\mathbf{u}=\mathbf{u}_{\nu}+\mathbf{u}_{T}
, where
\mathbf{u}_{\nu},\mathbf{u}_{T}
are the normal and tangential components respectively.
The associated pressure is denoted as
p
,
\eta(\cdot)
is the variable viscosity which depends on
\varphi
, and
\mathbf{F}
is some external force.
We have split the fourth order Cahn-Hilliard equation into two second order equations (
1.3
), (
1.4
) for
\varphi,\mu
where
\mu
is the associated chemical potential.
In our derivations we consider a non-constant mobility function
M(\cdot)
, but our analysis will consider a constant mobility
M(\cdot)\equiv 1
.
Lastly,
\boldsymbol{\nu}
denotes the outer unit normal to
\Gamma(t)
, and
H=\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot\boldsymbol{\nu}
the mean curvature of
\Gamma(t)
- with the convention that a sphere has positive mean curvature.
The differential operators used will be discussed below.

As in
[
24
,
34
,
44
]
, one may be interested in this system where the normal component of the surface evolution is known a priori.
That is,
\mathbf{u}_{\nu}=V_{N}\boldsymbol{\nu}
for some, sufficiently smooth, known function
V_{N}
.
For our theory, it is sufficient to assume that
V_{N}
is a
C^{3}
function, and so we are working on a
C^{3}
evolving surface,
\Gamma(t)
.
In this case one obtains the tangential Navier-Stokes-Cahn-Hilliard system

\displaystyle\rho\left(\mathbb{P}\partial^{\circ}\mathbf{u}_{T}+(\nabla_{\Gamma}\mathbf{u}_{T})\mathbf{u}_{T}+V_{N}\mathbb{H}\mathbf{u}_{T}-\frac{1}{2}\nabla_{\Gamma}V_{N}^{2}\right)=-\nabla_{\Gamma}\tilde{p}+\mathbb{P}\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot(2\eta(\varphi)\mathbb{E}(\mathbf{u}_{T}))+\mu\nabla_{\Gamma}\varphi+\mathbf{F}_{T},

(1.5)

\displaystyle\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot\mathbf{u}_{T}=-HV_{N},

(1.6)

\displaystyle\partial^{\circ}\varphi+\nabla_{\Gamma}\varphi\cdot\mathbf{u}_{T}=\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot(M(\varphi)\nabla_{\Gamma}\mu),

(1.7)

\displaystyle\mu=-\varepsilon\Delta_{\Gamma}\varphi+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}F^{\prime}(\varphi).

(1.8)

Here
\tilde{p}=p+\frac{\varepsilon}{2}|\nabla_{\Gamma}\varphi|^{2}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}F(\varphi)
is a modified pressure.
In both systems the pressure, and modified pressure, are unknown due to the incompressibility constraint.

Variants of this model have been considered on (mainly stationary) surfaces in
[
11
,
12
,
42
,
46
,
49
,
51
]
.
The main focus in the existing literature is on the derivation and numerical simulation of such a system, but there has been little consideration for the well-posedness thus far.
As such, our work considers the well-posedness of a somewhat simpler model (surface evolution notwithstanding) which still captures the main features of the coupling of the Navier-Stokes equations with the Cahn-Hilliard equation.
Lastly we note that the model in
[
11
]
considers the influence of a physically relevant bending energy, and the model in
[
46
]
consider a variable density - in accordance with the derivation in
[
2
]
.

Some geometric differential notation

The evolving two dimensional hypersurface
\Gamma
is assumed to be sufficiently smooth with a normal,
\boldsymbol{\nu}
, and normal velocity,
V_{N}\boldsymbol{\nu}
. Geometric quantities and differential operators are defined by

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}=\mathbb{I}-\boldsymbol{\nu}\otimes\boldsymbol{\nu},

\displaystyle\nabla_{\Gamma}\phi=\mathbb{P}\nabla\phi^{e},\quad(\nabla_{\Gamma}\phi)_{i}=\underline{D}_{i}\phi,\quad\nabla_{\Gamma}\mathbf{v}=\mathbb{P}\nabla\mathbf{v}^{e}\mathbb{P},\quad(\nabla_{\Gamma}\mathbf{v})_{i,j}:=\underline{D}_{j}v_{i}

\displaystyle(\nabla_{\Gamma}\mathbf{v}\cdot\mathbf{w})_{i}=\underline{D}_{j}v_{i}w_{j},\quad\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot\mathbf{v}=\text{tr}(\nabla_{\Gamma}\mathbf{v}),\quad\mathbb{H}=\nabla_{\Gamma}\boldsymbol{\nu},

where we have used Einstein summation convention.
Here
\phi
and
\mathbf{v},\mathbf{w}
are scalar and vector fields respectively,
\mathbb{I}
denotes the identity, and
(\cdot)^{e}
denotes an extension onto a neighbourhood of
\Gamma(t)
.
These expressions are independent of the specific choice of extension.
Likewise we use the following notation for the normal time derivatives of scalar and vector fields,

\displaystyle\partial^{\circ}\phi=\frac{\partial\phi^{e}}{\partial t}+V_{N}\nabla\phi^{e}\cdot\boldsymbol{\nu},\quad\partial^{\circ}\mathbf{v}=\frac{\partial\mathbf{v}^{e}}{\partial t}+V_{N}\nabla\mathbf{v}^{e}\cdot\boldsymbol{\nu}

which are again independent of choice of extension. Note that this is the time derivative along a trajectory evolving in the normal direction.
We use
\partial^{\bullet}
to note the time derivative which follows also the physical tangential flow
\mathbf{u}_{T}
,

\displaystyle\partial^{\bullet}\phi=\partial^{\circ}\phi+\mathbf{u}_{T}\cdot\nabla_{\Gamma}\phi,\quad\partial^{\bullet}\mathbf{v}=\partial^{\circ}\mathbf{v}+\nabla_{\Gamma}\mathbf{v}\cdot\mathbf{u}_{T},

On the other hand if we wish to use another tangential flow for a velocity field
\mathbf{w}=V_{N}\boldsymbol{\nu}+\mathbf{w}_{T}
with a tangential vector field
\mathbf{w}_{T}
, we write

\partial_{\mathbf{w}}^{\bullet}\phi=\partial^{\circ}\phi+\nabla_{\Gamma}\phi\cdot\mathbf{w}_{T},\quad\partial_{\mathbf{w}}^{\bullet}\mathbf{v}=\partial^{\circ}\mathbf{v}+\nabla_{\Gamma}\mathbf{v}\cdot\mathbf{w}_{T}.

Throughout we will use the convention that a vector quantity will be denoted in bold, e.g.
\mathbf{v}
, and a tensor quantity will be denoted in blackboard bold, e.g.
\mathbb{P}
.

Applications

On a stationary Euclidean domain, the Navier-Stokes-Cahn-Hilliard equations has found many applications, for example in studying thermocapillary flows
[
9
]
, and spinodal decomposition
[
31
]
.
For further details and applications we refer the reader to
[
8
]
.
A more recent application to a modified version of this system has been to the study chemotaxis, for example in modelling tumour growth
[
37
]
.
In this case there are suitable changes to allow for a transfer of mass - adding further complications.

Another biological application which has been of interest in recent years is in studying lipid bilayer membranes.
It is known that the curvature of the domain influences the dynamics of lipid membranes and enters through a bending energy, for example the Jülicher-Lipowsky energy

E^{\text{H}}[\varphi]=\int_{\Gamma}\frac{1}{2}\kappa(\varphi)(H-H_{0}(\varphi))^{2},

as presented in
[
36
]
.
Here
\kappa(\cdot)
is the bending stiffness, and
H_{0}(\cdot)
is the spontaneous curvature which depends on the diffuse interface - an example being
H_{0}(\varphi)=\Lambda\varphi
, for some curvature coefficient
\Lambda\in\mathbb{R}
.
We refer the reader to
[
23
,
39
]
for a discussion of these mechanisms, and to
[
32
]
for discussion and analysis of diffuse interface models for phase separation on biological membranes.
In
[
28
]
the authors consider a model for the kinetics of a lipid bilayer membrane, coupling the Cahn-Hilliard equation to the stationary Stokes equations in a planar domain.
This model has been extended to surfaces in the recent works
[
11
,
12
]
where the authors also include the contributions of the relevant bending energy, as well as considering the full time-dependent Navier-Stokes equations for the hydrodynamics.

Lastly we mention a different model of interest
[
13
]
, in which the authors consider the Cahn-Hilliard equations coupled with the Navier-Stokes equations on a free surface determined by the Navier-Stokes equation in a bulk domain.
This work also proposes semi-discrete and fully-discrete numerical schemes, and contains numerical examples.

2
Derivation of the surface Navier-Stokes-Cahn-Hilliard system

In this section we provide two derivations (
1.5
)-(
1.8
) - one by conservation laws, and the other by considering a thin film limit.

2.1
Derivation by conservation laws

We follow a similar presentation to that of
[
31
]
and derive (
1.1
)-(
1.4
) by using a balance of microstresses.
Consider a binary mixture of a fluid, with constituent densities
\rho_{1},\rho_{2}
.
The total density

\rho:=\rho_{1}+\rho_{2},

is assumed to be constant.
We define
c_{i}:=\frac{\rho_{i}}{\rho}
to be the corresponding concentration, so that
c_{1}+c_{2}=1
.
Following the assumption of
[
31
]
, we assume that the momenta and kinetic energies of the constituent components are negligible when computed relative to the gross motion of the fluid.
As such, we consider the gross velocity,
\mathbf{u}
, in our derivation instead of the velocities of each component.
By considering the total momentum of the fluid in an arbitrary region it is clear to see that

\mathbf{u}=c_{1}\mathbf{u}_{1}+c_{2}\mathbf{u}_{2},

where
\mathbf{u}_{i}
is the velocity of component with density
\rho_{i}
.
Throughout we consider an arbitrary material portion
\Sigma(t)\subset\Gamma(t)

whose boundary,
\partial\Sigma(t)
, moves with conormal material velocity of the surface fluid
\mathbf{u}_{T}\cdot\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\Sigma}
, where
\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\Sigma}
denotes the unit conormal vector for
\partial\Sigma(t)
(the outward unit normal vector which is tangential to
\Sigma(t)
).

Since
\rho
is constant, conservation of mass within the material region
\Sigma(t)
yields

0=\frac{d}{dt}\int_{\Sigma(t)}\rho=\rho\frac{d}{dt}\int_{\Sigma(t)}1

.
Applying the transport theorem we obtain that

0=\int_{\Sigma(t)}\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot\mathbf{v},

and since
\Sigma
is arbitrary this yields

\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot\mathbf{v}=0,\ \mathrm{on}\ \Gamma(t).

This shows that the material surface
\Gamma(t)
has the property of
local inextensiblity
, that is to say
\frac{d}{dt}|\Sigma(t)|=0
for all
\Sigma(t)\subset\Gamma(t)
such that the boundary moves with conormal material velocity
\mathbf{u}_{T}\cdot\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\Sigma}
.
As a consequence we have the property that the total area is preserved

|\Gamma(t)|=|\Gamma_{0}|,

for all
t\in[0,T]
.

For each component,
u_{i}
, we consider the mass balance

\frac{d}{dt}\int_{\Sigma(t)}c_{i}=-\int_{\partial\Sigma(t)}\mathbf{q}_{i}\cdot\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\Sigma},

where
\mathbf{q}_{i}
is some flux vector to be determined.
The normal component of
\mathbf{q}_{i}
does not contribute to the flux, and hence we assume
\mathbf{q}_{i}
is purely tangential.
Using the transport theorem, along with the incompressibility above we find that

\frac{d}{dt}\int_{\Sigma(t)}c_{i}=\int_{\Sigma(t)}\partial^{\bullet}c_{i}.

Using integration by parts on the boundary integral, one obtains

\int_{\partial\Sigma(t)}\mathbf{q}_{i}\cdot\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\Sigma}=\int_{\Sigma(t)}\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot\mathbf{q}_{i}-\int_{\Sigma(t)}\mathbf{q}_{i}\cdot\boldsymbol{\nu}H=\int_{\Sigma(t)}\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot\mathbf{q}_{i},

where
H
denotes the mean curvature.
Thus we obtain an equation for
u_{i}
,

\partial^{\bullet}c_{i}=-\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot\mathbf{q}_{i},\quad\text{on }\Gamma(t).

We define quantities
\varphi=c_{1}-c_{2},\mathbf{q}=\mathbf{q}_{1}-\mathbf{q}_{2}
, and observe that

\displaystyle\partial^{\bullet}\varphi=-\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot\mathbf{q},

(2.1)

where we choose
\mathbf{q}
later.
Since
c_{1}+c_{2}=1
, we can revert back to the individual concentrations by

c_{1}=\frac{1+\varphi}{2},\quad c_{2}=\frac{1-\varphi}{2}.

Lastly, one considers the linear momentum balance for the gross momentum, that is

\frac{d}{dt}\int_{\Sigma(t)}\rho\mathbf{u}=\int_{\partial\Sigma(t)}\mathbb{T}\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\Sigma}+\int_{\Sigma(t)}\mathbf{F},

where
\mathbb{T}
is the Cauchy stress tensor describing the stresses across the surface.
Integrating by parts together with
\Sigma(t)
being arbitrary to

\displaystyle\rho\partial^{\bullet}\mathbf{u}=\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot\mathbb{T}+\mathbf{F},

(2.2)

where again there is no term involving
\boldsymbol{\nu}
coming from the integration by parts
1
1

1

However there is, as we emphasise later, still a component of this equation in the normal direction.
as we assume
\mathbb{T}
maps onto the tangent space of
\Gamma(t)
.
Similarly, one can apply standard arguments to show that the balance of angular momentum yields

\displaystyle\mathbb{T}=\mathbb{T}^{T}.

(2.3)

Next we consider a local dissipation inequality for the energy.
For a region
\Sigma(t)
, the energy is given by

\int_{\Sigma(t)}E(\varphi,\nabla_{\Gamma}\varphi,\mathbf{u})=\int_{\Sigma(t)}E_{1}(\mathbf{u})+E_{2}(\varphi,\nabla_{\Gamma}\varphi)

where

E_{1}(\mathbf{z})=\frac{\rho}{2}|\mathbf{z}|^{2},\quad E_{2}(x,\mathbf{z})=\frac{\varepsilon}{2}|\mathbf{z}|^{2}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}F(x).

We assume, as in
[
2
,
31
]
, that there is a local dissipation inequality given by

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\int_{\Sigma(t)}E\leq\int_{\partial\Sigma(t)}\mathbb{T}\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\Sigma}\cdot\mathbf{u}+\int_{\partial\Sigma(t)}\partial^{\bullet}\varphi\,\boldsymbol{\xi}\cdot\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\Sigma}-\int_{\partial\Sigma(t)}\mu\,\mathbf{q}\cdot\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\Sigma}+\int_{\Gamma(t)}\mathbf{F}\cdot\mathbf{u},

(2.4)

where
\mu
is the difference of the chemical potentials,
\mu_{i}
, of each component, and
\boldsymbol{\xi}
is a stress (which we assume exists as in
[
2
,
31
]
) characterising the microforces across the boundary of a region - and acts only in the tangential direction.
This inequality is understood as being an appropriate form of the second law of thermodynamics.
The boundary terms correspond to the work done by the macroscopic stresses in the fluid, the work done by the microscopic stresses, and the change of potential energy respectively.

As
\Sigma
is arbitrary, we find that on
\Gamma(t)
one has

\partial^{\bullet}E-\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot\left(\mathbb{T}\mathbf{u}\right)-\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot\left(\partial^{\bullet}\varphi\boldsymbol{\xi}\right)+\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot\left(\mu\mathbf{q}\right)-\mathbf{F}\cdot\mathbf{u}\leq 0,

where we have used (
2.3
).
We use (
2.2
) and the form of
E
to see that this implies

\rho\partial^{\bullet}\mathbf{u}\cdot\mathbf{u}+\varepsilon\nabla_{\Gamma}\varphi\cdot\partial^{\bullet}\nabla_{\Gamma}\varphi+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}F^{\prime}(\varphi)\partial^{\bullet}\varphi-\rho\partial^{\bullet}\mathbf{u}\cdot\mathbf{u}-\mathbb{T}:\nabla_{\Gamma}\mathbf{u}-\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot\left(\partial^{\bullet}\varphi\boldsymbol{\xi}\right)+\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot\left(\mu\mathbf{q}\right)-\mathbf{F}\cdot\mathbf{u}\leq 0.

The stress
\boldsymbol{\xi}
is understood to have a microforce balance on an arbitrary region
\Sigma(t)
, given by

\int_{\partial\Sigma(t)}\boldsymbol{\xi}\cdot\boldsymbol{\nu}_{\Sigma}=\int_{\Sigma(t)}\sigma,

from which we obtain

\displaystyle\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot\boldsymbol{\xi}+\sigma=0,\text{ on }\Gamma(t),

(2.5)

where
\sigma
is a scalar function representing the internal forces in the surface.
Lastly we write
\mathbb{S}=\mathbb{T}+p\mathbb{P}
, where
\mathbb{P}
is the projection tensor, and
{p=-\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{tr}(\mathbb{T})}
is the pressure.
We note that
\mathbb{S}
maps onto tangent vectors, is trace-free, and we find

\mathbb{P}:\nabla_{\Gamma}\mathbf{u}=\mathbb{I}:\nabla_{\Gamma}\mathbf{u}-\boldsymbol{\nu}\otimes\boldsymbol{\nu}:\nabla_{\Gamma}\mathbf{u}=\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot\mathbf{u}=0.

Next by using (
2.1
), (
2.2
), (
2.5
) in (
2.4
) it is straightforward to see that

(\varepsilon\nabla_{\Gamma}\varphi-\boldsymbol{\xi})\cdot\partial^{\bullet}\nabla_{\Gamma}\varphi+\left(\sigma+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}F^{\prime}(\varphi)-\mu\right)\partial^{\bullet}\varphi-\left(\mathbb{S}+\nabla_{\Gamma}\varphi\otimes\boldsymbol{\xi}\right):\nabla_{\Gamma}\mathbf{u}+\mathbf{q}\cdot\nabla_{\Gamma}\mu-\mathbf{F}\cdot\mathbf{u}\leq 0,

where we have also used

\nabla_{\Gamma}(\partial^{\bullet}\varphi)=\partial^{\bullet}\nabla_{\Gamma}\varphi+(\nabla_{\Gamma}\mathbf{u})^{T}\nabla_{\Gamma}\varphi.

As noted in
[
2
,
31
]
the quantity

-(\varepsilon\nabla_{\Gamma}\varphi-\boldsymbol{\xi})\cdot\partial^{\bullet}\nabla_{\Gamma}\varphi-\left(\sigma+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}F^{\prime}(\varphi)-\mu\right)\partial^{\bullet}\varphi+\left(\mathbb{S}+\nabla_{\Gamma}\varphi\otimes\boldsymbol{\xi}\right):\nabla_{\Gamma}\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{q}\cdot\nabla_{\Gamma}\mu-\mathbf{F}\cdot\mathbf{u}=:\mathcal{D},

represents the dissipation, and hence the assumed inequality is equivalent to
\mathcal{D}\geq 0
.

One then argues as in
[
2
,
31
]
to show that if one allows
\mathbb{S},\mathbf{q},\boldsymbol{\xi},\sigma
to depend on
\varphi,\nabla_{\Gamma}\varphi,\mu,\nabla_{\Gamma}\mu,\mathbb{E}(\mathbf{u})
arbitrarily then the assumed dissipation inequality can fail to hold.
The argument requires one to assume the presence of general forces and external mass supplies.
In particular one finds that necessarily

\displaystyle\varepsilon\nabla_{\Gamma}\varphi-\boldsymbol{\xi}=0,\quad\sigma+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}F^{\prime}(\varphi)-\mu=0.

(2.6)

We then assume, as in
[
18
]
, that the mass flux takes the form

\mathbf{q}=-M(\varphi)\nabla_{\Gamma}\mu,

for some mobility function
M(\cdot)
.
Similarly, motivated by Newton’s rheological law we assume

\mathbb{S}+\varepsilon\nabla_{\Gamma}\varphi\otimes\nabla_{\Gamma}\varphi=2\eta(\varphi)\mathbb{E}(\mathbf{u}),

where

\mathbb{E}(\mathbf{u})=\frac{1}{2}\left(\nabla_{\Gamma}\mathbf{u}+(\nabla_{\Gamma}\mathbf{u})^{T}\right)

is the rate of strain tensor, and
\eta(\cdot)
is a variable viscosity (depending on the concentration).
As noted in
[
2
]
,
\mathbb{S}+\varepsilon\nabla_{\Gamma}\varphi\otimes\nabla_{\Gamma}\varphi
represents the change in energy due to friction in the fluids, and is referred to as the viscous strain tensor.
In summary, this allows us to observe that

\displaystyle\mathbb{T}=\underbrace{-p\mathbb{P}+2\eta(\varphi)\mathbb{E}(\mathbf{v})}_{\text{Newtonian term}}-\underbrace{\varepsilon\nabla_{\Gamma}\varphi\otimes\nabla_{\Gamma}\varphi}_{\text{Korteweg term}},

(2.7)

where the Newtonian term is the Boussinesq-Scriven ansatz seen in
[
16
]
.
Then by combining (
2.5
), (
2.6
) we find that

\mu=-\varepsilon\Delta_{\Gamma}\varphi+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}F^{\prime}(\varphi),

and similarly combining (
2.1
), and the assumption on
\mathbf{q}
we obtain

\partial^{\bullet}\varphi=\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot(M(\varphi)\nabla_{\Gamma}\mu),

which are the relations for
\varphi,\mu
as in
[
18
]
.
Lastly we observe that

\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot(p\mathbb{P})=\nabla_{\Gamma}p-pH\boldsymbol{\nu},

and hence by using (
2.2
), (
2.7
) we see

\rho\partial^{\bullet}\mathbf{u}=-\nabla_{\Gamma}p+pH\boldsymbol{\nu}+\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot(2\eta(\varphi)\mathbb{E}(\mathbf{u}))-\varepsilon\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot(\nabla_{\Gamma}\varphi\otimes\nabla_{\Gamma}\varphi)+\mathbf{F}.

Lastly by recalling that
\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot\mathbf{u}=0
, we observe that we have derived (
1.1
) - (
1.4
).

To obtain the tangential Navier-Stokes-Cahn-Hilliard system we assume that the geometric motion of
\Gamma(t)
is defined by the normal velocity field

V_{N}\boldsymbol{\nu}
so material continuity in the normal direction implies the equation

\mathbf{u}\cdot\boldsymbol{\nu}=V_{N}.

To find the unknown
\mathbf{u}_{T}
one considers the projection of this momentum equation.
For this we note that (as in
[
45
]
)

\displaystyle\partial^{\bullet}\varphi=\partial^{\circ}\varphi+\nabla_{\Gamma}\varphi\cdot\mathbf{u}_{T},

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\partial^{\bullet}\mathbf{v}=\mathbb{P}\partial^{\circ}\mathbf{v}_{T}+(\nabla_{\Gamma}\mathbf{v}_{T})\mathbf{u}_{T}+V_{N}\mathbb{H}\mathbf{v}_{T}-\frac{1}{2}\nabla_{\Gamma}V_{N}^{2}.

Similarly we compute

\displaystyle\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot(\nabla_{\Gamma}\varphi\otimes\nabla_{\Gamma}\varphi))=\Delta_{\Gamma}\varphi\nabla_{\Gamma}\varphi+\frac{1}{2}\nabla_{\Gamma}|\nabla_{\Gamma}\varphi|^{2}-\left(\nabla_{\Gamma}\varphi\cdot\mathbb{H}\nabla_{\Gamma}\varphi\right)\boldsymbol{\nu},

and by using (
1.4
) we see

-\varepsilon\Delta_{\Gamma}\varphi\nabla_{\Gamma}\varphi=\left(\mu-\frac{1}{\varepsilon}F^{\prime}(\varphi)\right)\nabla_{\Gamma}\varphi.

Thus defining the modified pressure to be

\tilde{p}=p+\frac{\varepsilon}{2}|\nabla_{\Gamma}\varphi|^{2}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}F(\varphi),

and recalling that
\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot\mathbf{v}_{T}=-HV_{N}
we obtain the tangential Navier-Stokes-Cahn-Hilliard equations (
1.5
) - (
1.8
).
The obvious modification to the calculations in
[
45
]
also yields an equation for the normal component

\displaystyle\partial^{\bullet}V_{N}=-2\eta(\varphi)\left(\text{tr}(\mathbb{H}\nabla_{\Gamma}\mathbf{u}_{T})+V_{N}\text{tr}(\mathbb{H}^{2})\right)+\mathbf{u}_{T}\cdot\mathbb{H}\mathbf{u}_{T}-\mathbf{u}_{T}\cdot\nabla_{\Gamma}V_{N}+pH-\varepsilon\nabla_{\Gamma}\varphi\cdot\mathbb{H}\nabla_{\Gamma}\varphi+F_{\nu},

(2.8)

where
F_{\nu}=\mathbf{F}\cdot\nu
, which must be satisfied and coupled with (
1.5
)-(
1.8
) .

Remark 2.1
.

(
1.1
)-(
1.4
) is a simplified form of the system derived in
[
11
]
, where the authors also consider the effects of bending/friction.
It is useful to note that these authors consider the modified pressure throughout.
Hence neglecting the effects of bending/friction terms and changing notation suitably one finds the two systems are identical.

2.2
Derivation by a thin film limit

In this subsection we consider the thin film limit of relevant Navier-Stokes-Cahn-Hilliard equations on an evolving Cartesian domain.
This approach has been considered for the heat equation
[
41
]
, and the Navier-Stokes equations
[
16
,
40
]
.
Besides use in derivation of a suitable system of surface Navier-Stokes-Cahn-Hilliard equations there has been interest in using a thin film approximation numerically
[
51
]
to study the limiting surface equations.

We assume throughout that
\Gamma(t)
does not undergo a change in topology.
Indeed, in the presence of a change in topology the modelling of this phenomenon is different and so one expects the systems (
1.1
)-(
1.4
) and (
1.5
)-(
1.8
) won’t necessarily make sense.
We discuss this more in Remark
2.5
.
We also ignore the effect of the external force,
\mathbf{F}
, for brevity.

As before we still consider a closed oriented evolving surface,
\Gamma(t)
, with a prescribed normal velocity
V_{N}
.
We define
\Omega_{\gamma}(t)
by

\Omega_{\gamma}(t):=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{3}\mid|d(x,t)|<\gamma\},

where
d(x,t)
is the signed distance function of
\Gamma(t)
, and
\gamma>0
is sufficiently small.
We then define the (noncylindrical) space-time domain

Q_{\gamma,T}:=\bigcup_{t\in[0,T]}\Omega_{\gamma}(t)\times\{t\},

where we pose our problem.
We consider a Navier-Stokes-Cahn-Hilliard system on
Q_{\gamma,T}
,

\displaystyle\rho\left(\frac{\partial\mathbf{u}^{\gamma}}{\partial t}+(\mathbf{u}^{\gamma}\cdot\nabla)\mathbf{u}^{\gamma}\right)=-\nabla p^{\gamma}+\nabla\cdot(2\eta(\varphi^{\gamma})\mathbb{E}_{\Omega}(\mathbf{u}^{\gamma}))-\varepsilon\nabla\cdot(\nabla\varphi^{\gamma}\otimes\nabla\varphi^{\gamma}),

(2.9)

\displaystyle\nabla\cdot\mathbf{u}^{\gamma}=0,

(2.10)

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\varphi^{\gamma}}{\partial t}-\nabla\cdot\left(M(\varphi^{\gamma})\nabla\mu^{\gamma}\right)+\mathbf{u}^{\gamma}\cdot\nabla\varphi^{\gamma}=0,

(2.11)

\displaystyle\mu^{\gamma}=-\varepsilon\Delta\varphi^{\gamma}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}F^{\prime}(\varphi^{\gamma}),

(2.12)

equipped with boundary conditions

\displaystyle\mathbf{u}^{\gamma}\cdot\boldsymbol{\nu}^{\gamma}=V_{N}^{\gamma},

(2.13)

\displaystyle\left[\mathbb{E}_{\Omega}(\mathbf{u}^{\gamma})\boldsymbol{\nu}^{\gamma}\right]_{\text{tan}}=0,

(2.14)

\displaystyle\nabla\varphi^{\gamma}\cdot\boldsymbol{\nu}^{\gamma}=0,

(2.15)

\displaystyle\nabla\mu^{\gamma}\cdot\boldsymbol{\nu}^{\gamma}=0,

(2.16)

on the lateral boundary
\partial_{\ell}Q_{\gamma,T}
defined as

\partial_{\ell}Q_{\gamma,T}:=\bigcup_{t\in[0,T]}\partial\Omega_{\gamma}(t)\times\{t\}.

Here we are using the notation
\mathbb{E}_{\Omega}:=\frac{1}{2}(\nabla\mathbf{u}^{\gamma}+\left(\nabla\mathbf{u}^{\gamma})^{T}\right)
for the rate of strain tensor in
\Omega_{\gamma}
, and
[\cdot]_{\text{tan}}
denoting the tangential component to
\partial\Omega_{\gamma}(t)
of a vector in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
.
The condition (
2.14
) is sometimes referred to as the perfect slip condition, and appears as a natural boundary condition.
One may expect different boundary conditions for
\varphi^{\gamma},\mu^{\gamma}
, similar to the Robin-type condition seen for the heat equation in
[
41
]
.
However the usual Neumann conditions are still sufficient our setting, and retain the mass conservation property.
To see this we use the Reynolds transport theorem so that

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\int_{\Omega_{\gamma}(t)}\varphi^{\gamma}

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega_{\gamma}(t)}\frac{\partial\varphi^{\gamma}}{\partial t}+\int_{\partial\Omega_{\gamma}(t)}\varphi^{\gamma}V_{N}^{\gamma}

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega_{\gamma}(t)}\left(\Delta\mu^{\gamma}-\mathbf{u}^{\gamma}\cdot\nabla\varphi^{\gamma}\right)+\int_{\partial\Omega_{\gamma}(t)}\varphi^{\gamma}V_{N}^{\gamma}

\displaystyle=\int_{\partial\Omega_{\gamma}(t)}\left(\nabla\mu^{\gamma}\cdot\boldsymbol{\nu}^{\gamma}-\varphi^{\gamma}\mathbf{u}^{\gamma}\cdot\boldsymbol{\nu}^{\gamma}+\varphi^{\gamma}V_{N}^{\gamma}\right)

\displaystyle=\int_{\partial\Omega_{\gamma}(t)}\nabla\mu^{\gamma}\cdot\boldsymbol{\nu}^{\gamma},

where we have used (
2.13
) for the final equality.
The Neumann condition for
\varphi^{\gamma}
follows in the usual way, without any need for Reynolds transport theorem.
We now expand
\varphi^{\gamma},\mu^{\gamma},\mathbf{u}^{\gamma},p^{\gamma}
in terms of the signed distance function as:

\displaystyle\varphi^{\gamma}(x,t)=\varphi^{0}(\pi(x,t),t)+d(x,t)\varphi^{1}(\pi(x,t),t)+d(x,t)^{2}\varphi^{2}(\pi(x,t),t)+\mathcal{O}(d(x,t)^{3}),

\displaystyle\mu^{\gamma}(x,t)=\mu^{0}(\pi(x,t),t)+d(x,t)\mu^{1}(\pi(x,t),t)+d(x,t)^{2}\mu^{2}(\pi(x,t),t)+\mathcal{O}(d(x,t)^{3}),

\displaystyle\mathbf{u}^{\gamma}(x,t)=\mathbf{u}^{0}(\pi(x,t),t)+d(x,t)\mathbf{u}^{1}(\pi(x,t),t)+d(x,t)^{2}\mathbf{u}^{2}(\pi(x,t),t)+\mathcal{O}(d(x,t)^{3}),

\displaystyle p^{\gamma}(x,t)=p^{0}(\pi(x,t),t)+d(x,t)p^{1}(\pi(x,t),t)+d(x,t)^{2}p^{2}(\pi(x,t),t)+\mathcal{O}(d(x,t)^{3}),

where here
\pi(x,t)
is the closed point projection of
x
onto
\Gamma(t)
.
This is uniquely defined on a small tubular neighbourhood,
\mathcal{N}(\Gamma(t))
, of
\Gamma(t)
.
Hence we have a requirement on
\gamma
being sufficiently small so that

Q_{\gamma,T}\subseteq\bigcup_{t\in[0,T]}\mathcal{N}(\Gamma(t))\times\{t\}.

Before considering the thin film limit we recall some preliminary results.
Firstly, we recall that

\nabla d(x,t)=\boldsymbol{\nu}(\pi(x,t),t),\qquad\frac{\partial d(x,t)}{\partial t}=-V_{N}(\pi(x,t),t),

and from these one can show the following results.

Lemma 2.2

(
[
40
]
, Lemma 2.7)
.

Let
f
be a scalar of vector valued function on
\mathcal{G}_{T}
.
Then the spatial/temporal derivatives of the composite function
f(\pi(x,t),t)
are such that

\displaystyle\nabla(f(\pi(x,t),t))=\nabla_{\Gamma}f(\pi(x,t),t)+d(x,t)\left(\mathbb{H}\nabla_{\Gamma}f\right)(\pi(x,t),t)+\mathcal{O}(d(x,t)^{2}),

\displaystyle\frac{\partial f(\pi(x,t),t)}{\partial t}=\partial^{\circ}f(\pi(x,t),t)+d(x,t)((\nabla_{\Gamma}V_{N}\cdot\nabla_{\Gamma})f)(\pi(x,t),t)+\mathcal{O}(d(x,t)^{2}),

for
(x,t)\in Q_{\gamma,T}
.

Lemma 2.3

(
[
40
]
, Lemma 2.8)
.

Let
\mathbb{S}^{0},\mathbb{S}^{1}
be
3\times 3
matrix valued functions on
\Gamma(t)
for each
t\in(0,T)
.
Then for
x\in\Omega_{\gamma}(t)
set

\mathbb{S}(x)=\mathbb{S}^{0}(\pi(x,t))+d(x,t)\mathbb{S}^{1}(\pi(x,t))+\mathcal{O}(d(x,t)^{2}).

Then we have

\nabla\cdot\mathbb{S}(x)=\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot\mathbb{S}^{0}(\pi(x,t),t)+(\mathbb{S}^{1}(\pi(x,t),t))^{T}\boldsymbol{\nu}(\pi(x,t),t)+\mathcal{O}(d(x,t)),

for
x\in\Omega_{\gamma}(t)
.

Theorem 2.4
.

Let
(\varphi^{\gamma},\mu^{\gamma},\mathbf{u}^{\gamma},p^{\gamma})
solve (
2.9
)-(
2.12
) with boundary conditions (
2.13
)-(
2.16
).
Then
(\varphi^{0},\mu^{0},\mathbf{u}^{0},p^{0},p^{1})
from the corresponding expansion in terms of the signed distance functions solve

\displaystyle\rho\partial^{\bullet}\mathbf{u}^{0}=-\nabla_{\Gamma}p^{0}+p^{1}\boldsymbol{\nu}+\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot(2\eta(\varphi^{0})\mathbb{E}(\mathbf{u}^{0}))-\varepsilon\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot(\nabla_{\Gamma}\varphi^{0}\otimes\nabla_{\Gamma}\varphi^{0}),

(2.17)

\displaystyle\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot\mathbf{v}^{0}=0,

(2.18)

\displaystyle\partial^{\bullet}\varphi^{0}=\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot(M(\varphi^{0})\nabla_{\Gamma}\mu^{0}),

(2.19)

\displaystyle\mu^{0}=-\varepsilon\Delta_{\Gamma}\varphi^{0}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}F^{\prime}(\varphi^{0}),

(2.20)

on
\mathcal{G}_{T}
, such that
\mathbf{u}^{0}\cdot\boldsymbol{\nu}=V_{N}
.

Proof.

We abbreviate
\pi(x,t)
to
\pi
, and
d(x,t)
to
d
throughout this proof.
Firstly by considering (
2.13
) we have
2
2

2

Here the
\pm\gamma
corresponds to the boundary of
\Omega_{\gamma}(t)
consisting of two disjoint sets,
\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{3}\mid d(x,t)=\gamma\},
and
\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{3}\mid d(x,t)=-\gamma\}
.

\mathbf{u}^{0}(\pi,t)\cdot\boldsymbol{\nu}(\pi,t)\pm\gamma\mathbf{u}^{1}(\pi,t)\cdot\boldsymbol{\nu}(\pi,t)+\gamma^{2}\mathbf{u}^{2}(\pi,t)\cdot\boldsymbol{\nu}(\pi,t)+\mathcal{O}(\gamma^{3})=V_{N}(\pi,t),

and so equating terms of order
\gamma^{k}
for
k=0,1,2
, one finds that

\displaystyle\mathbf{u}^{0}(\pi,t)\cdot\boldsymbol{\nu}(\pi,t)=V_{N}(\pi,t),

\displaystyle\mathbf{u}^{1}(\pi,t)\cdot\boldsymbol{\nu}(\pi,t)=0,

\displaystyle\mathbf{u}^{2}(\pi,t)\cdot\boldsymbol{\nu}(\pi,t)=0.

Taking the gradient of
\mathbf{u}^{\gamma}
, we find

\displaystyle\nabla\mathbf{u}^{\gamma}(x,t)=\nabla_{\Gamma}\mathbf{u}^{0}(\pi,t)+\boldsymbol{\nu}(\pi,t)\otimes\mathbf{u}^{1}(\pi,t)+d\left((\mathbb{H}\nabla_{\Gamma}\mathbf{u}^{0})(\pi,t)+\nabla_{\Gamma}\mathbf{u}^{1}(\pi,t)+2(\boldsymbol{\nu}\otimes\mathbf{u}^{2})(\pi,t)\right)+\mathcal{O}(d^{2}),

(2.21)

and hence taking the trace of the above, using (
2.10
) and
\mathbf{u}^{1}\cdot\boldsymbol{\nu}=0
, we obtain

\nabla\cdot\mathbf{u}^{\gamma}=\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot\mathbf{u}^{0}+\mathcal{O}(d),

from which the zeroth order terms yield (
2.18
).
Similar calculations let us verify that

\displaystyle\nabla\varphi^{\gamma}(x,t)=\nabla_{\Gamma}\varphi^{0}(\pi,t)+\varphi^{1}(\pi,t)\boldsymbol{\nu}(\pi,t)+d\left((\mathbb{H}\nabla_{\Gamma}\varphi^{0})(\pi,t)+\nabla_{\Gamma}\varphi^{1}(\pi,t)+2(\varphi^{2}\boldsymbol{\nu})(\pi,t)\right)+\mathcal{O}(d^{2}),

(2.22)

\displaystyle\nabla p^{\gamma}(x,t)=\nabla_{\Gamma}p^{0}(\pi,t)+p^{1}(\pi,t)\boldsymbol{\nu}(\pi,t)+\mathcal{O}(d).

(2.23)

Similarly, by considering the transpose of (
2.21
) one finds

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\Omega}(\mathbf{u}^{\gamma})(x,t)=\mathbb{S}^{0}(\pi,t)+d\mathbb{S}^{1}(\pi,t)+\mathcal{O}(d^{2}),

(2.24)

where

\displaystyle\mathbb{S}^{0}=\mathbb{E}(\mathbf{u}^{0})+\frac{\boldsymbol{\nu}\otimes\mathbf{u}^{1}+\mathbf{u}^{1}\otimes\boldsymbol{\nu}}{2},

\displaystyle\mathbb{S}^{1}=\frac{\mathbb{H}\nabla_{\Gamma}\mathbf{u}^{0}+(\mathbb{H}\nabla_{\Gamma}\mathbf{u}^{0})^{T}}{2}+\mathbb{E}(\mathbf{u}^{1})+\boldsymbol{\nu}\otimes\mathbf{u}^{2}+\mathbf{u}^{2}\otimes\boldsymbol{\nu}.

Now, by our smoothness assumption on
\eta(\cdot)
we may use Taylor’s theorem to write

\eta(\varphi^{\gamma}(x,t))=\eta(\varphi^{0}(\pi,t))+d\eta^{\prime}(\varphi^{*}(\pi,t))\varphi^{1}(\pi,t)+\mathcal{O}(d^{2}),

where
\varphi^{*}(\pi,t)
is some function valued between
\varphi^{0}(\pi,t)
and
\varphi^{\gamma}(x,t)
which arises from the remainder term in Taylor’s theorem.
Using this, we find that

\eta(\varphi^{\gamma}(x,t))\mathbb{E}_{\Omega}(\mathbf{u}^{\gamma})(x,t)=\widetilde{\mathbb{S}}^{0}(\pi,t)+d\widetilde{S}^{1}(\pi,t)+\mathcal{O}(d^{2}),

where

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathbb{S}}^{0}=\eta(\varphi^{0})\left(\mathbb{E}(\mathbf{u}^{0})+\frac{\boldsymbol{\nu}\otimes\mathbf{u}^{1}+\mathbf{u}^{1}\otimes\boldsymbol{\nu}}{2}\right),

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathbb{S}}^{1}=\eta^{\prime}(\varphi^{*})\left(\mathbb{E}(\mathbf{u}^{0})+\frac{\boldsymbol{\nu}\otimes\mathbf{u}^{1}+\mathbf{u}^{1}\otimes\boldsymbol{\nu}}{2}\right)+\eta(\varphi^{0})\left(\frac{\mathbb{H}\nabla_{\Gamma}\mathbf{u}^{0}+(\mathbb{H}\nabla_{\Gamma}\mathbf{u}^{0})^{T}}{2}+\mathbb{E}(\mathbf{u}^{1})+\boldsymbol{\nu}\otimes\mathbf{u}^{2}+\mathbf{u}^{2}\otimes\boldsymbol{\nu}\right).

Hence using Lemma
2.3
we see

\nabla\cdot(2\eta(\varphi^{\gamma})\mathbb{E}_{\Omega}(\mathbf{u}^{\gamma}))=2\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot\widetilde{\mathbb{S}}^{0}+2(\widetilde{\mathbb{S}}^{1})^{T}\boldsymbol{\nu}+\mathcal{O}(d),

and so we check which of these terms vanish.
To do this we firstly note that by rewriting (
2.14
) one has

\mathbb{P}(\pi,t)\mathbb{E}_{\Omega}(\mathbf{u}^{\gamma})\boldsymbol{\nu}(\pi,t)=0,\quad x\in\partial\Omega_{\gamma}(t),

and so by using (
2.24
) one finds

\mathbb{P}(\pi,t){\mathbb{S}}^{0}(\pi,t)\boldsymbol{\nu}(\pi,t)\pm\gamma\mathbb{P}(\pi,t){\mathbb{S}}^{1}(\pi,t)\boldsymbol{\nu}(\pi,t)+\mathcal{O}(\gamma^{2})=0,

and hence

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\pi,t){\mathbb{S}}^{0}(\pi,t)\boldsymbol{\nu}(\pi,t)=0,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\pi,t){\mathbb{S}}^{1}(\pi,t)\boldsymbol{\nu}(\pi,t)=0.

Then by using the form of
\mathbb{S}^{0}
, and

(\boldsymbol{\nu}\otimes\mathbf{u}^{1})\boldsymbol{\nu}=0,\quad(\mathbf{u}^{1}\otimes\boldsymbol{\nu}),\quad\mathbb{P}\mathbf{u}^{1}=\mathbf{u}^{1},

in the above yields

\mathbf{u}^{1}=-2\mathbb{P}(\pi,t)\mathbb{E}(\mathbf{u}^{0})\boldsymbol{\nu}(\pi,t).

Thus we find

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathbb{S}}^{0}

\displaystyle=\eta(\varphi^{0})\mathbb{E}(\mathbf{u}^{0})-\eta(\varphi^{0})(\boldsymbol{\nu}\otimes\boldsymbol{\nu})\mathbb{E}(\mathbf{u}^{0})\mathbb{P}-\eta(\varphi^{0})\mathbb{P}\mathbb{E}(\mathbf{u}^{0})(\boldsymbol{\nu}\otimes\boldsymbol{\nu})

\displaystyle=\eta(\varphi^{0})\mathbb{E}(\mathbf{u}^{0})-\eta(\varphi^{0})(\boldsymbol{\nu}\otimes\boldsymbol{\nu})\mathbb{E}(\mathbf{u}^{0})(\boldsymbol{\nu}\otimes\boldsymbol{\nu})

\displaystyle=\eta(\varphi^{0})\mathbb{E}(\mathbf{u}^{0}),

where we have used
\mathbb{P}=\mathbb{I}-(\boldsymbol{\nu}\otimes\boldsymbol{\nu})
and
\nabla_{\Gamma}\mathbf{u}^{0}=\mathbb{P}\nabla_{\Gamma}\mathbf{u}^{0}\mathbb{P}
.
Hence we find

\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot\widetilde{\mathbb{S}}^{0}=\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot(\eta(\varphi^{0})\mathbb{E}(\mathbf{u}^{0})).

We now show that
(\widetilde{\mathbb{S}}^{1})^{T}\boldsymbol{\nu}=0.

Firstly, we notice that from the above calculations it is clear that

(\widetilde{\mathbb{S}}^{1})^{T}\boldsymbol{\nu}=\eta^{\prime}(\varphi^{*})\mathbb{E}(\mathbf{u}^{0})\boldsymbol{\nu}+\eta(\varphi^{0})\left(\frac{\mathbb{H}\nabla_{\Gamma}\mathbf{u}^{0}\boldsymbol{\nu}+(\mathbb{H}\nabla_{\Gamma}\mathbf{u}^{0})^{T}\boldsymbol{\nu}}{2}+\mathbb{E}(\mathbf{u}^{1})\boldsymbol{\nu}+(\boldsymbol{\nu}\otimes\mathbf{u}^{2})\boldsymbol{\nu}+(\mathbf{u}^{2}\otimes\boldsymbol{\nu})\boldsymbol{\nu}\right).

Recalling that

\mathbb{E}(\mathbf{u}^{0})\boldsymbol{\nu}=0=\mathbb{E}(\mathbf{u}^{1})\boldsymbol{\nu},\quad\mathbb{H}\nabla_{\Gamma}\mathbf{u}^{0}\boldsymbol{\nu}=0=(\mathbb{H}\nabla_{\Gamma}\mathbf{u}^{0})^{T}\boldsymbol{\nu},\quad(\boldsymbol{\nu}\otimes\mathbf{u}^{2})\boldsymbol{\nu}=0,\quad(\mathbf{u}^{2}\otimes\boldsymbol{\nu})\boldsymbol{\nu}=\mathbf{u}^{2},

where we have used
\boldsymbol{\nu}\in\ker(\mathbb{H})
, and
\mathbf{u}^{2}\cdot\boldsymbol{\nu}=0,

we find

(\widetilde{\mathbb{S}}^{1})^{T}\boldsymbol{\nu}=\eta(\varphi^{0})\mathbf{u}^{2}.

Then using the form of
\mathbb{S}^{1}
in
\mathbb{P}(\pi,t){\mathbb{S}}^{1}(\pi,t)\boldsymbol{\nu}(\pi,t)=0
one finds that

\mathbf{u}^{2}=0
, and so
(\widetilde{\mathbb{S}}^{1})^{T}\boldsymbol{\nu}=0
.
Hence

\displaystyle\nabla\cdot(2\eta(\varphi^{\gamma})\mathbb{E}_{\Omega}(\mathbf{u}^{\gamma}))=\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot(2\eta(\varphi^{0})\mathbb{E}(\mathbf{u}^{0}))+\mathcal{O}(d).

(2.25)

The tensor product involving
\nabla\varphi^{\gamma}
is dealt with similarly, where it is straightforward to see that

\nabla\varphi^{\gamma}\otimes\nabla\varphi^{\gamma}=\nabla_{\Gamma}\varphi^{0}\otimes\nabla_{\Gamma}\varphi^{0}+d(\nabla_{\Gamma}\varphi^{1}\otimes\nabla_{\Gamma}\varphi^{0}+\nabla_{\Gamma}\varphi^{0}\otimes\nabla_{\Gamma}\varphi^{1})+\mathcal{O}(d^{2}),

and hence by using Lemma
2.3

\nabla\cdot(\nabla\varphi^{\gamma}\otimes\nabla\varphi^{\gamma})=\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot(\nabla_{\Gamma}\varphi^{0}\otimes\nabla_{\Gamma}\varphi^{0})+(\nabla_{\Gamma}\varphi^{0}\otimes\nabla_{\Gamma}\varphi^{1}+\nabla_{\Gamma}\varphi^{1}\otimes\nabla_{\Gamma}\varphi^{0})\boldsymbol{\nu}+\mathcal{O}(d),

where the latter term clearly vanishes as
\nabla_{\Gamma}\varphi^{0},\nabla_{\Gamma}\varphi^{1}
are tangential.
Thus one obtains

\displaystyle\varepsilon\nabla\cdot(\nabla\varphi^{\gamma}\otimes\nabla\varphi^{\gamma})=\varepsilon\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot(\nabla_{\Gamma}\varphi^{0}\otimes\nabla_{\Gamma}\varphi^{0})+\mathcal{O}(d).

(2.26)

For the momentum equation, all that remains to discuss is the time derivative and the advective term.
The time derivative follows immediately from Lemma
2.2
as

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\mathbf{u}^{\gamma}}{\partial t}(x,t)=\partial^{\circ}\mathbf{u}^{0}(\pi,t)-V_{N}(\pi,t)\mathbf{u}^{1}(\pi,t)+\mathcal{O}(d),

(2.27)

which we want to turn into a material time derivative by considering the advective term.
For the advective term we write
(\mathbf{u}^{\gamma}\cdot\nabla)\mathbf{u}^{\gamma}=(\mathbf{u}^{\gamma})^{T}\nabla\mathbf{u}^{\gamma}.

Hence from the expansion of
\mathbf{u}^{\gamma}
and using Lemma
2.2
one finds

(\mathbf{u}^{\gamma})^{T}\nabla\mathbf{u}^{\gamma}=(\mathbf{u}^{0})^{T}\nabla_{\Gamma}\mathbf{u}^{0}+\mathbf{u}^{1}(\mathbf{u}^{0}\cdot\boldsymbol{\nu})+\mathcal{O}(d)=(\mathbf{u}^{0}\cdot\nabla_{\Gamma})\mathbf{u}^{0}+V_{N}\mathbf{u}^{1}+\mathcal{O}(d).

Hence using this expression for the advection with (
2.27
) one finds

\displaystyle\rho\left(\frac{\partial\mathbf{u}^{\gamma}}{\partial t}+(\mathbf{u}^{\gamma}\cdot\nabla)\mathbf{u}^{\gamma}\right)=\rho\left(\partial^{\circ}\mathbf{u}^{0}+(\mathbf{u}^{0}\cdot\nabla_{\Gamma})\mathbf{u}^{0}\right)+\mathcal{O}(d)=\rho\partial^{\bullet}\mathbf{u}^{0}+\mathcal{O}(d),

(2.28)

where here we understand
\partial^{\bullet}
to mean the derivative along the velocity field given by
V_{N}\boldsymbol{\nu}+\mathbf{u}^{0}
- that is the tangential velocity is only considered up to the zeroth order term.
This point is made clearer by the notation of
[
40
]
where one would write this as
\partial^{\bullet}_{\mathbf{u}^{0}}
.
Now by combining (
2.23
), (
2.25
), (
2.26
), (
2.28
) in (
2.9
) one obtains

\rho\partial^{\bullet}\mathbf{u}^{0}=-\nabla_{\Gamma}p^{0}+p^{1}\boldsymbol{\nu}+\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot(2\eta(\varphi^{0})\mathbb{E}(\mathbf{u}^{0}))-\varepsilon\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot(\nabla_{\Gamma}\varphi^{0}\otimes\nabla_{\Gamma}\varphi^{0})+\mathcal{O}(d),

and as the functions
(\varphi^{0},\mu^{0},\mathbf{u}^{0},p^{0},p^{1})
are independent of
d
one obtains (
2.17
) from the zeroth order terms.

It remains to show that (
2.11
),(
2.12
) give (
2.19
), (
2.20
) at zeroth order.
This is largely the same, so we skim the details.
The advective term in (
2.11
) is the main point of interest here.
Using the expansions for
\varphi^{\gamma},\mathbf{u}^{\gamma}
and Lemma
2.2
one finds

\displaystyle\mathbf{u}^{\gamma}\cdot\nabla\varphi^{\gamma}=\mathbf{u}^{0}\cdot\nabla_{\Gamma}\varphi^{0}+\mathbf{u}^{0}\cdot\boldsymbol{\nu}\varphi^{1}+\mathcal{O}(d).

(2.29)

For the time derivative one uses Lemma
2.2
as before so that

\frac{\partial\varphi^{\gamma}}{\partial t}(x,t)=\partial^{\circ}\varphi^{0}(\pi,t)-V_{N}(\pi,t)\varphi^{1}(\pi,t)+\mathcal{O}(d).

We combine this with (
2.29
), recalling that
\mathbf{u}^{0}\cdot\boldsymbol{\nu}=V_{N}
, so that

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\varphi^{\gamma}}{\partial t}+\mathbf{u}^{\gamma}\cdot\nabla\varphi^{\gamma}=\partial^{\circ}\varphi^{0}+\mathbf{u}^{0}\cdot\nabla_{\Gamma}\varphi^{0}+\mathcal{O}(d)=\partial^{\bullet}\varphi^{0}+\mathcal{O}(d).

(2.30)

It remains to consider the term
\nabla\cdot(M(\varphi^{\gamma})\nabla\mu)
, which is dealt with almost identically to the term
\nabla\cdot(2\eta(\varphi^{\gamma})\mathbb{E}_{\Omega}(\mathbf{u}^{\gamma}))
.
From Lemma
2.2
we see

\nabla\mu^{\gamma}(x,t)=\nabla_{\Gamma}\mu^{0}(\pi,t)+\mu^{1}(\pi,t)\boldsymbol{\nu}(\pi,t)+d\left(\nabla_{\Gamma}\mu^{1}(\pi,t)+2\mu^{2}(\pi,t)\boldsymbol{\nu}(\pi,t)\right)+\mathcal{O}(d^{2}),

and by Taylor’s theorem (assuming
M(\cdot)
is sufficiently smooth)

M(\varphi^{\gamma}(x,t))=M(\varphi^{0}(\pi,t))+dM^{\prime}(\varphi^{*}(\pi,t))\varphi^{1}(\pi,t)+\mathcal{O}(d^{2}),

where we have abused notation and reused the
\varphi^{*}
for the intermediate point arising in the remainder - which is different from the
\varphi^{*}
before, but this does not matter.
Combining these we find

M(\varphi^{\gamma})\nabla\mu^{\gamma}=M(\varphi^{0})\nabla_{\Gamma}\mu^{0}+M(\varphi^{0})\mu^{1}\boldsymbol{\nu}+d\left(M^{\prime}(\varphi^{*})\varphi^{1}\nabla_{\Gamma}\mu^{0}+M^{\prime}(\varphi^{*})\varphi^{1}\mu^{1}\boldsymbol{\nu}+M(\varphi^{0})\nabla_{\Gamma}\mu^{1}+2M(\varphi^{0})\mu^{2}\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)\\
+\mathcal{O}(d^{2}).

Considering the gradient of this expression one finds

\nabla(M(\varphi^{\gamma})\nabla\mu^{\gamma})=\nabla_{\Gamma}(M(\varphi^{0})\nabla_{\Gamma}\mu^{0})+\nabla_{\Gamma}(M(\varphi^{0})\mu^{1})\otimes\boldsymbol{\nu}+M(\varphi^{0})\mu^{1}\mathbb{H}+M^{\prime}(\varphi^{*})\varphi^{1}\boldsymbol{\nu}\otimes\nabla_{\Gamma}\mu^{0}\\
+M^{\prime}(\varphi^{*})\varphi^{1}\mu^{1}\boldsymbol{\nu}\otimes\boldsymbol{\nu}+M(\varphi^{0})\boldsymbol{\nu}\otimes\nabla_{\Gamma}\mu^{1}+2M(\varphi^{0})\mu^{2}\boldsymbol{\nu}\otimes\boldsymbol{\nu}+\mathcal{O}(d),

and taking the trace of the above yields

\nabla\cdot(M(\varphi^{\gamma})\nabla\mu^{\gamma})=\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot(M(\varphi^{0})\nabla_{\Gamma}\mu^{0})+\nabla_{\Gamma}(M(\varphi^{0})\mu^{1})\cdot\boldsymbol{\nu}+M(\varphi^{0})\mu^{1}H+M^{\prime}(\varphi^{*})\varphi^{1}\boldsymbol{\nu}\cdot\nabla_{\Gamma}\mu^{0}\\
+M^{\prime}(\varphi^{*})\varphi^{1}\mu^{1}\boldsymbol{\nu}\cdot\boldsymbol{\nu}+M(\varphi^{0})\boldsymbol{\nu}\cdot\nabla_{\Gamma}\mu^{1}+2M(\varphi^{0})\mu^{2}\boldsymbol{\nu}\cdot\boldsymbol{\nu}+\mathcal{O}(d),

which simplifies to

\displaystyle\nabla\cdot(M(\varphi^{\gamma})\nabla\mu^{\gamma})=\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot(M(\varphi^{0})\nabla_{\Gamma}\mu^{0})+M(\varphi^{0})\mu^{1}H+M^{\prime}(\varphi^{*})\varphi^{1}\mu^{1}+2M(\varphi^{0})\mu^{2}+\mathcal{O}(d).

(2.31)

Now from the boundary condition (
2.16
) one finds

0=\nabla\mu^{\gamma}\cdot\boldsymbol{\nu}^{\gamma}=\nabla_{\Gamma}\mu^{0}\cdot\boldsymbol{\nu}+\mu^{1}\boldsymbol{\nu}\cdot\boldsymbol{\nu}\pm\gamma\left(\nabla_{\Gamma}\mu^{1}\cdot\boldsymbol{\nu}+2\mu^{2}\boldsymbol{\nu}\cdot\boldsymbol{\nu}\right)+\mathcal{O}(\gamma^{2}).

Thus equating terms of the same order in
\gamma
one finds

\displaystyle\nabla_{\Gamma}\mu^{0}\cdot\boldsymbol{\nu}+\mu^{1}=0,

\displaystyle\nabla_{\Gamma}\mu^{1}\cdot\boldsymbol{\nu}+2\mu^{2}=0,

from which one concludes
\mu^{1}=0=\mu^{2}
and hence (
2.31
) becomes

\displaystyle\nabla\cdot(M(\varphi^{\gamma})\nabla\mu^{\gamma})=\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot(M(\varphi^{0})\nabla_{\Gamma}\mu^{0})+\mathcal{O}(d).

(2.32)

By combining (
2.30
), (
2.32
) in (
2.11
) one obtains (
2.19
) by considering the zeroth order terms.

We skip the derivation of (
2.20
) from (
2.12
) and (
2.15
) as it follows the same arguments as we have used so far.
The only point worth mentioning is that we assume the potential
F(\cdot)
is
C^{2}
so that one may indeed use Taylor’s theorem as we have for previous terms.
∎

Remark 2.5
.

1.

The key difference between the systems (
1.1
)-(
1.4
) and (
2.17
)-(
2.20
) is that the latter system has two Lagrange multipliers to be determined,
p^{0}
which is understood as enforcing the divergence free constraint, and
p^{1}
which is understood as enforcing the normal velocity constraint.
This difference is the same as observed in a comparison of various derivations of the evolving surface Navier-Stokes equations in
[
16
]
.
Moreover, this only occurs in the normal direction and has no bearing on our following analysis of the tangential system (
1.5
)-(
1.8
).

2.

As remarked in
[
40
]
the equations (
2.10
) and (
2.18
) imply
3
3

3

Here we are using
|\Omega_{\gamma}(t)|
to denote the
\mathscr{L}^{3}
Lebesgue measure of
\Omega_{\gamma}(t)
, and
|\Gamma(t)|
the
\mathcal{H}^{2}
Hausdorff measure of
\Gamma(t)
.
that

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}|\Omega_{\gamma}(t)|=0,

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}|\Gamma(t)|=0,

respectively.
However, one may also use a corollary of the coarea formula (see
[
27
]
for example) proven in
[
40
]
, Appendix A, to see that

|\Omega_{\gamma}(t)|=\int_{\Omega_{\gamma}(t)}1=\int_{-\gamma}^{\gamma}\int_{\Gamma(t)}J(t;x,r),

where
J(t;x,r)
is a corresponding Jacobian of the form

J(t;x,r)=1-rH(t;x)+r^{2}K(t;x),

where
K(t;x)=\det(\mathbb{H}(t;x))
is the Gaussian curvature of
\Gamma(t)
.
From this one finds

|\Omega_{\gamma}(t)|=2\gamma|\Gamma(t)|+\frac{2\gamma^{3}}{3}\int_{\Gamma(t)}K(t;x),

where one finds the mean curvature term vanishes by using the divergence theorem and the fact that
\Gamma(t)
is closed.
Hence assuming that
\frac{d}{dt}|\Gamma(t)|=0
is not sufficient for the existence of a solution to (
2.17
)-(
2.20
), one also requires

\frac{d}{dt}\int_{\Gamma(t)}K(t;x)=0.

As seen for the evolving surface Euler, and Navier-Stokes equations in
[
40
]
this can be assured by imposing that
\Gamma(t)
does not change its topology as by the Gauss-Bonnet theorem, see
[
35
]
,

\int_{\Gamma(t)}K(t;x)=2\pi\chi(\Gamma(t)),

where
\chi(\Gamma(t))
is the Euler characteristic of
\Gamma(t)
.

2.3
Equivalence of the derived systems

In this subsection we discuss the equivalence of the systems (
1.5
)-(
1.8
) and (
2.17
)-(
2.20
).
Firstly, when considering (
1.5
)-(
1.8
), one notes that in prescribing the normal velocity one does not have to solve (
2.8
) but this equation must still be resolved for the normal component to be given by the prescribed velocity.
Hence there must be some normal force
F_{\nu}\boldsymbol{\nu}
such that the normal velocity one would obtain from (
2.8
) is the prescribed normal velocity
V_{N}
.
With this in mind the form of (
1.1
)-(
1.4
) with a prescribed normal velocity becomes

\displaystyle\rho\partial^{\bullet}\mathbf{u}=-\nabla_{\Gamma}p+pH\boldsymbol{\nu}+\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot(2\eta(\varphi)\mathbb{E}(\mathbf{u}))-\varepsilon\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot(\nabla_{\Gamma}\varphi\otimes\nabla_{\Gamma}\varphi)+F_{\nu}\boldsymbol{\nu},

(2.33)

\displaystyle\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot\mathbf{v}=0,

(2.34)

\displaystyle\partial^{\bullet}\varphi=\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot(M(\varphi)\nabla_{\Gamma}\mu),

(2.35)

\displaystyle\mu=-\varepsilon\Delta_{\Gamma}\varphi+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}F^{\prime}(\varphi).

(2.36)

It is clear to see that one must have

\displaystyle F_{\nu}=\partial^{\bullet}V_{N}+2\eta(\varphi)\left(\text{tr}(\mathbb{H}\nabla_{\Gamma}\mathbf{u}_{T})-V_{N}\text{tr}(\mathbb{H}^{2})\right)-\mathbf{u}_{T}\cdot\mathbb{H}\mathbf{u}_{T}+\mathbf{u}_{T}\cdot\nabla_{\Gamma}V_{N}-pH+\varepsilon\nabla_{\Gamma}\varphi\cdot\mathbb{H}\nabla_{\Gamma}\varphi,

(2.37)

which one can find directly from after solving (
1.5
)-(
1.8
).

Similarly, by considering the normal component of (
2.17
)-(
2.20
) one finds (up to a change of notation)

\displaystyle p^{1}=\partial^{\bullet}V_{N}+2\eta(\varphi)\left(\text{tr}(\mathbb{H}\nabla_{\Gamma}\mathbf{u}_{T})-V_{N}\text{tr}(\mathbb{H}^{2})\right)-\mathbf{u}_{T}\cdot\mathbb{H}\mathbf{u}_{T}+\mathbf{u}_{T}\cdot\nabla_{\Gamma}V_{N}+\varepsilon\nabla_{\Gamma}\varphi\cdot\mathbb{H}\nabla_{\Gamma}\varphi=F_{\nu}+pH.

Thus one finds that we may express (
2.33
)-(
2.36
) as

\displaystyle\rho\partial^{\bullet}\mathbf{u}=-\nabla_{\Gamma}p+p^{1}\boldsymbol{\nu}+\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot(2\eta(\varphi)\mathbb{E}(\mathbf{u}))-\varepsilon\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot(\nabla_{\Gamma}\varphi\otimes\nabla_{\Gamma}\varphi),

\displaystyle\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot\mathbf{v}=0,

\displaystyle\partial^{\bullet}\varphi=\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot(M(\varphi)\nabla_{\Gamma}\mu),

\displaystyle\mu=-\varepsilon\Delta_{\Gamma}\varphi+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}F^{\prime}(\varphi),

which is precisely the form of (
2.17
)-(
2.20
) from the thin film limit (up to a change of notation).
This equivalence will also hold in the presence of some tangential force,
\mathbf{F}_{T}
.

3
Notation, Function Spaces and Inequalities

3.1
Notation

Next we introduce some notation which will be used throughout.
For a
\mathcal{H}^{2}-
measurable set,
X\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
and a function
f\in L^{1}(X)
, we denote
the
\mathcal{H}^{2}
measure of
X
and the mean value of
f
on
X
by

|X|:=\mathcal{H}^{2}(X),\quad\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.83337pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.11674pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.48965pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.31259pt}}\!\int_{X}f:=\frac{1}{|X|}\int_{X}f.

The components of the tangential gradient are denoted by

\nabla_{\Gamma}\phi=(\underline{D}_{1}\phi,\underline{D}_{2}\phi,\underline{D}_{3}\phi).

The (scalar) Sobolev spaces on
\Gamma(t)
are defined by

\displaystyle H^{k,p}(\Gamma(t))=\left\{\phi\in L^{p}(\Gamma(t)\mid\underline{D}_{i}\phi\in H^{k-1,p}(\Gamma(t)),\ i=1,2,3\right\},

and
H^{0,p}(\Gamma(t)):=L^{p}(\Gamma(t)).

We refer the reader to
[
22
]
for further details.
We also use the following notation for tangential vector-valued Sobolev spaces,

\displaystyle\mathbf{L}^{p}(\Gamma(t))=\left\{\boldsymbol{\phi}\in L^{p}(\Gamma(t))^{3}\mid\boldsymbol{\phi}\cdot\boldsymbol{\nu}=0\text{ almost everywhere}\right\},

\displaystyle\mathbf{H}^{k,p}(\Gamma(t))=\left\{\boldsymbol{\phi}\in\mathbf{L}^{p}(\Gamma(t)\mid\underline{D}_{i}\boldsymbol{\phi}\in\mathbf{H}^{k-1,p}(\Gamma(t))\ i=1,2,3\right\},

where
\underline{D}_{i}\boldsymbol{\phi}
denotes the ‘
i
’th column of
\nabla_{\Gamma}\boldsymbol{\phi}
, and
\mathbf{H}^{0,p}(\Gamma(t)):=\mathbf{L}^{p}(\Gamma(t))
.
As is standard, in the case
p=2
we omit the
p
, and write
\mathbf{H}^{k}(\Gamma(t))
.
Similarly we write
\mathbf{H}^{-1}(\Gamma(t))
for the dual space of
\mathbf{H}^{1}(\Gamma(t))
.

3.2
Pushforward map and compatible time dependent spaces

From our assumptions we obtain the existence of a
C^{3}
diffeomorphism

\Phi_{t}^{n}:\Gamma_{0}\rightarrow\Gamma(t),

which is defined as
\Phi_{t}^{n}(x_{0})=x(t)
, where
x(t)
solves

\frac{dx}{dt}=V_{N}(x(t),t)\boldsymbol{\nu}(x(t),t),\quad x(0)=x_{0}.

We denote the corresponding inverse as
\Phi_{-t}^{n}:\Gamma(t)\rightarrow\Gamma_{0}
.
We then may use the framework established in
[
6
,
7
]
, where we use the normal pushforward map defined by
\Phi_{t}^{n}\phi=\phi\circ\Phi_{t}^{n}
, and the pullback
\Phi_{-t}^{n}\psi=\psi\circ\Phi_{-t}^{n}
, for some functions
\phi,\psi
on
\Gamma_{0}
and
\Gamma(t)
respectively.

In can then be shown that we have compatibility of the pairs
(H^{k,p}(\Gamma(t)),\Phi_{t}^{n})
,
(\mathbf{H}^{k,p}(\Gamma(t)),\Phi_{t}^{n})
for
k=0,1,2
,
p\in[1,\infty]
, in the sense of
[
6
]
.
However, a known issue with the associated pushforward map is that is doesn’t necessarily preserve the divergence free properties of solenoidal vector fields on
\Gamma_{0}
.
We remedy this by using the Piola transform as in
[
24
,
45
]
.

3.3
The Piola transform and time differentiation

It is clear that the differentials
D\Phi_{t}^{n}(p):T_{p}\Gamma_{0}\rightarrow T_{\Phi_{t}^{n}(p)}\Gamma(t)
are invertible.
We introduce the notation
J(p,t)=\det(D\Phi_{t}^{n}(p))
,
J^{-1}(x,t)=\det(D\Phi_{-t}^{n}(x))=J(t,\Phi_{-t}^{n}x)^{-1}
,
\mathbb{D}(p,t)=D\Phi_{t}^{n}(p))\mathbb{P}(p,0)
, and
\mathbb{D}^{-1}(x,t)=D\Phi_{-t}^{n}(x))\mathbb{P}(x,t)
.
These matrices are such that
\mathbb{D}\mathbb{D}^{-1}=\mathbb{D}^{-1}\mathbb{D}=\mathbb{P}.

One then defines the operator

\mathbb{A}(p,t):=J^{-1}(\Phi_{t}^{n}(p),t)\mathbb{D}(p,t)+\boldsymbol{\nu}(\Phi_{t}^{n}(p),t)\otimes\boldsymbol{\nu}(p,0),

for
p\in\Gamma_{0}
,
t\in[0,T]
.
One can readily observes that

\mathbb{A}(p,t)|_{T_{p}\Gamma_{0}}:T_{p}\Gamma_{0}\rightarrow T_{\Phi_{t}^{n}(p)}\Gamma(t),\quad\mathbb{A}(p,t)|_{T_{p}\Gamma_{0}}:T_{p}\Gamma_{0}^{\perp}\rightarrow T_{\Phi_{t}^{n}(p)}\Gamma(t)^{\perp}.

We then define the Piola pushforward map, for a vector field
\boldsymbol{\psi}
on
\Gamma_{0}
as

\mathcal{P}_{t}\boldsymbol{\psi}(x)=\mathbb{A}(\Phi_{-t}^{n}(x),t)\boldsymbol{\psi}(\Phi_{-t}^{n}(x)),

where it is known that for some sufficiently smooth, tangential vector field,
\boldsymbol{\psi}
, on
\Gamma_{0}
, then
\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot\boldsymbol{\psi}=0
almost everywhere
\Gamma_{0}
if, and only if,
\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot\mathcal{P}_{t}\boldsymbol{\psi}=0
almost everywhere on
\Gamma(t)
.

One similarly defines an inverse operator
\mathbb{A}^{-1}
by

\mathbb{A}^{-1}(x,t):=J(\Phi_{-t}^{n}(x),t)\mathbb{D}^{-1}(x,t)+\boldsymbol{\nu}(\Phi_{-t}^{n}(x),0)\otimes\boldsymbol{\nu}(x,t),

for
x\in\Gamma(t)
,
t\in[0,T]
.
The Piola pullback is defined as one would expect,

\mathcal{P}_{-t}\boldsymbol{\psi}(p)=\mathbb{A}^{-1}(\Phi_{t}^{n}(p),t)\boldsymbol{\psi}(\Phi_{t}^{n}(p)),

for a vector field
\boldsymbol{\psi}
on
\Gamma(t)
.
As one would expect (and hope) this is such that
\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot\boldsymbol{\psi}=0
almost everywhere on
\Gamma(t)
if, and only if,
\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot\mathcal{P}_{-t}\boldsymbol{\psi}=0
almost everywhere on
\Gamma_{0}
.
We now recall the following result.

Lemma 3.1

(
[
45
]
, Lemma 3.1)
.

We have that
\mathbb{D},\mathbb{A}\in C^{2}(\Gamma_{0}\times[0,T])
, and
\mathbb{D}^{-1},\mathbb{A}^{-1}\in C^{2}(\mathcal{G}_{T})
, and are hence uniformly bounded in space and time.

We note that we have improved regularity compared to the result in
[
45
]
, as we assume
\Phi_{t}^{n}
are
C^{3}
-diffeomorphisms instead of
C^{2}
.

This result is then used to show compatibility, in the sense of
[
7
]
, of the pairs
(\mathbf{H}^{k}(t),\mathcal{P}_{t})
(
k=0,1,2
), and the divergence free space
(\mathbf{V}_{\sigma}(t),\mathcal{P}_{t})
, which we discuss later.
We refer the reader to
[
45
]
for details.
With this compatibility of spaces in hand one can refer to a derivative associated to the Piola transform pushforward/pullback maps, as in the sense of
[
7
]
, defined by

\partial^{*}\boldsymbol{\psi}=\mathcal{P}_{t}\left(\frac{d}{dt}\mathcal{P}_{-t}\boldsymbol{\psi}\right).

We refer to this as the strong Piola derivative.
The utility of this choice of derivative is that for a sufficiently smooth vector field,
\boldsymbol{\psi}
, on
\mathcal{G}_{T}
we have

\boldsymbol{\psi}(t)\cdot\boldsymbol{\nu}=0\Rightarrow\partial^{*}\boldsymbol{\psi}\cdot\boldsymbol{\nu}=0,\qquad\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot\boldsymbol{\psi}(t)=0\Rightarrow\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot\partial^{*}\boldsymbol{\psi}(t)=0.

The corresponding weak Piola derivative is defined in the same way as the weak material/normal derivative (see
[
7
]
).
The normal derivative,
\partial^{\circ}
, and the Piola derivative,
\partial^{*}
, are related through the following.

Lemma 3.2

(
[
45
]
, Lemma 3.6)
.

For
\boldsymbol{\psi}\in\mathcal{D}
we have

\displaystyle\partial^{\circ}\boldsymbol{\psi}=\partial^{*}\boldsymbol{\psi}-\mathbb{A}(\partial^{\circ}\mathbb{A}^{-1})\boldsymbol{\psi},

(3.1)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\partial^{\circ}\boldsymbol{\psi}=\partial^{*}\boldsymbol{\psi}-\mathbb{A}\mathbb{P}(\partial^{\circ}\mathbb{A}^{-1})\boldsymbol{\psi}.

(3.2)

From here on we define
\bar{\mathbb{A}}:=\mathbb{A}\mathbb{P}(\partial^{\circ}\mathbb{A}^{-1})\in C^{1}(\mathcal{G}_{T})
.

3.4
Some bilinear and trilinear forms

Here we introduce some bilinear/trilinear forms to be used in our weak formulation later.

\displaystyle m(t;\phi,\psi)=\int_{\Gamma(t)}\phi\psi,

\displaystyle\mathbf{m}(t;\boldsymbol{\phi},\boldsymbol{\psi})=\int_{\Gamma(t)}\boldsymbol{\phi}\cdot\boldsymbol{\psi},

\displaystyle m_{*}(t;\Lambda,\phi)=\langle\Lambda,\phi\rangle_{H^{-1}(\Gamma(t),H^{1}(\Gamma(t)))},

\displaystyle\mathbf{m}_{*}(t;\boldsymbol{\Lambda},\boldsymbol{\phi})=\langle\boldsymbol{\Lambda},\boldsymbol{\phi}\rangle_{\mathbf{V}_{\sigma}(t)^{\prime},\mathbf{V}_{\sigma}(t)},

\displaystyle a(t;\phi,\psi)=\int_{\Gamma(t)}\nabla_{\Gamma}\phi\cdot\nabla_{\Gamma}\psi,

\displaystyle\mathbf{a}(t;\boldsymbol{\phi},\boldsymbol{\psi})=2\int_{\Gamma(t)}\mathbb{E}(\boldsymbol{\phi}):\mathbb{E}(\boldsymbol{\psi}),

\displaystyle\hat{\mathbf{a}}(t;\phi,\boldsymbol{\psi},\boldsymbol{\chi})=2\int_{\Gamma(t)}\phi\mathbb{E}(\boldsymbol{\psi}):\mathbb{E}(\boldsymbol{\chi}),

\displaystyle\mathbf{c}_{1}(t;\boldsymbol{\phi},\boldsymbol{\psi},\boldsymbol{\chi})=\int_{\Gamma(t)}(\nabla_{\Gamma}\boldsymbol{\phi})\boldsymbol{\psi}\cdot\boldsymbol{\chi},

\displaystyle\mathbf{c}_{2}(t;\phi,\psi,\boldsymbol{\chi})=\int_{\Gamma(t)}\phi\nabla_{\Gamma}\psi\cdot\boldsymbol{\chi},

\displaystyle\mathbf{l}(t;\boldsymbol{\phi},\boldsymbol{\psi})=\mathbf{m}(t;V_{N}\mathbb{H}\boldsymbol{\phi},\boldsymbol{\psi}),

\displaystyle\mathbf{d}_{1}(t;\boldsymbol{\phi},\boldsymbol{\psi})=\mathbf{c}_{1}(t;\boldsymbol{\phi},\widetilde{\mathbf{u}_{T}},\boldsymbol{\psi})+\mathbf{c}_{1}(t;\widetilde{\mathbf{u}_{T}},\boldsymbol{\phi},\boldsymbol{\psi}),

\displaystyle\mathbf{d}_{2}(t;\phi,\boldsymbol{\psi})=\hat{\mathbf{a}}(t;\phi,\boldsymbol{\widetilde{\mathbf{u}_{T}}},\boldsymbol{\psi}),

for sufficiently smooth scalar functions
\phi,\psi
, vector functions
\boldsymbol{\phi},\boldsymbol{\psi},\boldsymbol{\chi}
, and linear functionals
\Lambda\in H^{-1}(\Gamma(t)),\boldsymbol{\Lambda}\in\mathbf{V}_{\sigma}(t)^{\prime}
.
We will omit the
t
argument throughout, as above. We note the following antisymmetry properties of the trilinear forms
\mathbf{c}_{1},\mathbf{c}_{2}
, which one can readily verify by using the divergence theorem,

\displaystyle\mathbf{c}_{1}(\boldsymbol{\phi},\boldsymbol{\phi},\boldsymbol{\chi})=-\mathbf{c}_{1}(\boldsymbol{\chi},\boldsymbol{\phi},\boldsymbol{\phi}),

\displaystyle\mathbf{c}_{1}(\boldsymbol{\phi},\boldsymbol{\phi},\boldsymbol{\phi})=0,

\displaystyle\mathbf{c}_{2}(\phi,\psi,\boldsymbol{\chi})=-\mathbf{c}_{2}(\psi,\phi,\boldsymbol{\chi}),

for
\phi,\psi\in H^{1}(\Gamma(t))
and
\boldsymbol{\phi},\boldsymbol{\chi}\in\mathbf{V}_{\sigma}(t)
.
We will use these throughout.

We relate some of these bilinear forms to the normal derivatives using the transport theorem.

Lemma 3.3

(Transport theorem)
.

1.

Let
\phi,\psi\in H^{1}_{H^{-1}}\cap L^{2}_{L^{2}}
, then

\frac{d}{dt}m(\phi,\psi)=m_{*}(\partial^{\circ}\phi,\psi)+m_{*}(\partial^{\circ}\psi,\phi)+m(\phi,\psi HV_{N}).

Moreover, if we have that
\phi,\psi\in H^{1}_{H^{1}}
then

\frac{d}{dt}a(\phi,\psi)=a(\partial^{\circ}\phi,\psi)+a(\partial^{\circ}\phi,\psi)+b(\phi,\psi),

where

b(t;\phi,\psi):=\int_{\Gamma(t)}V_{N}(H\mathbb{I}-2\mathbb{H})\nabla_{\Gamma}\phi\cdot\nabla_{\Gamma}\psi.

2.

Let
\boldsymbol{\phi},\boldsymbol{\psi}\in H^{1}_{\mathbf{V}_{\sigma}^{\prime}}\cap L^{2}_{\mathbf{V}_{\sigma}}
, then

\frac{d}{dt}\mathbf{m}(\boldsymbol{\phi},\boldsymbol{\psi})=\mathbf{m}_{*}(\partial^{\circ}\boldsymbol{\phi},\boldsymbol{\psi})+m_{*}(\partial^{\circ}\boldsymbol{\psi},\boldsymbol{\phi})+m(\boldsymbol{\phi},\boldsymbol{\psi}HV_{N}).

Moreover, if we have that
\boldsymbol{\phi},\boldsymbol{\psi}\in H^{1}_{\mathbf{V}_{\sigma}}
then

\frac{d}{dt}\mathbf{a}(\boldsymbol{\phi},\boldsymbol{\phi})=a(\partial^{\circ}\boldsymbol{\phi},\boldsymbol{\phi})+a(\boldsymbol{\phi},\partial^{\circ}\boldsymbol{\psi})+\mathbf{b}(\boldsymbol{\phi},\boldsymbol{\psi}),

where

\mathbf{b}(t;\boldsymbol{\phi},\boldsymbol{\psi}):=\int_{\Gamma(t)}V_{N}(H\mathbb{I}-2\mathbb{H})\nabla_{\Gamma}\boldsymbol{\phi}:\nabla_{\Gamma}\boldsymbol{\psi}.

The relevant form for the bilinear form
\mathbf{b}
can be deduced from the identity

\partial^{\circ}\nabla_{\Gamma}\boldsymbol{\phi}=\nabla_{\Gamma}(\partial^{\circ}\boldsymbol{\phi})-(\nabla_{\Gamma}V_{N}\boldsymbol{\nu})^{T}\nabla_{\Gamma}\boldsymbol{\phi},

and noting that
\nabla_{\Gamma}(V_{N}\boldsymbol{\nu})=V_{N}\mathbb{H}+\nabla_{\Gamma}V_{N}\otimes\boldsymbol{\nu}
, where it is clear that

(\nabla_{\Gamma}V_{N}\otimes\boldsymbol{\nu})\nabla_{\Gamma}\boldsymbol{\phi}:\nabla_{\Gamma}\boldsymbol{\psi}=0=(\boldsymbol{\nu}\otimes\nabla_{\Gamma}V_{N})\nabla_{\Gamma}\boldsymbol{\phi}:\nabla_{\Gamma}\boldsymbol{\psi},

due to the presence of the normal vector.

3.5
Preliminary rewriting of the system

In order to set up the weak formulation we rewrite the system (
1.5
)-(
1.8
) in such a way that the unknown
\mathbf{u}_{T}
is divergence free and we may eliminate the pressure.
To do this we consider the unique solution,
\Psi
, of the elliptic PDE

-\Delta_{\Gamma}\Psi(t)=H(t)V_{N}(t),

on
\Gamma(t)
, subject to the constraint
\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.83337pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.11674pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.48965pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.31259pt}}\!\int_{\Gamma(t)}\Psi=0
, for all
t\in[0,T].

We then define
\widetilde{\mathbf{u}_{T}}=\nabla_{\Gamma}\Psi
, from which we see that
\widetilde{\mathbf{u}_{T}}\in\mathbf{H}^{2,p}(\Gamma(t))
for all
p\in[1,\infty)
, and
-\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot\widetilde{\mathbf{u}_{T}}=HV_{N}
.
Then defining
\widehat{\mathbf{u}_{T}}:=\mathbf{u}_{T}-\widetilde{\mathbf{u}_{T}}
we find that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\partial^{\circ}\widehat{\mathbf{u}_{T}}+(\nabla_{\Gamma}\widehat{\mathbf{u}_{T}})\widehat{\mathbf{u}_{T}}+V_{N}\mathbb{H}\widehat{\mathbf{u}_{T}}-\frac{1}{2}\nabla_{\Gamma}V_{N}^{2}=-\nabla_{\Gamma}\tilde{p}+\mathbb{P}\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot(2\eta(\varphi)\mathbb{E}(\widehat{\mathbf{u}_{T}}))+\mu\nabla_{\Gamma}\varphi+\mathbf{F}_{T}-\mathfrak{D}_{1}(\varphi,\mathbf{u}_{T},\widetilde{\mathbf{u}_{T}}),

(3.3)

\displaystyle\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot\widehat{\mathbf{u}_{T}}=0,

(3.4)

\displaystyle\partial^{\circ}\varphi+\nabla_{\Gamma}\varphi\cdot\widehat{\mathbf{u}_{T}}=\Delta_{\Gamma}\mu-\mathfrak{D}_{2}(\varphi,\widetilde{\mathbf{u}_{T}}),

(3.5)

\displaystyle\mu=-\varepsilon\Delta_{\Gamma}\varphi+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}F^{\prime}(\varphi),

(3.6)

where

\displaystyle\mathfrak{D}_{1}(\varphi,\mathbf{u}_{T},\widetilde{\mathbf{u}_{T}})=\mathbb{P}\partial^{\circ}\widetilde{\mathbf{u}_{T}}+(\nabla_{\Gamma}\widetilde{\mathbf{u}_{T}})\widetilde{\mathbf{u}_{T}}+\nabla_{\Gamma}(\widetilde{\mathbf{u}_{T}})\widehat{\mathbf{u}_{T}}+\nabla_{\Gamma}(\widehat{\mathbf{u}_{T}})\widetilde{\mathbf{u}_{T}}+V_{N}\mathbb{H}\widetilde{\mathbf{u}_{T}}-\mathbb{P}\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot(2\eta(\varphi)\mathbb{E}(\widetilde{\mathbf{u}_{T}}))

\displaystyle\mathfrak{D}_{2}(\varphi,\widetilde{\mathbf{u}_{T}})=\nabla_{\Gamma}\varphi\cdot\widetilde{\mathbf{u}_{T}}

This suggest a new “body force”,
\mathbf{B}
, which is defined as

\mathbf{B}=\mathbf{F}_{T}-(\nabla_{\Gamma}\widetilde{\mathbf{u}_{T}})\widetilde{\mathbf{u}_{T}}-\mathbb{P}\partial^{\circ}\widetilde{\mathbf{u}_{T}}-V_{N}\mathbb{H}\widetilde{\mathbf{u}_{T}}.

From Appendix
A
we know
\widetilde{\mathbf{u}_{T}}\in C^{0}_{\mathbf{H}^{2,p}}\cap C^{1}_{\mathbf{L}^{p}}
, for all
p\in[1,\infty)
, and so for
\mathbf{F}_{T}\in L^{2}_{\mathbf{L}^{2}}
one can readily show that
\mathbf{B}\in L^{2}_{\mathbf{L}^{2}}
.

The above reformulation is formal, but for sufficiently smooth
\widetilde{\mathbf{u}_{T}}
we find that this holds in a weak setting.
We show the necessary regularity properties of
\Psi
(and
\widetilde{\mathbf{u}_{T}}
) in Appendix
A
, and will discuss this later.
From here on we will now denote
\widehat{\mathbf{u}_{T}}
as
\mathbf{u}_{T}
and treat this as the unknown velocity.
This formulation allows us to work in the space of divergence free test functions, as is typical in the analysis of the Navier-Stokes equations.
As such we introduce some notation for a suitable space of divergence free functions,

\mathbf{V}_{\sigma}(t):=\left\{\boldsymbol{\phi}\in\mathbf{H}^{1}(\Gamma(t))\mid\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot\boldsymbol{\phi}=0\right\}.

Similarly we define
\mathbf{H}_{\sigma}(t)
to be the following closure in the
\|\cdot\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma(t))}
norm,

\mathbf{H}_{\sigma}(t):=\left\{\boldsymbol{\phi}\in C^{1}(\Gamma(t))^{3}\mid\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot\boldsymbol{\phi}=0,\boldsymbol{\phi}\cdot\boldsymbol{\nu}=0\right\}^{\|\cdot\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma(t))}}.

Moreover, we have compact, dense embeddings

\mathbf{V}_{\sigma}(t){\hookrightarrow}\mathbf{H}_{\sigma}(t){\hookrightarrow}\mathbf{V}_{\sigma}(t)^{\prime}.

The appropriate weak formulation follows from multiplying by a sufficiently smooth, solenoidal test function,
\boldsymbol{\phi}
, in (
3.3
) and a sufficiently smooth test function,
\phi
, in (
3.5
), (
3.6
) and integrate over
\Gamma(t)
.
This yields (
4.1
)-(
4.3
) below.
Notice now that, by using the divergence theorem, one finds the pressure term vanishes as

0=\int_{\Gamma(t)}\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot(\tilde{p}\boldsymbol{\phi})=\int_{\Gamma(t)}\nabla_{\Gamma}\tilde{p}\cdot\boldsymbol{\phi}+\int_{\Gamma({t})}\tilde{p}\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot\boldsymbol{\phi}.

3.6
Inequalities

We end this section by recalling some useful inequalities.
The following results on Sobolev spaces are proven in
[
10
,
33
]
.

Theorem 3.4
.

1.

[Poincaré inequality]

There exists a constant
C_{P}>0
, independent of
t\in[0,T]
, such that for
f\in H^{1}(\Gamma(t))
we have

\left\|f-\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.98003pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.26338pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.6363pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.45924pt}}\!\int_{\Gamma(t)}f\right\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma(t))}\leq C_{P}\|\nabla_{\Gamma}f\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma(t))}.

2.

[Sobolev Embeddings]

(a)

Let
0\leq l\leq k
be two integers, and
1\leq p<q
be two real numbers such that
\frac{1}{q}=\frac{1}{p}-\frac{k-l}{2}
.
Then we have continuous embedding
H^{k,p}(\Gamma(t))\hookrightarrow H^{l,q}(\Gamma(t))
.

(b)

If
\frac{k-r-\alpha}{2}\geq\frac{1}{p}
, where
\alpha\in(0,1)
, then we have continuous embedding
H^{k,p}(\Gamma(t))\hookrightarrow C^{r+\alpha}(\Gamma(t))
.

Notice in particular that this implies

H^{1}(\Gamma(t))\hookrightarrow L^{p}(\Gamma(t)),

for all
p\in[1,\infty)
, and in fact these embeddings are compact.
It can be shown that the operator norm of the above continuous injections are independent of time - which follows from the fact that we consider a compact time interval and sufficiently smooth evolution of
\Gamma(t)
.
We obtain analogous Sobolev embeddings for the spaces
\mathbf{H}^{k,p}(\Gamma(t))
.
We also recall the following inequalities which are used throughout the analysis.

Lemma 3.5

(Ladyzhenskaya’s interpolation inequality,
[
45
]
, Lemma 3.4)
.

For
\phi\in H^{1}(\Gamma(t))
and
\boldsymbol{\phi}\in\mathbf{H}^{1}(\Gamma(t))
we have

\displaystyle\|\phi\|_{L^{4}(\Gamma(t))}\leq C\|\phi\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma(t))}^{\frac{1}{2}}\|\phi\|_{H^{1}(\Gamma(t))}^{\frac{1}{2}},

(3.7)

\displaystyle\|\boldsymbol{\phi}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{4}(\Gamma(t))}\leq C\|\boldsymbol{\phi}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma(t))}^{\frac{1}{2}}\|\boldsymbol{\phi}\|_{\mathbf{H}^{1}(\Gamma(t))}^{\frac{1}{2}},

(3.8)

for a constant
C
independent of
t
.

Lemma 3.6

(Korn’s inequality,
[
45
]
, Lemma 3.2)
.

For
\boldsymbol{\phi}\in\mathbf{H}^{1}(\Gamma(t))
we have

\displaystyle\|\boldsymbol{\phi}\|_{\mathbf{H}^{1}(\Gamma(t))}\leq C\left(\|\boldsymbol{\phi}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma(t))}+\|\mathbb{E}(\boldsymbol{\phi})\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma(t))}\right),

(3.9)

where the constant
C
is independent of
t
.

Remark 3.7
.

In fact, one can show an
L^{p}
version of Korn’s inequality,

\displaystyle\|\boldsymbol{\phi}\|_{\mathbf{H}^{1,p}(\Gamma(t))}\leq C\left(\|\boldsymbol{\phi}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{p}(\Gamma(t))}+\|\mathbb{E}(\boldsymbol{\phi})\|_{\mathbf{L}^{p}(\Gamma(t))}\right),

(3.10)

for
\boldsymbol{\phi}\in\mathbf{H}^{1,p}(\Gamma(t))
and
p\in(1,\infty)
.
The argument for this on a stationary surface follows as in the proof of
[
34
]
Lemma 4.1, where one uses an
L^{p}
version of Korn’s inequality in Euclidean space (see for example
[
21
]
) after considering a local parametrisation of
\Gamma(t)
.
The inequality then extends to an evolving surface with a constant independent of
t
.

In order to establish energy estimates we use the following nonlinear generalisation of the Grönwall inequality.

Lemma 3.8

(Bihari-LaSalle inequality,
[
14
]
)
.

Let
X,K:[0,T]\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
, be non-negative continuous
4
4

4

By density this can be shown to extend to
K\in L^{1}([0,T])
.
functions,
\omega:\mathbb{R}^{+}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{+}
be a decreasing continuous function, and
k\geq 0
.
Then the inequality

X(t)\leq k+\int_{0}^{t}K(s)\omega(X(s))\,ds,

implies the inequality

\displaystyle X(t)\leq\Omega^{-1}\left(\Omega(k)+\int_{0}^{t}K(s)\,ds\right),

(3.11)

where

\Omega(t)=\int_{t_{0}}^{t}\frac{1}{\omega(s)}\,ds,

for some constant
t_{0}
chosen so that

\Omega(k)+\int_{0}^{t}K(s)\,ds\in\text{dom}(\Omega^{-1}).

Finally we recall three results which will be used in proving uniqueness.

Lemma 3.9

(
[
26
]
, Lemma 4.3)
.

Let
z\in H^{-1}(\Gamma(t))
be such that
m_{*}(z,1)=0
.
Define the inverse Laplacian
\mathcal{G}z\in H^{1}(\Gamma(t))\in H^{1}(\Gamma(t))
as the unique solution of

a(\mathcal{G}z,\phi)=m_{*}(z,\phi),

for all
\phi\in H^{1}(\Gamma(t))
. If
z\in H^{1}_{H^{-1}}
then

\|\mathcal{G}z||_{H^{1}_{H^{1}}}\leq C\|z\|_{H^{1}_{H^{-1}}}.

In fact in
[
26
]
it is assumed that
z\in H^{1}_{H^{1}}
but examining the proof it is sufficient to assume
z\in H^{1}_{H^{-1}}
.

Lemma 3.10

(
[
38
]
, Lemma 2.2)
.

Let
m_{1},m_{2},S
be non-negative functions on
(0,T)
such that
m_{1},S\in L^{1}(0,T)
and
m_{2}\in L^{2}(0,T)
, with
S>0
a.e. on
(0,T)
.
Now suppose
f,g
are non-negative functions on
(0,T)
,
f
is absolutely continuous on
[0,T)
, such that
5
5

5

Here we are using the notation
\log^{+}(x):=\max(0,\log(x))
.

\displaystyle f^{\prime}(t)+g(t)\leq m_{1}(t)f(t)+m_{2}(t)\left(f(t)g(t)\log^{+}\left(\frac{S(t)}{g(t)}\right)\right)^{\frac{1}{2}},

(3.12)

holds a.e. on
(0,T)
, and
f(0)=0
.
Then
f(t)\equiv 0
on
[0,T)
.

The final result we mention is an evolving surface analogue of the Brezis-Gallouët-Wainger inequality (which originates from work on the nonlinear Schrödinger equation, see
[
17
]
).

Lemma 3.11
.

For
\phi\in H^{2}(\Gamma(t))
, one has

\displaystyle\|\phi\|_{L^{\infty}(\Gamma(t))}\leq C\|\phi\|_{H^{1}(\Gamma(t))}\left(1+\log\left(1+\frac{C\|\phi\|_{H^{2}(\Gamma(t))}}{\|\phi\|_{H^{1}(\Gamma(t))}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\right),

(3.13)

for constants
C
independent of
t
.

Proof.

From
[
30
]
, Theorem 1.1 we see that for
\phi\in H^{2}(\Gamma_{0})
one has

\|\phi\|_{L^{\infty}(\Gamma_{0})}\leq C\|\phi\|_{H^{1}(\Gamma_{0})}\left(1+\log\left(1+\frac{\|\phi\|_{H^{2}(\Gamma_{0})}}{\|\phi\|_{H^{1}(\Gamma_{0})}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\right),

where examining the proof one finds that our assumption that
\Gamma_{0}
is
C^{3}
is sufficient.
To see that one can choose the constant independent of time we observe that
\|\phi\|_{L^{\infty}(\Gamma(t))}=\|\Phi_{-t}^{n}\phi\|_{L^{\infty}(\Gamma_{0})}.

Hence by pulling back to
\Gamma_{0}
and using the above inequality, the compatibility of the pairs
(H^{k}(\Gamma(t)),\Phi_{t}^{n})
in the sense of
[
7
]
, and the monotonicity of
x\mapsto\log(x)^{\frac{1}{2}}
, it is clear that the inequality holds on
\Gamma(t)
with constants independent of
t
.
We note that this also introduces a constant into the logarithmic term.
∎

4
Weak formulation and well posedness theorems

We are now in a position to discuss the weak formulation.
For simplicity we assume constant mobility,
M(\cdot)\equiv 1
and a scaling such that
\rho=1
.

4.1
Regular potential

We firstly consider a smooth potential,
F
, under the same assumptions as in
[
18
]
.
That is, we assume
F(r)=F_{1}(r)+F_{2}(r)
for
F_{1},F_{2}\in C^{2}(\mathbb{R})
such that

1.

F(r)\geq\beta
,

2.

F_{1}\geq 0
is convex,

3.

\exists q\in[1,\infty)
such that
|F_{1}^{\prime}(r)|\leq\alpha|r|^{q}+\alpha
,

4.

|F_{1}^{\prime}(r)|+|rF_{1}^{\prime}(r)|\leq\alpha F_{1}(r)+\beta
,

5.

|F_{2}^{\prime}(r)|\leq\alpha|r|+\alpha
,

where
\alpha
denote some non-negative constant, and
\beta
some real constant.
We also assume the viscosity function,
\eta(\cdot)
is Lipschitz continuous.
An typical example of a viscosity function is

\eta(r)=\eta_{1}\frac{(1+r)}{2}+\eta_{2}\frac{(1-r)}{2},\quad r\in[-1,1],

for two positive constants
\eta_{1},\eta_{2}
, which can then be suitably extended to a
C^{2}
, Lipschitz continuous function on
\mathbb{R}
.
The previous section then allows the following weak formulation, using the notation introduced in section
3.4
.

Given initial data
\varphi_{0}\in H^{1}(\Gamma_{0}),\mathbf{u}_{T,0}\in\mathbf{H}_{\sigma}(0)
, find
\varphi\in H^{1}_{H^{-1}}\cap L^{2}_{H^{1}},\mu\in L^{2}_{H^{1}},\mathbf{u}_{T}\in H^{1}_{\mathbf{V}_{\sigma}^{\prime}}\cap L^{2}_{\mathbf{V}_{\sigma}}
such that

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathbf{m}_{*}(\partial^{\circ}\mathbf{u}_{T},\boldsymbol{\phi})+\hat{\mathbf{a}}(\eta(\varphi),\mathbf{u}_{T},\boldsymbol{\phi})+\mathbf{c}_{1}(\mathbf{u}_{T},\mathbf{u}_{T},\boldsymbol{\phi})+\mathbf{l}(\mathbf{u}_{T},\boldsymbol{\phi})+\mathbf{d}_{1}(\mathbf{u}_{T},\boldsymbol{\phi})+\mathbf{d}_{2}(\eta(\varphi),\boldsymbol{\phi})\\
=\mathbf{m}(\mathbf{B},\boldsymbol{\phi})+\mathbf{c}_{2}(\mu,\varphi,\boldsymbol{\phi})\end{split},

(4.1)

\displaystyle m_{*}(\partial^{\circ}\varphi,\phi)+a(\mu,\phi)+\mathbf{c}_{2}(\phi,\varphi,\mathbf{u}_{T})+\mathbf{c}_{2}(\phi,\varphi,\widetilde{\mathbf{u}_{T}})=0,

(4.2)

\displaystyle m(\mu,\phi)=\varepsilon a(\varphi,\phi)+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}m(F^{\prime}(\varphi),\phi),

(4.3)

for all
\phi\in H^{1}(\Gamma(t)),\boldsymbol{\phi}\in\mathbf{V}_{\sigma}(t)
for almost all
t\in[0,T]
, and such that
\varphi(0)=\varphi_{0}
,
\mathbf{u}_{T}(0)=\mathbf{u}_{T,0}
almost everywhere on
\Gamma_{0}
.

Theorem 4.1
.

Let
\Gamma(t)
be a
C^{3}
evolving surface,
F
a potential function satisfying the assumptions at the beginning of the section, and
\varphi_{0}\in H^{1}(\Gamma(0)),\mathbf{u}_{T,0}\in\mathbf{H}_{\sigma}(0)
be initial data.
Then there exists a solution triple
(\varphi,\mu,\mathbf{u}_{T})
such that

\displaystyle\varphi\in L^{\infty}_{H^{1}}\cap L^{2}_{H^{2}}\cap H^{1}_{H^{-1}},

\displaystyle\mu\in L^{2}_{H^{1}},

\displaystyle\mathbf{u}_{T}\in L^{\infty}_{L^{2}}\cap H^{1}_{\mathbf{V}_{\sigma}^{\prime}},

and solving (
4.1
)-(
4.3
) for all
\phi\in H^{1}(\Gamma(t)),\boldsymbol{\phi}\in\mathbf{V}_{\sigma}(t)
for almost all
t\in[0,T]
, and such that
\varphi(0)=\varphi_{0}
,
\mathbf{u}_{T}(0)=\mathbf{u}_{T,0}
almost everywhere on
\Gamma_{0}
.

4.2
Logarithmic potential

In this section we consider the well-posedness theory for the singular logarithmic potential,

F(r)=\frac{\theta}{2}\left((1+r)\log(1+r)-(1-r)\log(1-r)\right)+\frac{1-r^{2}}{2}=:\frac{\theta}{2}F_{\log}(r)+\frac{1-r^{2}}{2},

for
\theta\in(0,1)
.
Here
\theta
can be understood as a temperature in the system, which we have scaled for notational simplicity.
As the equations (
1.5
)-(
1.8
) consider the derivative of
F
we introduce some shorthand notation,

f(r):=(F_{\log}(r))^{\prime}=\log\left(\frac{1+r}{1-r}\right).

The corresponding version of (
4.1
)-(
4.3
) for the logarithmic potential is as follows.
Given initial data
\varphi_{0}\in\mathcal{I}_{0},\mathbf{u}_{T,0}\in\mathbf{H}_{\sigma}(0)
, find
\varphi\in H^{1}_{H^{-1}}\cap L^{2}_{H^{1}},\mu\in L^{2}_{H^{1}},\mathbf{u}_{T}\in H^{1}_{\mathbf{V}_{\sigma}^{\prime}}\cap L^{2}_{\mathbf{V}_{\sigma}}
such that

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathbf{m}_{*}(\partial^{\circ}\mathbf{u}_{T},\boldsymbol{\phi})+\hat{\mathbf{a}}(\eta(\varphi),\mathbf{u}_{T},\boldsymbol{\phi})+\mathbf{c}_{1}(\mathbf{u}_{T},\mathbf{u}_{T},\boldsymbol{\phi})+\mathbf{l}(\mathbf{u}_{T},\boldsymbol{\phi})+\mathbf{d}_{1}(\mathbf{u}_{T},\boldsymbol{\phi})+\mathbf{d}_{2}(\eta(\varphi),\boldsymbol{\phi})\\
=\mathbf{m}(\mathbf{B},\boldsymbol{\phi})+\mathbf{c}_{2}(\mu,\varphi,\boldsymbol{\phi})\end{split},

(4.4)

\displaystyle m_{*}(\partial^{\circ}\varphi,\phi)+a(\mu,\phi)+\mathbf{c}_{2}(\phi,\varphi,\mathbf{u}_{T})+\mathbf{c}_{2}(\phi,\varphi,\widetilde{\mathbf{u}_{T}})=0,

(4.5)

\displaystyle m(\mu,\phi)=\varepsilon a(\varphi,\phi)+\frac{\theta}{2\varepsilon}m(f(\varphi),\phi)-\frac{1}{\varepsilon}m(\varphi,\phi),

(4.6)

for all
\phi\in H^{1}(\Gamma(t)),\boldsymbol{\phi}\in\mathbf{V}_{\sigma}(t)
for almost all
t\in[0,T]
, and such that
\varphi(0)=\varphi_{0}
,
\mathbf{u}_{T}(0)=\mathbf{u}_{T,0}
almost everywhere on
\Gamma_{0}
.

An advantage of this singular potential is that it necessarily has “physical solutions”, that is
|\varphi(t)|<1
almost everywhere on
\Gamma(t)
for almost all
t\in[0,T]
, due to the logarithmic nonlinearity.
As in
[
18
,
19
]
there is a constraint on the initial conditions which allow us to find such a solution.
For
t\in[0,T]
and
\eta\in H^{1}(\Gamma_{0})
, we define

m_{\eta}(t):=\frac{1}{|\Gamma(t)|}\left|\int_{\Gamma_{0}}\eta\right|.

The following result is essentially unchanged from
[
18
]
Proposition 5.1 and Corollary 5.2.

Proposition 4.2
.

Let
\varphi_{0}\in H^{1}(\Gamma_{0})
, and
\mathbf{u}_{T,0}\in\mathbf{H}_{\sigma}(0)
.
If there exists
t^{*}\in[0,T]
such that
m_{\varphi_{0}}(t)>1
then there doesn’t exist a solution triple
(\varphi,\mu,\mathbf{u}_{T})
solving (
4.4
)-(
4.6
) such that
|\varphi|<1
almost everywhere on
\Gamma(t)
for almost all
t\in[0,T]
.

Proof.

Firstly, we notice that
t\mapsto m_{\varphi_{0}}(t)
is continuous.
Hence, there exists some
\gamma>0
such that

t\in(t^{*}-\gamma,t^{*}+\gamma)\Rightarrow m_{\varphi_{0}}(t)>1.

Now from testing (
4.5
) against
\phi\equiv 1
we find the mass conservation property

\int_{\Gamma(t)}\varphi(t)=\int_{\Gamma_{0}}\varphi_{0},

holds for almost all
t\in[0,T]
.
In particular, as
(t^{*}-\gamma,t^{*}+\gamma)
has positive measure, the mass conservation holds for almost all
t\in(t^{*}-\gamma,t^{*}+\gamma)
, and hence

\frac{1}{|\Gamma(t)|}\left|\int_{\Gamma(t)}\varphi(t)\right|=\frac{1}{|\Gamma(t)|}\left|\int_{\Gamma_{0}}\varphi_{0}\right|=m_{\varphi_{0}}(t)>1.

Then, for contradiction, suppose
\varphi(t)<1
almost everywhere on
\Gamma(t)
for almost all
t\in[0,T]
.
Then for almost all
t\in(t^{*}-\gamma,t^{*}+\gamma)
the above mass conservation holds, and
|\varphi(t)|<1
.
Hence

\frac{1}{|\Gamma(t)|}\left|\int_{\Gamma(t)}\varphi(t)\right|\leq\frac{1}{|\Gamma(t)|}\int_{\Gamma(t)}\left|\varphi(t)\right|<1,

and we have a contradiction.
∎

This motivates the choice of admissible initial conditions for the logarithmic potential to be

\mathcal{I}_{0}:=\left\{\eta\in H^{1}(\Gamma_{0})\mid E^{\text{CH}}[\eta;0]<\infty,\ m_{\eta}(t)<1\text{ for all }t\in[0,T]\right\}.

We note there is no condition on the choice of initial velocity, so we still choose
\mathbf{u}_{T,0}\in\mathbf{H}_{\sigma}(0)
.

Theorem 4.3
.

Let
\Gamma(t)
be a
C^{3}
