evolving surface,
F
be the logarithmic potential, and
\varphi_{0}\in\mathcal{I}_{0},\mathbf{u}_{T,0}\in\mathbf{H}_{\sigma}(0)
be initial data.
Then there exists a solution triple
(\varphi,\mu,\mathbf{u}_{T})
such that

\displaystyle\varphi\in L^{\infty}_{H^{1}}\cap L^{2}_{H^{2}}\cap H^{1}_{H^{-1}},

\displaystyle\mu\in L^{2}_{H^{1}},

\displaystyle\mathbf{u}_{T}\in L^{\infty}_{L^{2}}\cap H^{1}_{\mathbf{V}_{\sigma}^{\prime}},

solving (
4.4
)-(
4.6
)
for all
\phi\in H^{1}(\Gamma(t)),\boldsymbol{\phi}\in\mathbf{V}_{\sigma}(t)
for almost all
t\in[0,T]
, and such that
\varphi(0)=\varphi_{0}
,
\mathbf{u}_{T}(0)=\mathbf{u}_{T,0}
almost everywhere on
\Gamma_{0}
.

5
Proof of existence

5.1
The regular potential

5.1.1
Galerkin approximation

We now show existence of a solution triple to (
4.1
)-(
4.3
) via the Faedo-Galerkin method.
For
M\in\mathbb{N}
we define the Galerkin approximations

\displaystyle\varphi^{M}(t)=\sum_{i=1}^{M}\alpha_{i}^{M}(t)\Phi_{t}^{n}\psi_{i},\qquad\mu^{M}(t)=\sum_{i=1}^{M}\beta_{i}^{M}(t)\Phi_{t}^{n}\psi_{i},

\displaystyle\mathbf{u}_{T}^{M}(t)=\sum_{i=1}^{M}\gamma_{i}^{M}(t)\mathcal{P}_{t}\boldsymbol{\chi}_{i},

where
(\psi_{i})_{i=1,...\infty}
form a countable basis of
H^{1}(\Gamma_{0})
, and
(\boldsymbol{\chi}_{i})_{i=1,...\infty}
form a countable basis of
\mathbf{V}_{\sigma}(0)
, and hence pushforward onto countable bases of
H^{1}(\Gamma(t))
and
\mathbf{V}_{\sigma}(t)
respectively.
For example, by Hilbert-Schmidt theory, one can choose
(\psi_{i})_{i=1,...\infty}
to be the eigenfunctions of the Laplace-Beltrami operator on
\Gamma_{0}
, and
(\boldsymbol{\chi}_{i})_{i=1,...\infty}
to be the eigenfunctions of the surface Stokes operator on
\Gamma_{0}
.
We define the following spaces:

\displaystyle V^{M}(t):=\text{span}\{\Phi_{t}^{n}\psi_{i}\mid i=1,...,M\}\leq H^{1}(\Gamma(t)),

\displaystyle\mathbf{V}_{\sigma}^{M}(t):=\text{span}\{\mathcal{P}_{t}\boldsymbol{\chi}_{i}\mid i=1,...,M\}\leq\mathbf{V}_{\sigma}(t),

and the corresponding projections as
P^{M}_{V}(t):H^{1}(\Gamma(t))\rightarrow V^{M}(t),P^{M}_{\mathbf{V}}(t):\mathbf{H}_{\sigma}(t)\rightarrow\mathbf{V}_{\sigma}^{M}(t)
, which are defined by

\displaystyle(P^{M}_{V}(t)\phi,\psi)_{H^{1}(\Gamma(t))}=(\phi,\psi)_{H^{1}(\Gamma(t))},\ \forall\psi\in V^{M}(t),

\displaystyle(P^{M}_{\mathbf{V}}(t)\boldsymbol{\phi},\boldsymbol{\psi})_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma(t))}=(\boldsymbol{\phi},\boldsymbol{\psi})_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma(t))},\ \forall\boldsymbol{\psi}\in\mathbf{H}_{\sigma}(t).

We emphasise that these are
H^{1}
and
\mathbf{L}^{2}
projections respectively, as it is sufficient to choose initial data such that
\mathbf{u}_{T,0}\in\mathbf{H}_{\sigma}(0)
, rather than
\mathbf{V}_{\sigma}(0)
, but require
\varphi_{0}\in H^{1}(\Gamma_{0})
.

Lemma 5.1
.

There exists a solution triple
(\varphi^{M},\mu^{M},\mathbf{u}_{T}^{M})
on
[0,t^{*})
solving

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathbf{m}(\partial^{\circ}\mathbf{u}_{T}^{M},\boldsymbol{\phi})+\hat{\mathbf{a}}(\eta(\varphi^{M}),\mathbf{u}_{T}^{M},\boldsymbol{\phi})+\mathbf{c}_{1}(\mathbf{u}_{T}^{M},\mathbf{u}_{T}^{M},\boldsymbol{\phi})+\mathbf{l}(\mathbf{u}_{T}^{M},\boldsymbol{\phi})+\mathbf{d}_{1}(\mathbf{u}_{T}^{M},\boldsymbol{\phi})+\mathbf{d}_{2}(\eta(\varphi^{M}),\boldsymbol{\phi})\\
=\mathbf{m}(\mathbf{B},\boldsymbol{\phi})+\mathbf{c}_{2}(\mu^{M},\varphi^{M},\boldsymbol{\phi}),\end{split}

(5.1)

\displaystyle m(\partial^{\circ}\varphi^{M},\phi)+a(\mu^{M},\phi)+\mathbf{c}_{2}(\phi,\varphi^{M},\mathbf{u}_{T}^{M})+\mathbf{c}_{2}(\phi,\varphi^{M},\widetilde{\mathbf{u}_{T}})=0,

(5.2)

\displaystyle m(\mu^{M},\phi)=\varepsilon a(\varphi^{M},\phi)+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}m(F^{\prime}(\varphi^{M}),\phi),

(5.3)

for all
\phi\in V^{M}(t),\boldsymbol{\phi}\in V^{M}_{\sigma}(t)
for almost all
t\in[0,t^{*})
, and such that
\varphi^{M}(0)=P_{V}^{M}\varphi_{0}
,
\mathbf{u}_{T}(0)=P_{\mathbf{V}}^{M}\mathbf{u}_{T,0}
almost everywhere on
\Gamma_{0}
.

Proof.

To begin we show the system (
5.1
)-(
5.3
) is equivalent to an ODE for the coefficients
\alpha,\beta,\gamma
.
Firstly, by (
3.2
) we note that we may write

\mathbf{m}(\partial^{\circ}\mathbf{u}_{T}^{M},\boldsymbol{\phi})=\mathbf{m}(\partial^{*}\mathbf{u}_{T}^{M},\boldsymbol{\phi})+\mathbf{m}(\bar{\mathbb{A}}\mathbf{u}_{T}^{M},\boldsymbol{\phi}).

Now we note that by definition of the strong derivative one finds

\partial^{\circ}\Phi_{t}^{n}\psi_{i}=0,\qquad\partial^{*}\mathcal{P}_{t}\boldsymbol{\chi}_{i}=0,

and hence

\displaystyle\partial^{\circ}\varphi^{M}(t)=\sum_{i=1}^{M}(\alpha_{i}^{M})^{\prime}(t)\Phi_{t}^{n}\psi_{i},

\displaystyle\partial^{*}\mathbf{u}_{T}^{M}(t)=\sum_{i=1}^{M}(\gamma_{i}^{M})^{\prime}(t)\mathcal{P}_{t}\boldsymbol{\chi}_{i}.

With this in hand, it is clear that testing (
5.1
) with
\mathcal{P}_{t}\boldsymbol{\chi}_{k}
yields

\sum_{i=1}^{M}(\gamma_{i}^{M})^{\prime}\mathbf{m}(\mathcal{P}_{t}\boldsymbol{\chi}_{i},\mathcal{P}_{t}\boldsymbol{\chi}_{k})+\sum_{i=1}^{M}\gamma_{i}^{M}\mathbf{m}(\bar{\mathbb{A}}\mathcal{P}_{t}\boldsymbol{\chi}_{i},\mathcal{P}_{t}\boldsymbol{\chi}_{k})+\sum_{i=1}^{M}\gamma_{i}^{M}\hat{\mathbf{a}}\left(\eta\left(\sum_{j=1}^{M}\alpha_{j}^{M}\Phi_{t}^{n}\psi_{j}\right)\mathcal{P}_{t}\boldsymbol{\chi}_{i},\mathcal{P}_{t}\boldsymbol{\chi}_{k}\right)\\
+\sum_{i=1}^{M}(\gamma_{i}^{M})^{2}\mathbf{c}_{1}(\mathcal{P}_{t}\boldsymbol{\chi}_{i},\mathcal{P}_{t}\boldsymbol{\chi}_{i},\mathcal{P}_{t}\boldsymbol{\chi}_{k})+\sum_{i=1}^{M}\gamma_{i}^{M}\mathbf{l}(\mathcal{P}_{t}\boldsymbol{\chi}_{i},\mathcal{P}_{t}\boldsymbol{\chi}_{k})+\sum_{i=1}^{M}\gamma_{i}^{M}\mathbf{d}_{1}(\mathcal{P}_{t}\boldsymbol{\chi}_{i},\mathcal{P}_{t}\boldsymbol{\chi}_{k})+\mathbf{d}_{2}\left(\eta\left(\sum_{j=1}^{M}\alpha_{j}^{M}\Phi_{t}^{n}\psi_{j}\right),\mathcal{P}_{t}\boldsymbol{\chi}_{k}\right)\\
=\mathbf{m}(\mathbf{B},\mathcal{P}_{t}\boldsymbol{\chi}_{k})+\sum_{i=1}^{M}\sum_{j=1}^{M}\alpha_{j}^{M}\beta_{i}^{M}\mathbf{c}_{2}(\Phi_{t}^{n}\psi_{i},\Phi_{t}^{n}\psi_{j},\mathcal{P}_{t}\boldsymbol{\chi}_{k}).

Next testing (
5.2
) and (
5.3
) with
\Phi_{t}^{n}\psi_{k}
we obtain

\sum_{i=1}^{M}(\alpha_{i}^{M})^{\prime}m(\Phi_{t}^{n}\psi_{i},\Phi_{t}^{n}\psi_{k})+\sum_{i=1}^{M}\beta_{i}^{M}a(\Phi_{t}^{n}\psi_{i},\Phi_{t}^{n}\psi_{i})+\sum_{i=1}^{M}\sum_{j=1}^{M}\alpha_{i}^{M}\gamma_{j}^{M}\mathbf{c}_{2}(\Phi_{t}^{n}\psi_{k},\Phi_{t}^{n}\psi_{i},\mathcal{P}_{t}\boldsymbol{\chi}_{j})\\
+\sum_{i=1}^{M}\alpha_{i}^{M}\mathbf{c}_{2}(\Phi_{t}^{n}\psi_{k},\Phi_{t}^{n}\psi_{i},\widetilde{\mathbf{u}_{T}})=0,

and

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{M}\beta_{i}^{M}m(\Phi_{t}^{n}\psi_{i},\Phi_{t}^{n}\psi_{k})=\varepsilon\sum_{i=1}^{M}\alpha_{i}^{M}a(\Phi_{t}^{n}\psi_{i},\Phi_{t}^{n}\psi_{k})+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}m\left(F^{\prime}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{M}\alpha_{i}^{M}\Phi_{t}^{n}\psi_{i}\right),\Phi_{t}^{n}\psi_{k}\right).

By considering the system this generates for
k=1,...,M
one obtain an ODE system for the vectors
\alpha^{M}(t),\beta^{M}(t),\gamma^{M}(t)\in\mathbb{R}^{M}
,
It is straightforward to see that the nonlinearities are locally Lipschitz, but we omit these details. Applying standard ODE theory one obtains the short time existence of a solution triple
(\alpha^{M},\beta^{M},\gamma^{M})
.
∎

Next we establish existence on
[0,T]
by use of energy estimates.
To do this we recall the Ginzburg-Landau functional,

\displaystyle E^{\text{CH}}[\varphi;t]:=\int_{\Gamma(t)}\frac{\varepsilon}{2}|\nabla_{\Gamma}\varphi|^{2}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}F(\varphi)=\frac{\varepsilon}{2}a(\varphi,\varphi)+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}m(F(\varphi),1),

(5.4)

which we use in the following result.

Lemma 5.2
.

The solution triple
(\varphi^{M},\mu^{M},\mathbf{u}_{T}^{M})
is such that

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left(\frac{1}{2}\|\mathbf{u}_{T}^{M}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma(t))}^{2}+E^{\text{CH}}[\varphi^{M};t]\right)+\frac{\eta_{*}}{2}\int_{0}^{T}\|\mathbb{E}(\mathbf{u}_{T}^{M})\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma(t))}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}\|\nabla_{\Gamma}\mu^{M}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma(t))}^{2}\leq C,

(5.5)

for a constant
C
independent of
M
.

Proof.

To begin, we test (
5.1
) with
\mathbf{u}_{T}^{M}
for

\mathbf{m}(\partial^{\circ}\mathbf{u}_{T}^{M},\mathbf{u}_{T}^{M})+\hat{\mathbf{a}}(\eta(\varphi^{M}),\mathbf{u}_{T}^{M},\mathbf{u}_{T}^{M})=\mathbf{m}(\mathbf{B},\mathbf{u}_{T}^{M})+\mathbf{c}_{2}(\mu^{M},\varphi^{M},\mathbf{u}_{T}^{M})-\mathbf{l}(\mathbf{u}_{T}^{M},\mathbf{u}_{T}^{M})-\mathbf{d}_{1}(\mathbf{u}_{T}^{M},\mathbf{u}_{T}^{M})\\
-\mathbf{d}_{2}(\eta(\varphi^{M}),\mathbf{u}_{T}^{M}),

where we have used
\mathbf{c}_{1}(\mathbf{u}_{T}^{M},\mathbf{u}_{T}^{M},\mathbf{u}_{T}^{M})=0
.
Next we notice that from (
5.2
) that

\displaystyle\mathbf{c}_{2}(\mu^{M},\varphi^{M},\mathbf{u}_{T}^{M})=-m(\partial^{\circ}\varphi^{M},\mu^{M})-a(\mu^{M},\mu^{M})-\mathbf{c}_{2}(\mu^{M},\varphi^{M},\widetilde{\mathbf{u}_{T}}),

and from (
5.3
) we find

m(\partial^{\circ}\varphi^{M},\mu^{M})=\varepsilon a(\varphi^{M},\partial^{\circ}\varphi^{M})+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}m(F^{\prime}(\varphi^{M}),\partial^{\circ}\varphi^{M}).

Hence combining these three equalities it is clear that

\mathbf{m}(\partial^{\circ}\mathbf{u}_{T}^{M},\mathbf{u}_{T}^{M})+\hat{\mathbf{a}}(\eta(\varphi^{M}),\mathbf{u}_{T}^{M},\mathbf{u}_{T}^{M})+\varepsilon a(\varphi^{M},\partial^{\circ}\varphi^{M})+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}m(F^{\prime}(\varphi^{M}),\partial^{\circ}\varphi^{M})+a(\mu^{M},\mu^{M})=\mathbf{m}(\mathbf{B},\mathbf{u}_{T}^{M})\\
-\mathbf{c}_{2}(\mu^{M},\varphi^{M},\widetilde{\mathbf{u}_{T}})-\mathbf{l}(\mathbf{u}_{T}^{M},\mathbf{u}_{T}^{M})-\mathbf{d}_{1}(\mathbf{u}_{T}^{M},\mathbf{u}_{T}^{M})-\mathbf{d}_{2}(\eta(\varphi^{M}),\mathbf{u}_{T}^{M}).

(5.6)

Recalling Lemma
3.3
we find that

\displaystyle\mathbf{m}(\partial^{\circ}\mathbf{u}_{T}^{M},\mathbf{u}_{T}^{M})=\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dt}\mathbf{m}(\mathbf{u}_{T}^{M},\mathbf{u}_{T}^{M})-\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{m}(\mathbf{u}_{T}^{M},HV_{N}\mathbf{u}_{T}^{M}),

\displaystyle a(\varphi^{M},\partial^{\circ}\varphi^{M})=\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dt}a(\varphi^{M},\varphi^{M})-\frac{1}{2}b(\varphi^{M},\varphi^{M}),

\displaystyle m(F^{\prime}(\varphi^{M}),\partial^{\circ}\varphi^{M})=\frac{d}{dt}m(F(\varphi^{M}),1)-m(F(\varphi^{M}),HV_{N}).

Next, by using the bound
\eta_{*}\leq\eta(\cdot)
and the above we see

\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dt}\mathbf{m}(\mathbf{u}_{T}^{M},\mathbf{u}_{T}^{M})+\frac{d}{dt}E^{\text{CH}}[\varphi^{M};t]+\eta_{*}\mathbf{a}(\mathbf{u}_{T}^{M},\mathbf{u}_{T}^{M})+a(\mu^{M},\mu^{M})=\mathbf{m}(\mathbf{B},\mathbf{u}_{T}^{M})+\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{m}(\mathbf{u}_{T}^{M},HV_{N}\mathbf{u}_{T}^{M})+\frac{\varepsilon}{2}b(\varphi^{M},\varphi^{M})\\
+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}m(F(\varphi^{M}),HV_{N})-\mathbf{c}_{2}(\mu^{M},\varphi^{M},\widetilde{\mathbf{u}_{T}})-\mathbf{l}(\mathbf{u}_{T}^{M},\mathbf{u}_{T}^{M})-\mathbf{d}_{1}(\mathbf{u}_{T}^{M},\mathbf{u}_{T}^{M})-\mathbf{d}_{2}(\eta(\varphi^{M}),\mathbf{u}_{T}^{M}).

(5.7)

The focus now is bounding these terms on the right hand side.
Firstly we find

\mathbf{m}(\mathbf{B},\mathbf{u}_{T}^{M})+\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{m}(\mathbf{u}_{T}^{M},HV_{N}\mathbf{u}_{T}^{M})+\mathbf{l}(\mathbf{u}_{T}^{M},\mathbf{u}_{T}^{M})\leq\left(\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{2}\|HV_{N}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Gamma(t))}+\|V_{N}\mathbb{H}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{\infty}(\Gamma(t))}\right)\|\mathbf{u}_{T}^{M}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma(t))}^{2}\\
+\frac{1}{2}\|\mathbf{B}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma(t))}^{2},

(5.8)

where we note that
H,V_{N}\in C^{1}(\mathcal{G}_{T}),\mathbb{H}\in(C^{1}(\mathcal{G}_{T}))^{3\times 3}
by our assumptions.
Similarly, this smoothness assumption on
HV_{N}
allows us to bound

\displaystyle\frac{\varepsilon}{2}b(\varphi^{M},\varphi^{M})+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}m(F(\varphi^{M}),HV_{N})\leq CE^{\text{CH}}[\varphi^{M};t],

(5.9)

for a constant independent of
t
and
M
.
We now look at the terms introduced by the influence of
\widetilde{\mathbf{u}_{T}}
.
It is straightforward to see that

\displaystyle|\mathbf{d}_{1}(\mathbf{u}_{T}^{M},\mathbf{u}_{T}^{M})|\leq\|\widetilde{\mathbf{u}_{T}}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{\infty}(\Gamma(t))}\|\mathbf{u}_{T}^{M}\|_{\mathbf{H}^{1}(\Gamma(t))}\|\mathbf{u}_{T}^{M}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma(t))}+\|\widetilde{\mathbf{u}_{T}}\|_{\mathbf{H}^{1,\infty}(\Gamma(t))}\|\mathbf{u}_{T}^{M}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma(t))}^{2}

where we have used the regularity result from Appendix
A
to bound
\widetilde{\mathbf{u}_{T}}
.
We then use (
3.8
) to see that

\|\mathbf{u}_{T}^{M}\|_{\mathbf{H}^{1}(\Gamma(t))}\|\mathbf{u}_{T}^{M}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma(t))}\leq C\|\mathbf{u}_{T}^{M}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma(t))}^{\frac{3}{2}}\|\mathbb{E}(\mathbf{u}_{T}^{M})\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma(t))}^{\frac{1}{2}},

and hence from Young’s inequality we obtain

\|\widetilde{\mathbf{u}_{T}}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{\infty}(\Gamma(t))}\|\mathbf{u}_{T}^{M}\|_{\mathbf{H}^{1}(\Gamma(t))}\|\mathbf{u}_{T}^{M}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma(t))}\leq\frac{\eta_{*}}{4}\|\mathbb{E}(\mathbf{u}_{T}^{M})\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma(t))}^{2}+C\|\widetilde{\mathbf{u}_{T}}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{\infty}(\Gamma(t))}^{\frac{4}{3}}\|\mathbf{u}_{T}^{M}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma(t))}^{2}.

All in all, this yields a bound on
\mathbf{d}_{1}(\mathbf{u}_{T}^{M},\mathbf{u}_{T}^{M})
given by

\displaystyle|\mathbf{d}_{1}(\mathbf{u}_{T}^{M},\mathbf{u}_{T}^{M})|\leq\frac{\eta_{*}}{4}\|\mathbb{E}(\mathbf{u}_{T}^{M})\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma(t))}^{2}+\left(\|\widetilde{\mathbf{u}_{T}}\|_{\mathbf{H}^{1,\infty}(\Gamma(t))}+C\|\widetilde{\mathbf{u}_{T}}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{\infty}(\Gamma(t))}^{\frac{4}{3}}\right)\|\mathbf{u}_{T}^{M}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma(t))}^{2}.

(5.10)

Likewise it is straightforward to see that

\displaystyle|\mathbf{d}_{2}(\eta(\varphi^{M}),\mathbf{u}_{T}^{M})|\leq\frac{\eta_{*}}{4}\|\mathbb{E}(\mathbf{u}_{T}^{M})\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma(t))}^{2}+\frac{2(\eta^{*})^{2}}{\eta_{*}}\|\mathbb{E}(\widetilde{\mathbf{u}_{T}})\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma(t))}^{2}.

(5.11)

We lastly consider the
\mathbf{c}_{2}
term and see that

\mathbf{c}_{2}(\mu^{M},\varphi^{M},\widetilde{\mathbf{u}_{T}})=\int_{\Gamma(t)}\mu^{M}\nabla_{\Gamma}\varphi^{M}\cdot\widetilde{\mathbf{u}_{T}}=-\int_{\Gamma(t)}\varphi^{M}\nabla_{\Gamma}\mu^{M}\cdot\widetilde{\mathbf{u}_{T}}-\int_{\Gamma(t)}\varphi^{M}\mu^{M}\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot\widetilde{\mathbf{u}_{T}},

which follows from integration by parts.
By construction of
\widetilde{\mathbf{u}_{T}}
we now find that

-\mathbf{c}_{2}(\mu^{M},\varphi^{M},\widetilde{\mathbf{u}_{T}})=\mathbf{c}_{2}(\varphi^{M},\mu^{M},\widetilde{\mathbf{u}_{T}})-m(\mu^{M},HV_{N}\varphi^{M}).

The difficulty now is in establishing an
L^{2}
bound on
\mu^{M}
.
To do this we test (
5.3
) with
\mu^{M}
and use Young’s inequality to see that

\|\mu^{M}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma(t))}^{2}\leq\frac{1}{4}\|\nabla_{\Gamma}\mu^{M}\|^{2}+\varepsilon^{2}\|\nabla_{\Gamma}\varphi^{M}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma(t))}^{2}+\frac{1}{2\varepsilon^{2}}\|F^{\prime}(\varphi^{M})\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma(t))}^{2}.

One then deals with the nonlinearity here by using the growth conditions on
F^{\prime}
, from which we readily see that

\frac{1}{2\varepsilon^{2}}\|F^{\prime}(\varphi^{M})\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma(t))}^{2}\leq C+C\|\varphi^{M}\|_{L^{2q}(\Gamma(t))}^{2q}+C\|\varphi^{M}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma(t))}^{2},

for some constants
C,q
dependent on the choice of
F
.
By using the Sobolev embedding
H^{1}(\Gamma(t))\hookrightarrow L^{2q}(\Gamma(t))
and the Poincaré inequality (noting that
\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.83337pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.11674pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.48965pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.31259pt}}\!\int_{\Gamma(t)}\varphi^{M}
is constant and can be bounded independent of
M
) it is straightforward to show that

\|\varphi^{M}\|_{L^{2q}(\Gamma(t))}^{2q}+\|\varphi^{M}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma(t))}^{2}\leq C+C\|\nabla_{\Gamma}\varphi^{M}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma(t))}^{2q}+C\|\nabla_{\Gamma}\varphi^{M}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma(t))}^{2}.

Combining these bounds one finds that

\displaystyle|\mathbf{c}_{2}(\mu^{M},\varphi^{M},\widetilde{\mathbf{u}_{T}})|\leq C+\frac{1}{2}\|\nabla_{\Gamma}\mu^{M}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma(t))}^{2}+CE^{\text{CH}}[\varphi^{M};t]+CE^{\text{CH}}[\varphi^{M};t]^{q},

(5.12)

for constants
C
independent of
M
.

Finally, by using the estimates (
5.8
)-(
5.12
) in (
5.7
) and integrating over
[0,t]
we obtain an inequality of the form

\frac{1}{2}\|\mathbf{u}_{T}^{M}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma(t))}^{2}+E^{\text{CH}}[\varphi^{M};t]+\frac{\eta_{*}}{2}\int_{0}^{t}\|\mathbb{E}(\mathbf{u}_{T}^{M})\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma(t))}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{t}\|\nabla_{\Gamma}\mu^{M}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma(t))}^{2}\leq k\\
+\int_{0}^{t}K(s)\left(\frac{1}{2}\|\mathbf{u}_{T}^{M}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma(s))}^{2}+E^{\text{CH}}[\varphi^{M};s]+\left(\frac{1}{2}\|\mathbf{u}_{T}^{M}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma(s))}^{2}+E^{\text{CH}}[\varphi^{M};s]\right)^{q}\right)\,ds,

(5.13)

where

k=C+\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}\|\mathbf{B}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma(s))}^{2}\,ds+\frac{2(\eta^{*})^{2}}{\eta_{*}}\int_{0}^{T}\|\mathbb{E}(\widetilde{\mathbf{u}_{T}})\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma(s))}^{2}\,ds+\frac{1}{2}\|P_{\mathbf{V}}^{M}\mathbf{u}_{T,0}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma(0))}^{2}+E^{\text{CH}}[P_{V}^{M}\varphi_{0};0],

and

\displaystyle K(s)=C+\frac{1}{2}\|HV_{N}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Gamma(s))}+\|V_{N}\mathbb{H}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{\infty}(\Gamma(s))}+\|\widetilde{\mathbf{u}_{T}}\|_{\mathbf{H}^{1,\infty}(\Gamma(s))}+C\|\widetilde{\mathbf{u}_{T}}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{\infty}(\Gamma(s))}^{\frac{4}{3}}.

We can bound
k
independently of
M
by noting that

\|P_{V}^{M}\varphi_{0}\|_{H^{1}(\Gamma(0))}\leq\|P_{V}^{M}\varphi_{0}\|_{H^{1}(\Gamma(0))},\qquad\|P^{M}_{\mathbf{V}}\mathbf{u}_{T,0}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma(0))}\leq\|\mathbf{u}_{T,0}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma(0))},

and as above we can bound the potential term in
E^{\text{CH}}
by using Sobolev embeddings.

Lastly, we note that while
E^{\text{CH}}[\varphi^{M};t]
is not necessarily non-negative, it is bounded below.
Hence we add some sufficiently large constant to (
5.13
) so that the analogous inequality holds for the modified energy,

\widetilde{E^{\text{CH}}}[\varphi;t]:=E^{\text{CH}}[\varphi;t]+\tilde{\beta}\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\|\nabla_{\Gamma}\varphi\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma(t))}^{2}.

From our assumptions it is clear such a constant exists and depends only on the evolution of
\Gamma(t)
and choice of
F
.
Thus one obtains (
5.5
) by using Lemma
3.8
with
\omega(y)=y+y^{q}
, and choosing some sufficiently small
t_{0}
, which is independent of
M
.
∎

By using the
L^{2}
bound we established in the preceding proof, the Sobolev embedding
H^{1}(\Gamma(t))\hookrightarrow L^{2q}(\Gamma(t))
, and the energy estimate (
5.5
) it is clear that one obtains uniform
L^{2}_{H^{1}}
bounds on
\mu^{M}
.
Likewise, using (
3.9
), one can establish uniform
L^{2}_{\mathbf{H}^{1}}
bounds for
\mathbf{u}_{T}^{M}
.

5.1.2
Passage to the limit

We have established uniform bounds for
\mathbf{u}_{T}^{M}
in
L^{\infty}_{\mathbf{H}_{\sigma}}
and
L^{2}_{\mathbf{H^{1}}}
, for
\varphi^{M}
in
L^{\infty}_{H^{1}}
, and for
\mu^{M}
in
L^{2}_{H^{1}}
.
Thus, there exist limiting functions
\mathbf{u}_{T},\varphi,\mu
such that

\displaystyle\mathbf{u}_{T}^{M}\rightharpoonup\mathbf{u}_{T},\text{ weakly in }L^{2}_{\mathbf{V}_{\sigma}},

\displaystyle\mathbf{u}_{T}^{M}\overset{*}{\rightharpoonup}\mathbf{u}_{T},\text{ weak-}*\text{ in }L^{\infty}_{\mathbf{H}_{\sigma}},

\displaystyle\varphi^{M}\overset{*}{\rightharpoonup}\varphi,\text{ weak-}*\text{ in }L^{\infty}_{H^{1}},

\displaystyle\mu^{M}\rightharpoonup\mu,\text{ weakly in }L^{2}_{H^{1}}.

Moreover, arguing as in
[
18
]
, one can show that
\varphi^{M}\rightarrow\varphi
strongly in
L^{2}_{L^{2}}
.
This is useful as one cannot use (a variant of) the Aubin-Lions theorem to show strong convergence of
\varphi,\mathbf{u}_{T}
because we do not have uniform estimates for
\varphi^{M}
in
H^{1}_{H^{-1}}
and
\mathbf{u}_{T}^{M}
in
H^{1}_{\mathbf{V}_{\sigma}^{\prime}}
.
Now by proceeding as in the proof of
[
18
]
, Proposition 4.10, one shows the existence of
\partial^{\circ}\varphi
, but we omit the details.
We compute a bound for
\partial^{\circ}\varphi
in
L^{2}_{H^{-1}}
in Lemma
5.3
below.

Recalling the compact embeddings

{H^{1}}(\Gamma(t))\hookrightarrow L^{p}(\Gamma(t)),

for all
p\in(1,\infty)
, by using
[
6
]
, Theorem 6.2, one obtains strongly convergent subsequences such that

\displaystyle\varphi^{M}\rightarrow\varphi,\text{ strongly in }L^{2}_{L^{p}}.

The case
p=4
will be useful when we discuss the passage to the limit of
\mathbf{u}_{T}^{M}
.
Similarly, as noted in
[
45
]
, one has a compact embedding
L^{2}_{\mathbf{V}_{\sigma}}\hookrightarrow L^{2}_{\mathbf{H}_{\sigma}}
, and hence one can extract a subsequence such that

\displaystyle\mathbf{u}_{T}^{M}\rightarrow\mathbf{u}_{T},\text{ strongly in }L^{2}_{\mathbf{H}_{\sigma}}.

We now discuss passage to the limit in the nonlinear terms.
Firstly, it is shown in
[
18
]
that

F^{\prime}(\varphi^{M})\rightharpoonup F^{\prime}(\varphi),\text{ weakly in }L^{2}_{L^{2}},

where the authors use a generalisation of the dominated convergence theorem.
To see that we can pass to the limit in the
\mathbf{c}_{1}
term one argues as in
[
50
]
Lemma 3.2.
Similarly, to see the convergence in the
\mathbf{c}_{2}(\mu^{M},\varphi^{M},\boldsymbol{\phi})
term in (
5.1
) we write

\mathbf{c}_{2}(\mu^{M},\varphi^{M},\boldsymbol{\phi})=-\mathbf{c}_{2}(\varphi^{M},\mu^{M},\boldsymbol{\phi})=\mathbf{c}_{2}(\varphi-\varphi^{M},\mu^{M},\boldsymbol{\phi})-\mathbf{c}_{2}(\varphi,\mu^{M},\boldsymbol{\phi}).

Firstly, we notice that
\int_{0}^{T}\mathbf{c}_{2}(\varphi,\cdot,\boldsymbol{\phi})
is an element of
L^{2}_{H^{-1}}
from the established bounds, and hence the weak convergence of
\mu^{M}
yields

\int_{0}^{T}\mathbf{c}_{2}(\varphi,\mu^{M},\boldsymbol{\phi})\rightarrow\int_{0}^{T}\mathbf{c}_{2}(\varphi,\mu,\boldsymbol{\phi})

as
M\rightarrow\infty
.
For the other term, we use the strong convergence
\varphi^{M}\rightarrow\varphi
in
L^{2}_{L^{4}}
, and write

\left|\int_{0}^{T}\mathbf{c}_{2}(\varphi-\varphi^{M},\mu^{M},\boldsymbol{\phi})\right|\leq\int_{0}^{T}\|\varphi-\varphi^{M}\|_{L^{4}(\Gamma(t))}\|\nabla_{\Gamma}\mu^{M}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma(t))}\|\boldsymbol{\phi}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{4}(\Gamma(t))}\rightarrow 0.

Thus we find

\int_{0}^{T}\mathbf{c}_{2}(\mu^{M},\varphi^{M},\boldsymbol{\phi})\rightarrow-\int_{0}^{T}\mathbf{c}_{2}(\varphi,\mu,\boldsymbol{\phi})=\int_{0}^{T}\mathbf{c}_{2}(\mu,\varphi,\boldsymbol{\phi}),

and by using standard localisation arguments one concludes that
\mathbf{c}_{2}(\mu^{M},\varphi^{M},\boldsymbol{\phi})\rightarrow\mathbf{c}_{2}(\mu,\varphi,\boldsymbol{\phi})
for almost all
t\in[0,T]
and all
\boldsymbol{\phi}\in\mathbf{V}_{\sigma}(t)
.

\mathbf{c}_{2}(\phi,\varphi^{M},\mathbf{u}_{T}^{M})
converges by similar logic, but with the added advantage that one can use strong convergence of both
\varphi^{M}
and
\mathbf{u}_{T}^{M}
.

The final nonlinear term to consider the convergence of is
\hat{\mathbf{a}}(\eta(\varphi^{M}),\mathbf{u}_{T}^{M},\boldsymbol{\phi})
.
This follows essentially the same calculations as above, owing to the assumption that we have a Lipschitz continuous viscosity
\eta(\cdot)
.
One writes

\hat{\mathbf{a}}(\eta(\varphi^{M}),\mathbf{u}_{T}^{M},\boldsymbol{\phi})=\hat{\mathbf{a}}(\eta(\varphi),\mathbf{u}_{T}^{M},\boldsymbol{\phi})+\hat{\mathbf{a}}(\eta(\varphi^{M})-\eta(\varphi),\mathbf{u}_{T}^{M},\boldsymbol{\phi}),

and as above we find
\int_{0}^{T}\hat{\mathbf{a}}(\eta(\varphi),\cdot,\boldsymbol{\phi})
is an element of
L^{2}_{\mathbf{V}_{\sigma}^{\prime}}
.
Likewise, the integral of the second term over
[0,T]
vanishes
6
6

6

Here one considers
\boldsymbol{\phi}
sufficiently smooth so that
\mathbb{E}(\boldsymbol{\phi})\in L^{\infty}_{\mathbf{L}^{4}}
and extends to
\boldsymbol{\phi}\in L^{2}_{\mathbf{V}_{\sigma}(t)}
by density.
as
M\rightarrow 0
by using the Lipschitz property of
\eta(\cdot)
and the strong convergence of
\varphi^{M}
in
L^{2}_{L^{4}}
.

With these considerations, one argues as in
[
45
]
to see that
\partial^{\circ}\mathbf{u}_{T}
exists.
We omit the details here as the main point of interest is in the nonlinear terms.
Now we know these derivatives exist, we may show the following bounds.

Lemma 5.3
.

We have
\partial^{\circ}\varphi\in L^{2}_{H^{-1}}
and
\partial^{\circ}\mathbf{u}_{T}\in L^{2}_{\mathbf{V}_{\sigma}^{\prime}}
.

Proof.

To see the bound for
\partial^{\circ}\varphi^{M}
observe from (
4.1
) that

|m(\partial^{\circ}\varphi,\phi)|\leq|a(\mu,\phi)|+|\mathbf{c}_{2}(\phi,\varphi,\mathbf{u}_{T})|+|\mathbf{c}_{2}(\phi,\varphi,\widetilde{\mathbf{u}_{T}})|,

and we recall

\displaystyle\mathbf{c}_{2}(\phi,\varphi,\mathbf{u}_{T})=-\mathbf{c}_{2}(\varphi,\phi,\mathbf{u}_{T}),

\displaystyle\mathbf{c}_{2}(\phi,\varphi,\widetilde{\mathbf{u}_{T}})=m(\phi,HV_{N}\varphi)-\mathbf{c}_{2}(\varphi,\phi,\widetilde{\mathbf{u}_{T}}).

From this it is clear to see that

\frac{|m(\partial^{\circ}\varphi,\phi)|}{\|\phi\|_{H^{1}(\Gamma(t))}}\leq\|\nabla_{\Gamma}\mu\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma(t))}+\|\varphi\|_{L^{4}(\Gamma(t))}\|\mathbf{u}_{T}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{4}(\Gamma(t))}+\|HV_{N}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Gamma(t))}\|\varphi\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma(t))}\\
+\|\varphi\|_{L^{4}(\Gamma(t))}\|\widetilde{\mathbf{u}_{T}}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{4}(\Gamma(t))},

and hence using (
3.7
), (
3.8
), and the uniform bounds established by (
5.5
) we obtain the
L^{2}_{H^{-1}}
bound.

Similarly to bound
\partial^{\circ}\mathbf{u}_{T}
, we see from (
5.1
) that

|\mathbf{m}(\partial^{\circ}\mathbf{u}_{T},\boldsymbol{\phi})|\leq|\hat{\mathbf{a}}(\eta(\varphi),\mathbf{u}_{T},\boldsymbol{\phi})|+|\mathbf{c}_{1}(\mathbf{u}_{T},\mathbf{u}_{T},\boldsymbol{\phi})|+|\mathbf{l}(\mathbf{u}_{T},\boldsymbol{\phi})|+|\mathbf{d}_{1}(\mathbf{u}_{T},\boldsymbol{\phi})|+|\mathbf{d}_{2}(\eta(\varphi),\boldsymbol{\phi})|\\
+|\mathbf{m}(\mathbf{B},\boldsymbol{\phi})|+|\mathbf{c}_{2}(\mu,\varphi,\boldsymbol{\phi})|.

Recall from properties of
\mathbf{c}_{1},\mathbf{c}_{2}
that

\displaystyle|\mathbf{c}_{1}(\mathbf{u}_{T},\mathbf{u}_{T},\boldsymbol{\phi})|=|\mathbf{c}_{1}(\boldsymbol{\phi},\mathbf{u}_{T},\mathbf{u}_{T})|\leq\|\boldsymbol{\phi}\|_{\mathbf{H}^{1}(\Gamma(t))}\|\mathbf{u}_{T}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{4}(\Gamma(t))}^{2},

\displaystyle|\mathbf{c}_{2}(\mu,\varphi,\boldsymbol{\phi})|=|\mathbf{c}_{2}(\varphi,\mu,\boldsymbol{\phi})|\leq C\|\varphi\|_{L^{4}(\Gamma(t))}\|\nabla_{\Gamma}\mu\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma(t))}\|\boldsymbol{\phi}\|_{\mathbf{H}^{1}(\Gamma(t))},

where we have used the Sobolev embedding
\mathbf{H}^{1}(\Gamma(t))\hookrightarrow\mathbf{L}^{4}(\Gamma(t))
in the second inequality.
The only other problematic term here is
|\mathbf{d}_{1}(\mathbf{u}_{T},\boldsymbol{\phi})|
, which we bound as

\displaystyle|\mathbf{d}_{1}(\mathbf{u}_{T},\boldsymbol{\phi})|

\displaystyle\leq|\mathbf{c}_{1}(\mathbf{u}_{T},\widetilde{\mathbf{u}_{T}},\boldsymbol{\phi})|+|\mathbf{c}_{1}(\widetilde{\mathbf{u}_{T}},\mathbf{u}_{T},\boldsymbol{\phi})|=|\mathbf{c}_{1}(\boldsymbol{\phi},\widetilde{\mathbf{u}_{T}},\mathbf{u}_{T})|+|\mathbf{c}_{1}(\boldsymbol{\phi},\mathbf{u}_{T},\widetilde{\mathbf{u}_{T}})|+2|\mathbf{m}(\mathbf{u}_{T}HV_{N},\boldsymbol{\phi})|

\displaystyle\leq C\left(\|\widetilde{\mathbf{u}_{T}}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{4}(\Gamma(t))}+1\right)\|\mathbf{u}_{T}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{4}(\Gamma(t))}\|\boldsymbol{\phi}\|_{\mathbf{H}^{1}(\Gamma(t))}.

From these inequalities it is straightforward to see that

\frac{|\mathbf{m}(\partial^{\circ}\mathbf{u}_{T},\boldsymbol{\phi})|}{\|\boldsymbol{\phi}\|_{\mathbf{H}^{1}(\Gamma(t))}}\leq\eta^{*}\|\mathbb{E}(\mathbf{u}_{T})\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma(t))}+\|\mathbf{u}_{T}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{4}(\Gamma(t))}^{2}+C\|\varphi\|_{L^{4}(\Gamma(t))}\|\nabla_{\Gamma}\mu\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma(t))}+\|V_{N}\mathbb{H}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{\infty}(\Gamma(t))}\|\mathbf{u}_{T}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma(t))}\\
+C\|\mathbf{u}_{T}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma(t))}+C\|\mathbf{u}_{T}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{4}(\Gamma(t))}\|\widetilde{\mathbf{u}_{T}}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{4}(\Gamma(t))}+\eta^{*}\|\mathbb{E}(\widetilde{\mathbf{u}_{T}})\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma(t))}+\|\mathbf{B}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma(t))}.

From this inequality it is straightforward to see how one obtains the
L^{2}_{\mathbf{V}_{\sigma}^{\prime}}
bound by using (
3.8
), (
5.5
), and Sobolev embeddings where necessary.
∎

Lastly it remains to discuss the initial conditions.
We note that
\mathbf{u}_{T}^{M}(0)=P^{M}_{\mathbf{V}}\mathbf{u}_{T,0}
, and
\varphi^{M}(0)=P^{M}_{V}\varphi_{0}
.
These projections are such that
P^{M}_{\mathbf{V}}\mathbf{u}_{T,0}\rightarrow\mathbf{u}_{T,0}
strongly in
\mathbf{H}_{\sigma}(0)
, and
P^{M}_{V}\varphi_{0}\rightarrow\varphi_{0}
strongly in
H^{1}(\Gamma(0))
.
By using standard arguments (again we refer to
[
18
,
45
]
) one can then verify that
\mathbf{u}_{T}(0)=\mathbf{u}_{T,0}
, and
\varphi(0)=\varphi_{0}
.
All in all we have shown Theorem
4.1
.

5.2
The logarithmic potential

As is common in the literature, see for example
[
18
,
25
]
, we consider a regularised version of this potential and use the preceding theory to show existence.
To this end, we choose
\delta\in(0,1)
and define a regularised function

F_{\log}^{\delta}(r)=\begin{cases}(1-r)\log(\delta)+(1+r)\log(2-\delta)+\frac{(1-r)^{2}}{2\delta}+\frac{(1+r)^{2}}{2(2-\delta)},&r\geq 1-\delta,\\
F_{\log}(r),&r\in(-1+\delta,1-\delta),\\
(1+r)\log(\delta)+(1-r)\log(2-\delta)+\frac{(1+r)^{2}}{2\delta}+\frac{(1-r)^{2}}{2(2-\delta)},&r\leq-1+\delta.\end{cases}

It is a straightforward calculation to see that
F_{\log}^{\delta}\in C^{2}(\mathbb{R})
.
As before, we also introduce shorthand notation
f^{\delta}(r)=(F_{\log}^{\delta})^{\prime}(r)
.

We now focus on the following version of (
4.4
)-(
4.6
).
We want to find
\varphi^{\delta}\in H^{1}_{H^{-1}}\cap L^{\infty}_{H^{1}}
,
\mu^{\delta}\in L^{2}_{H^{1}}
, and
\mathbf{u}_{T}^{\delta}\in H^{1}_{\mathbf{V}_{\sigma}^{\prime}}\cap L^{2}_{\mathbf{V}_{\sigma}}
such that

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathbf{m}_{*}(\partial^{\circ}\mathbf{u}_{T}^{\delta},\boldsymbol{\phi})+\hat{\mathbf{a}}(\eta(\varphi^{\delta}),\mathbf{u}_{T}^{\delta},\boldsymbol{\phi})+\mathbf{c}_{1}(\mathbf{u}_{T}^{\delta},\mathbf{u}_{T}^{\delta},\boldsymbol{\phi})+\mathbf{l}(\mathbf{u}_{T}^{\delta},\boldsymbol{\phi})+\mathbf{d}_{1}(\mathbf{u}_{T}^{\delta},\boldsymbol{\phi})+\mathbf{d}_{2}(\eta(\varphi^{\delta}),\boldsymbol{\phi})\\
=\mathbf{m}(\mathbf{B},\boldsymbol{\phi})+\mathbf{c}_{2}(\mu^{\delta},\varphi^{\delta},\boldsymbol{\phi})\end{split},

(5.14)

\displaystyle m_{*}(\partial^{\circ}\varphi^{\delta},\phi)+a(\mu^{\delta},\phi)+\mathbf{c}_{2}(\phi,\varphi^{\delta},\mathbf{u}_{T}^{\delta})+\mathbf{c}_{2}(\phi,\varphi^{\delta},\widetilde{\mathbf{u}_{T}}),

(5.15)

\displaystyle m(\mu^{\delta},\phi)=\varepsilon a(\varphi^{\delta},\phi)+\frac{\theta}{2\varepsilon}m(f^{\delta}(\varphi^{\delta}),\phi)-\frac{1}{\varepsilon}m(\varphi^{\delta},\phi),

(5.16)

for all
\phi\in H^{1}(\Gamma(t)),\boldsymbol{\phi}\in\mathbf{V}_{\sigma}(t)
and almost all
t\in[0,T]
, such that
\varphi^{\delta}(0)=\varphi_{0}\in\mathcal{I}_{0}
and
\mathbf{u}_{T}^{\delta}(0)=\mathbf{u}_{T,0}\in\mathbf{H}_{\sigma}(0).

Existence then follows from Theorem
4.1
.
However, as our polynomial conditions depend on our regularisation parameter,
\delta
, we now have to establish new energy estimates before passing to the limit
\delta\rightarrow 0
.

Lemma 5.4
.

The solution triple
(\varphi^{\delta},\mu^{\delta},\mathbf{u}_{T}^{\delta})
is such that

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\left(\frac{1}{2}\|\mathbf{u}_{T}^{\delta}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma(t))}^{2}+E^{\text{CH},\delta}[\varphi^{\delta};t]\right)+\frac{\eta_{*}}{2}\int_{0}^{T}\|\mathbb{E}(\mathbf{u}_{T}^{\delta})\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma(t))}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}\|\nabla_{\Gamma}\mu^{\delta}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma(t))}^{2}\leq C,

(5.17)

for a constant
C
independent of
\delta
.

Proof.

For the interest of brevity we try to recycle as much of the proof of the previous energy estimate.
Examining the proof of Lemma
5.2
one finds that the analogues of (
5.6
)-(
5.11
) still hold.
Continuing in the same way, we find that

-\mathbf{c}_{2}(\mu^{\delta},\varphi^{\delta},\widetilde{\mathbf{u}_{T}})=\mathbf{c}_{2}(\varphi^{\delta},\mu^{\delta},\widetilde{\mathbf{u}_{T}})-m(\mu^{\delta},HV_{N}\varphi^{\delta}).

In particular, all we need to establish is an
L^{2}
bound on
\mu^{\delta}
.
To do this we test (
5.16
) against
\varphi^{\delta}HV_{N}\in H^{1}(\Gamma(t))
for

\displaystyle m(\mu^{\delta},HV_{N}\varphi^{\delta})=\varepsilon a(\varphi^{\delta},HV_{N}\varphi^{\delta})+\frac{\theta}{2\varepsilon}m(f^{\delta}(\varphi^{\delta}),\varphi^{\delta}HV_{N})-\frac{1}{\varepsilon}m(\varphi^{\delta},\varphi^{\delta}HV_{N}).

(5.18)

We then notice that
rf^{\delta}(r)\geq 0
, and so

\frac{\theta}{2\varepsilon}m(f^{\delta}(\varphi^{\delta}),\varphi^{\delta}HV_{N})\leq\frac{\theta}{2\varepsilon}\|HV_{N}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Gamma(t))}\|f^{\delta}(\varphi^{\delta})\varphi^{\delta}\|_{L^{1}(\Gamma(t))}=\frac{\theta}{2\varepsilon}\|HV_{N}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Gamma(t))}m(f^{\delta}(\varphi^{\delta}),\varphi^{\delta}),

and using (
5.16
) we see

\displaystyle\frac{\theta}{2\varepsilon}m(f^{\delta}(\varphi^{\delta}),\varphi^{\delta})=m(\mu^{\delta},\varphi^{\delta})-\varepsilon a(\varphi^{\delta},\varphi^{\delta})+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}m(\varphi^{\delta},\varphi^{\delta}).

(5.19)

Now by using the inverse Laplacian, we see

m(\mu^{\delta},\varphi^{\delta})=m\left(\mu^{\delta},\varphi^{\delta}-\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.83337pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.11674pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.48965pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.31259pt}}\!\int_{\Gamma(t)}\varphi^{\delta}\right)+m\left(\mu^{\delta},\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.83337pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.11674pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.48965pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.31259pt}}\!\int_{\Gamma(t)}\varphi^{\delta}\right)=a\left(\mu^{\delta},\mathcal{G}\left(\varphi^{\delta}-\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.83337pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.11674pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.48965pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.31259pt}}\!\int_{\Gamma(t)}\varphi^{\delta}\right)\right)+m\left(\mu^{\delta},\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.83337pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.11674pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.48965pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.31259pt}}\!\int_{\Gamma(t)}\varphi^{\delta}\right).

Now by using the definition of the inverse Laplacian, and Poincaré’s inequality one finds

a\left(\mu^{\delta},\mathcal{G}\left(\varphi^{\delta}-\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.83337pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.11674pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.48965pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.31259pt}}\!\int_{\Gamma(t)}\varphi^{\delta}\right)\right)\leq C\|\nabla_{\Gamma}\mu^{\delta}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma(t))}\|\nabla_{\Gamma}\varphi^{\delta}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma(t))}.

Also, as we chose
\varphi_{0}\in\mathcal{I}_{0}
there exists some constant
\alpha<1
such that

m\left(\mu^{\delta},\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.83337pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.11674pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.48965pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.31259pt}}\!\int_{\Gamma(t)}\varphi^{\delta}\right)=m(\mu^{\delta},1)\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.83337pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-6.11674pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.48965pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.31259pt}}\!\int_{\Gamma(t)}\varphi^{\delta}\leq|m(\mu^{\delta},1)|m_{\varphi_{0}}(t)\leq\alpha|m(\mu^{\delta},1)|,

and hence we find

\displaystyle|m(\mu^{\delta},\varphi^{\delta})|\leq C\|\nabla_{\Gamma}\mu^{\delta}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma(t))}\|\nabla_{\Gamma}\varphi^{\delta}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma(t))}+\alpha|m(\mu^{\delta},1)|.

(5.20)

Testing (
5.15
) against
\phi\equiv 1
and noting that
f^{\delta}(r)\leq rf^{\delta}(r)+1
,

m(\mu^{\delta},1)=\frac{\theta}{2\varepsilon}m(f^{\delta}(\varphi^{\delta}),1)-\frac{1}{\varepsilon}m(\varphi^{\delta},1)\leq\frac{\theta}{2\varepsilon}|\Gamma(t)|+\frac{\theta}{2\varepsilon}m(f^{\delta}(\varphi^{\delta}),\varphi^{\delta})-\frac{1}{\varepsilon}m(\varphi^{\delta},1),

thus using (
5.19
) we see

\displaystyle|m(\mu^{\delta},1)|\leq C+C\|\nabla_{\Gamma}\varphi^{\delta}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma(t)}^{2}+|m(\varphi^{\delta},\mu^{\delta})|,

(5.21)

where we have used Young’s and Poincaré’s inequalities as appropriate.
Now by combining (
5.20
), (
5.21
) we see

|m(\mu^{\delta},\varphi^{\delta})|\leq C+C\|\nabla_{\Gamma}\varphi^{\delta}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma(t))}^{2}+C\|\nabla_{\Gamma}\mu^{\delta}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma(t))}\|\nabla_{\Gamma}\varphi^{\delta}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma(t))},

for constants which depend on
\alpha
.
Now using this and (
5.19
) in (
5.18
) one can show that

|m(\mu^{\delta},HV_{N}\varphi^{\delta})|\leq C+C\|\nabla_{\Gamma}\varphi^{\delta}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma(t))}^{2}+C\|\nabla_{\Gamma}\mu^{\delta}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma(t))}\|\nabla_{\Gamma}\varphi^{\delta}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma(t))},

for constants which depend on
\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|HV_{N}\|_{H^{1,\infty}(\Gamma(t))}
.
Now by using Young’s inequality it then follows that

|\mathbf{c}_{2}(\mu^{\delta},\varphi^{\delta},\widetilde{\mathbf{u}_{T}})|\leq C+C\|\nabla_{\Gamma}\varphi^{\delta}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma(t))}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\|\nabla_{\Gamma}\mu^{\delta}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma(t))}^{2},

which is the analogue of the bound (
5.12
).
Combining this with the analogues of (
5.6
)-(
5.11
) it is straightforward to see that

\frac{1}{2}\|\mathbf{u}_{T}^{M}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma(t))}^{2}+E^{\text{CH},\delta}[\varphi^{M};t]+\frac{\eta_{*}}{2}\int_{0}^{t}\|\mathbb{E}(\mathbf{u}_{T}^{M})\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma(t))}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{t}\|\nabla_{\Gamma}\mu^{M}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma(t))}^{2}\leq k\\
+\int_{0}^{t}K(s)\left(\frac{1}{2}\|\mathbf{u}_{T}^{M}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma(s))}^{2}+E^{\text{CH},\delta}[\varphi^{M};s]\right)\,ds,

(5.22)

k=C+\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}\|\mathbf{B}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma(s))}^{2}\,ds+\frac{2(\eta^{*})^{2}}{\eta_{*}}\int_{0}^{T}\|\mathbb{E}(\widetilde{\mathbf{u}_{T}})\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma(s))}^{2}\,ds+\frac{1}{2}\|\mathbf{u}_{T,0}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma(0))}^{2}+E^{\text{CH},\delta}[\varphi_{0};0],

and

\displaystyle K(s)=C+\frac{1}{2}\|HV_{N}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Gamma(s))}+\|V_{N}\mathbb{H}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{\infty}(\Gamma(s))}+\|\widetilde{\mathbf{u}_{T}}\|_{\mathbf{H}^{1,\infty}(\Gamma(s))}+C\|\widetilde{\mathbf{u}_{T}}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{\infty}(\Gamma(s))}^{\frac{4}{3}}.

The claim then follows from Grönwall’s inequality once we establish that
E^{\text{CH},\delta}[\varphi_{0};0]
is independent of
\delta
.
To see this we find that

E^{\text{CH},\delta}[\varphi_{0};0]=\int_{\Gamma(t)}\frac{\varepsilon|\nabla_{\Gamma}\varphi_{0}|^{2}}{2}+\frac{\theta}{2\varepsilon}F_{\log}^{\delta}(\varphi_{0})=\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\|\varphi_{0}\|_{H^{1}(\Gamma_{0})}^{2}+\frac{1}{2\varepsilon}\|1-\varphi_{0}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma_{0})}^{2}+\frac{\theta}{2\varepsilon}\int_{\{|\varphi_{0}|<1-\delta\}}F_{\log}(\varphi_{0})\\
+\frac{\theta}{2\varepsilon}\int_{\{\varphi_{0}\geq 1-\delta\}}F_{\log}^{\delta}(\varphi_{0})+\frac{\theta}{2\varepsilon}\int_{\{\varphi_{0}\leq-1+\delta\}}F_{\log}^{\delta}(\varphi_{0}),

and so it is only necessary to bound the last three terms.
Firstly, from the definition of
F_{\log}(\cdot)
, it is straightforward to see that

\frac{\theta}{2\varepsilon}\int_{\{|\varphi_{0}|<1-\delta\}}F_{\log}(\varphi_{0})\leq\frac{\theta\log(2)|\Gamma_{0}|}{\varepsilon}.

For the terms involving
F_{\log}^{\delta}(\cdot)
we use the set they’re integrating over to bound them, for example

\displaystyle\int_{\{\varphi_{0}\geq 1-\delta\}}(1-\varphi_{0})\log(\delta)+(1+\varphi_{0})\log(2-\delta)+\frac{(1-\varphi_{0})^{2}}{2\delta}+\frac{(1+\varphi_{0})^{2}}{2(2-\delta)}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\Gamma_{0}}-\delta\log(\delta)+2\log(2)+\frac{\delta}{2}+2

\displaystyle\leq\left(\frac{1}{e}+2\log(2)+\frac{5}{2}\right)|\Gamma_{0}|,

and the other term is bounded similarly.
Thus we see
E^{\text{CH},\delta}[\varphi_{0};0]
is bounded independent of
\delta
and we obtain (
5.17
).
∎

Notice that this proof also gave us uniform bounds for
\mu^{\delta}
in
L^{2}_{L^{2}}
.
We now use this energy estimate to obtain some further
\delta
-independent bounds.

Lemma 5.5
.

There exists a constant
C
independent of
\delta
such that:

1.

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\|\partial^{\circ}\mathbf{u}_{T}^{\delta}\|_{\mathbf{V}_{\sigma}(t)^{\prime}}^{2}+\int_{0}^{T}\|\partial^{\circ}\varphi^{\delta}\|_{H^{-1}(\Gamma(t))}^{2}\leq C,

2.

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\|f^{\delta}(\varphi^{\delta})\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma(t))}^{2}\leq C.

Proof.

The proof for the bounds on the time derivatives is identical to that of Lemma
5.3
, and is hence omitted.
The bound for the regularised potential term was not required in the setting of a smooth potential as we used the polynomial growth conditions and Sobolev embeddings to bound this term.
Testing (
5.16
) against
f^{\delta}(\varphi^{\delta})\in H^{1}(\Gamma(t))
yields

\|f^{\delta}(\varphi^{\delta})\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma(t)}^{2}=\frac{2\varepsilon}{\theta}m(\mu^{\delta},\varphi^{\delta})-\frac{2\varepsilon^{2}}{\theta}a(\varphi^{\delta},f^{\delta}(\varphi^{\delta}))+\frac{2}{\theta}m(\varphi^{\delta},f^{\delta}(\varphi^{\delta})).

Now we notice that as
(f^{\delta})^{\prime}(\cdot)\geq 0
one has

-a(\varphi^{\delta},f^{\delta}(\varphi^{\delta}))=-\int_{\Gamma(t)}(f^{\delta})^{\prime}(\varphi^{\delta})\nabla_{\Gamma}\varphi^{\delta}\cdot\nabla_{\Gamma}\varphi^{\delta}\leq 0,

hence by using Young’s inequality it is clear that

\|f^{\delta}(\varphi^{\delta})\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma(t)}^{2}\leq\frac{8}{\theta^{2}}\|\varphi^{\delta}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma(t))}^{2}+\varepsilon^{2}\|\mu^{\delta}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma(t))}^{2}.

The bound then follows from integrating in time and using the uniform
L^{2}_{L^{2}}
bounds.
∎

5.2.1
Passage to the limit

Now we want to pass to the limit as
\delta\rightarrow 0
.
We have established uniform bounds for
\mathbf{u}_{T}^{\delta}
in
L^{\infty}_{\mathbf{L}^{2}},L^{2}_{\mathbf{H^{1}}}
and
H^{1}_{\mathbf{V}_{\sigma}^{\prime}}
, hence there is a limiting function
\mathbf{u}_{T}
such that

\displaystyle\mathbf{u}_{T}^{\delta}\rightharpoonup\mathbf{u}_{T},\text{ weakly in }L^{2}_{\mathbf{V}_{\sigma}},

\displaystyle\mathbf{u}_{T}^{\delta}\overset{*}{\rightharpoonup}\mathbf{u}_{T},\text{ weak-}*\text{ in }L^{\infty}_{\mathbf{L}^{2}},

\displaystyle\mathbf{u}_{T}^{\delta}\rightharpoonup\mathbf{u}_{T},\text{ weakly in }H^{1}_{\mathbf{V}_{\sigma}^{\prime}}.

Likewise
\varphi^{\delta}
is uniformly bounded in
L^{\infty}_{H^{1}}
and
H^{1}_{H^{-1}}
, and
\mu^{\delta}
is uniformly bounded in
L^{2}_{H^{1}}
.
Thus, there exist limiting functions
\varphi,\mu
such that

\displaystyle\varphi^{\delta}\overset{*}{\rightharpoonup}\varphi,\text{ weak-}*\text{ in }L^{\infty}_{H^{1}},

\displaystyle\varphi^{\delta}\rightharpoonup\varphi,\text{ weakly in }H^{1}_{H^{-1}},

\displaystyle\mu^{\delta}\rightharpoonup\mu,\text{ weakly in }L^{2}_{H^{1}}.

Lastly, the uniform bounds for
f^{\delta}(\varphi^{\delta})
in
L^{2}_{L^{2}}
imply the existence of some
\tilde{\varphi}\in L^{2}_{L^{2}}
such that

\displaystyle f^{\delta}(\varphi^{\delta})\rightharpoonup\tilde{f},\text{ weakly in }L^{2}_{L^{2}}.

One also has the same strong convergence properties as seen for smooth potentials, which follow from the relevant compact embeddings.

It remains for us to show that the limiting functions are such that
|\varphi(t)|<1
almost everywhere on
\Gamma(t)
for almost all
t\in[0,T]
, and that
\tilde{f}=f(\varphi)
.
From the piecewise definition of
f^{\delta}
it is clear that these two issues are related.
We firstly recall a result from
[
18
]
.

Lemma 5.6

(
[
18
]
, Lemma 5.8)
.

There exist constants
C_{1},C_{2}
independent of
\delta,t
such that

\int_{\Gamma(t)}[\varphi^{\delta}(t)-1]_{+}+\int_{\Gamma(t)}[-\varphi^{\delta}(t)-1]_{+}\leq C_{1}\delta+\frac{C_{2}}{|\log(\delta)|},

for almost all
t\in[0,T]
.

The proof of this result is unchanged with the coupling with the Navier-Stokes equations, and hence follows without any adaptations.
In particular, in the limit
\delta\rightarrow 0
, we find that
|\varphi(t)|\leq 1
almost everywhere on
\Gamma(t)
for almost all
t\in[0,T]
.
There is still the issue of the set of values such that
|\varphi(t)|=1
, which we discuss using the same arguments as in
[
18
]
.

Lemma 5.7
.

For almost all
t\in[0,T]
the set
\{x\in\Gamma(t)\mid|\varphi(x,t)|=1\}
has
\mathcal{H}^{2}
measure 0.

Proof.

To begin, we claim that up to a subsequence of
\delta\rightarrow 0
we have for almost all
t\in[0,T]
that

\lim_{\delta\rightarrow 0}f^{\delta}(\varphi^{\delta}(t))=\begin{cases}f(\varphi(t)),&\text{ if }|\varphi(t)|<1\text{ almost everywhere on }\Gamma(t),\\
\infty,&\text{ otherwise.}\end{cases}

This is in fact exactly the content of
[
18
]
, Lemma 5.10 - and so we do not prove this.
Then we have that

\lim_{\delta\rightarrow 0}\varphi^{\delta}(t)f^{\delta}(\varphi^{\delta}(t))=\begin{cases}\varphi(t)f(\varphi(t)),&\text{ if }|\varphi(t)|<1\text{ almost everywhere on }\Gamma(t),\\
\infty,&\text{ otherwise,}\end{cases}

pointwise, almost everywhere on
\Gamma(t)
for almost all
t\in[0,T]
.
Now by testing (
5.16
) against
\varphi^{\delta}
, integrating in time, and using the established uniform bounds for
\varphi^{\delta}
and
\mu^{\delta}
one finds

\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Gamma(t)}\varphi^{\delta}f^{\delta}(\varphi^{\delta})\leq C,

for a constant
C
independent of
\delta
.
Now noting that
rf^{\delta}(r)\geq 0
, by Fatou’s lemma one has

\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Gamma(t)}\liminf_{\delta\rightarrow 0}\varphi^{\delta}f^{\delta}(\varphi^{\delta})\leq\liminf_{\delta\rightarrow 0}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Gamma(t)}\varphi^{\delta}f^{\delta}(\varphi^{\delta})\leq C.

From the claim it is now evident that one necessarily has that for almost all
t\in[0,T]
the set
\{x\in\Gamma(t)\mid|\varphi(x,t)|=1\}
has measure 0.
∎

In particular, we see that
f^{\delta}(\varphi^{\delta}(x,t))\rightarrow f(\varphi(x,t))
for almost all
x\in\Gamma(t),t\in[0,T]
.
Hence, again by using Fatou’s lemma, one finds

\int_{0}^{T}\|f(\varphi)\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma(t))}^{2}=\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Gamma(t)}\liminf_{\delta\rightarrow 0}|f^{\delta}(\varphi^{\delta})|^{2}\leq\liminf_{\delta\rightarrow 0}\int_{0}^{T}\|f^{\delta}(\varphi^{\delta})\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma(t))}^{2}\leq C,

and hence
\varphi(\varphi)\in L^{2}_{L^{2}}
.
Now by the uniqueness of weak limits and a suitable variant of the dominated convergence theorem for evolving surfaces (see
[
18
]
, Theorem B.2), one finds that
f(\varphi)=\tilde{f}
.

6
Proof of uniqueness

6.1
Uniqueness for the regular potential

In this section we prove the uniqueness of solutions to (
4.1
)-(
4.3
).
As a preliminary result, we note that by elliptic regularity theory one has that

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\|\varphi\|_{H^{2}(\Gamma(t))}^{2}\leq C\int_{0}^{T}\left(\|\mu\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma(t))}^{2}+\|F^{\prime}(\varphi)\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma(t))}^{2}\right).

(6.1)

This
L^{2}_{H^{2}}
regularity of
\varphi
is invaluable for proving the uniqueness of solutions, as it allows one to eliminate
\mu
from (
4.1
).
To see this we notice that for almost all
t\in[0,T]
, and all
\boldsymbol{\phi}\in\mathbf{V}_{\sigma}(t)

\mathbf{c}_{2}(\mu,\varphi,\boldsymbol{\phi})=\int_{\Gamma(t)}\mu\nabla_{\Gamma}\varphi\cdot\boldsymbol{\phi}=\int_{\Gamma(t)}\left(-\varepsilon\Delta_{\Gamma}\varphi+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}F^{\prime}(\varphi)\right)\nabla_{\Gamma}\varphi\cdot\boldsymbol{\phi}.

Now, as observed in the derivation, one formally calculates that

-\varepsilon\Delta_{\Gamma}\varphi\nabla_{\Gamma}\varphi=-\varepsilon\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot(\nabla_{\Gamma}\varphi\otimes\nabla_{\Gamma}\varphi)+\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\nabla_{\Gamma}|\nabla_{\Gamma}\varphi|^{2}-\varepsilon\left(\nabla_{\Gamma}\varphi\cdot\mathbb{H}\nabla_{\Gamma}\varphi\right)\boldsymbol{\nu},

almost everywhere on
\Gamma(t)
.
Thus one finds

\mathbf{c}_{2}(\mu,\varphi,\boldsymbol{\phi})=\int_{\Gamma(t)}\nabla_{\Gamma}\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{2}|\nabla_{\Gamma}\varphi|^{2}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}F(\varphi)\right)\cdot\boldsymbol{\phi}-\varepsilon\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot(\nabla_{\Gamma}\varphi\otimes\nabla_{\Gamma}\varphi)\cdot\boldsymbol{\phi},

where the normal term has vanished as
\boldsymbol{\phi}
is tangential.
Now using integration by parts, and the fact that
\boldsymbol{\phi}
is solenoidal, it is clear that

\displaystyle\int_{\Gamma(t)}\nabla_{\Gamma}\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{2}|\nabla_{\Gamma}\varphi|^{2}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}F(\varphi)\right)\cdot\boldsymbol{\phi}=\int_{\Gamma(t)}\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{2}|\nabla_{\Gamma}\varphi|^{2}\boldsymbol{\phi}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}F(\varphi)\boldsymbol{\phi}\right)-\int_{\Gamma(t)}\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{2}|\nabla_{\Gamma}\varphi|^{2}+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}F(\varphi)\right)\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot\boldsymbol{\phi}=0,

\displaystyle-\varepsilon\int_{\Gamma(t)}\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot(\nabla_{\Gamma}\varphi\otimes\nabla_{\Gamma}\varphi))\cdot\boldsymbol{\phi}=\varepsilon\int_{\Gamma(t)}(\nabla_{\Gamma}\varphi\otimes\nabla_{\Gamma}\varphi):\nabla_{\Gamma}\boldsymbol{\phi}.

Hence we find

\mathbf{c}_{2}(\mu,\varphi,\boldsymbol{\phi})=\varepsilon\mathbf{c}_{3}(\varphi,\varphi,\boldsymbol{\phi}),

where the trilinear form
\mathbf{c}_{3}
is defined as

\mathbf{c}_{3}(t;\phi,\psi,\boldsymbol{\chi}):=\int_{\Gamma(t)}(\nabla_{\Gamma}\phi\otimes\nabla_{\Gamma}\psi):\nabla_{\Gamma}\boldsymbol{\chi}.

The structure of this proof is similar to that in
[
29
]
, with relevant modifications for an evolving surface - as discussed in Appendix
B
.

Theorem 6.1
.

Let
\Gamma(t),F
be such that the assumptions in Theorem
4.1
hold.
Moreover, assume
F_{2}^{\prime}
is Lipschitz continuous.
Then the solution triple,
(\varphi,\mu,\mathbf{u}_{T})
solving (
4.1
)-(
4.3
) is unique.

The first step is to observe that if we have two solution triples,
(\varphi^{i},\mu^{i},\mathbf{u}_{T}^{i})
,
i=1,2
, and defining

\displaystyle\bar{\varphi}:=\varphi^{1}-\varphi^{2},

\displaystyle\bar{\mu}:=\mu^{1}-\mu^{2},

\displaystyle\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}:=\mathbf{u}_{T}^{1}-\mathbf{u}_{T}^{2},

then these solve the system

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathbf{m}_{*}\left(\partial^{\circ}{\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}},\boldsymbol{\phi}\right)+\hat{\mathbf{a}}(\eta(\varphi^{1}),\mathbf{u}_{T}^{1},\boldsymbol{\phi})-\hat{\mathbf{a}}(\eta(\varphi^{2}),\mathbf{u}_{T}^{2},\boldsymbol{\phi})+\mathbf{c}_{1}(\mathbf{u}_{T}^{1},\mathbf{u}_{T}^{1},\boldsymbol{\phi})-\mathbf{c}_{1}(\mathbf{u}_{T}^{2},\mathbf{u}_{T}^{2},\boldsymbol{\phi})+\mathbf{l}(\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}},\boldsymbol{\phi})+\mathbf{d}_{1}(\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}},\boldsymbol{\phi})\\
+\mathbf{d}_{2}(\eta(\varphi^{1})-\eta(\varphi^{2}),\boldsymbol{\phi})=\varepsilon\mathbf{c}_{3}(\varphi^{1},\varphi^{1},\boldsymbol{\phi})-\varepsilon\mathbf{c}_{3}(\varphi^{2},\varphi^{2},\boldsymbol{\phi})\end{split},

(6.2)

\displaystyle m_{*}\left(\partial^{\circ}{\bar{\varphi}},\phi\right)+a(\bar{\mu},\phi)+\mathbf{c}_{2}(\phi,\varphi^{1},\mathbf{u}_{T}^{1})-\mathbf{c}_{2}(\phi,\varphi^{2},\mathbf{u}_{T}^{2})+\mathbf{c}_{2}(\phi,\bar{\varphi},\widetilde{\mathbf{u}_{T}})=0,

(6.3)

\displaystyle m(\bar{\mu},\phi)=\varepsilon a(\bar{\varphi},\phi)+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}m(F^{\prime}(\varphi^{1})-F^{\prime}(\varphi^{2}),\phi),

(6.4)

for almost all
t\in[0,T]
, and all
\phi\in H^{1}(\Gamma(t)),\boldsymbol{\phi}\in\mathbf{V}_{\sigma}(t)
.
This proof firstly requires obtaining bounds for
\bar{\varphi},P_{\mathcal{K}}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}},P_{\mathcal{K}^{\perp}}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}
in appropriate norms.
We refer to Appendix
B
for definitions and properties of the operators
P_{\mathcal{K}},P_{\mathcal{K}^{\perp}}
.

The proof relies on proving the following differential inequalities.

Lemma 6.2
.

P_{\mathcal{K}^{\perp}}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}
is such that

\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dt}\|P_{\mathcal{K}^{\perp}}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}\|_{\perp}^{2}+\frac{\eta_{*}}{2}\|P_{\mathcal{K}^{\perp}}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma(t))}^{2}\leq C\|P_{\mathcal{K}}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma(t))}^{2}+\frac{\varepsilon}{4}\|\nabla_{\Gamma}\bar{\varphi}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma(t))}\\
+K_{1}(t)\|P_{\mathcal{K}^{\perp}}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma(t))}^{2}+C_{\log}(\bar{\varphi},\mathbf{u}_{T}^{1})\|P_{\mathcal{K}^{\perp}}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}\|_{\perp},

(6.5)

where

K_{1}(t)=C\left(\|\mathbb{E}(\mathbf{u}_{T}^{1})\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma(t))}^{2}+\|\mathbf{u}_{T}^{1}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{4}(\Gamma(t))}^{4}+\|\mathbf{u}_{T}^{2}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{4}(\Gamma(t))}^{4}+\|\varphi^{1}\|_{H^{1,4}(\Gamma(t))}^{4}+\|\varphi^{2}\|_{H^{1,4}(\Gamma(t))}^{4}\right),

and

C_{\log}(\bar{\varphi},\mathbf{u}_{T}^{1})=C\log\left(\frac{C_{2}(\|\nabla_{\Gamma}\bar{\varphi}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma(t))}+\|\bar{\varphi}\|_{H^{2}(\Gamma(t))})}{\|\nabla_{\Gamma}\bar{\varphi}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma(t))}^{2}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\|\nabla_{\Gamma}\bar{\varphi}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma(t))}\|\mathbb{E}(\mathbf{u}_{T}^{1})\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma(t))}.

P_{\mathcal{K}}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}
is such that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dt}\|P_{\mathcal{K}}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma(t))}^{2}\leq\frac{\eta_{*}}{4}\|P_{\mathcal{K}^{\perp}}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma(t))}^{2}+\frac{\varepsilon}{4}\|\nabla_{\Gamma}\bar{\varphi}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma(t))}^{2}+K_{2}(t)\|P_{\mathcal{K}}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma(t))}^{2},

(6.6)

where

K_{2}(t)=C\left(1+\|\widetilde{\mathbf{u}_{T}}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{4}(\Gamma(t))}^{2}+\|\mathbf{u}_{T}^{1}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{4}(\Gamma(t))}^{2}+\|\mathbf{u}_{T}^{2}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{4}(\Gamma(t))}^{2}+\|\varphi^{1}\|_{H^{1,4}(\Gamma(t))}^{4}+\|\varphi^{2}\|_{H^{1,4}(\Gamma(t))}^{4}\right).

Lastly,
\bar{\varphi}
is such that

\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dt}\|\bar{\varphi}\|_{-1}^{2}+\varepsilon\|\nabla_{\Gamma}\bar{\varphi}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma(t))}^{2}\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{4}\|\nabla_{\Gamma}\bar{\varphi}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma(t))}^{2}+\frac{\eta_{*}}{4}\|P_{\mathcal{K}}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma(t))}^{2}+\frac{\eta_{*}}{4}\|P_{\mathcal{K}^{\perp}}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma(t))}^{2}\\
+K_{3}(t)\|\bar{\varphi}\|_{-1}^{2},

(6.7)

where

K_{3}(t)=C\left(1+\|\widetilde{\mathbf{u}_{T}}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{\infty}(\Gamma(t))}^{2}+\|\mathbf{u}_{T}^{1}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{4}(\Gamma(t))}^{2}+\|\varphi^{2}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Gamma(t))}^{2}\right).

Proof of uniqueness.

With these bounds we are now in a position to show uniqueness.
Taking the sum of (
6.5
), (
6.6
), (
6.7
) one finds

\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dt}\left(\|P_{\mathcal{K}^{\perp}}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}\|_{\perp}^{2}+\|P_{\mathcal{K}}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma(t))}^{2}+\|\bar{\varphi}\|_{-1}^{2}\right)+\frac{\varepsilon}{4}\|\nabla_{\Gamma}\bar{\varphi}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma(t))}^{2}\\
\leq K(t)\left(\|P_{\mathcal{K}^{\perp}}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}\|_{\perp}^{2}+\|P_{\mathcal{K}}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma(t))}^{2}+\|\bar{\varphi}\|_{-1}^{2}\right)\\
+C_{\log}(\bar{\varphi},\mathbf{u}_{T}^{1})\left(\|P_{\mathcal{K}^{\perp}}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}\|_{\perp}^{2}+\|P_{\mathcal{K}}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma(t))}^{2}+\|\bar{\varphi}\|_{-1}^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}},

where

K(t)=K_{1}(t)+K_{2}(t)+K_{3}(t).

Now recall that we have
\mathbf{u}_{T}^{i}\in L^{\infty}_{\mathbf{L}^{2}}\cap L^{2}_{\mathbf{H}^{1}},\varphi^{i}\in L^{\infty}_{H^{1}}\cap L^{2}_{H^{2}}
, and in particular this implies
\varphi^{i}\in L^{4}_{H^{1,4}}
as

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\|\varphi^{i}\|_{H^{1,4}(\Gamma(t))}^{4}

\displaystyle\leq C\int_{0}^{T}\|\varphi^{i}\|_{H^{1}(\Gamma(t))}^{2}\|\varphi^{i}\|_{H^{2}(\Gamma(t))}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C\left(\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|\varphi^{i}\|_{H^{1}(\Gamma(t))}^{2}\right)\int_{0}^{T}\|\varphi^{i}\|_{H^{2}(\Gamma(t))}^{2}<\infty.

Moreover
\|P_{\mathcal{K}^{\perp}}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}(0)\|_{\perp}^{2}+\|P_{\mathcal{K}}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}(0)\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma(t))}+\|\bar{\varphi}(0)\|_{-1}^{2}=0
by definition, we see that we may use Lemma
3.10
to see that
\|P_{\mathcal{K}^{\perp}}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}\|_{\perp},\|P_{\mathcal{K}}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma(t))},\|\bar{\varphi}\|_{-1}
all vanish on
[0,T]
.
In particular, since
\|\cdot\|_{\perp}
and
\|\cdot\|_{-1}
are norms on
\mathcal{K}^{\perp}
and
\{z\in H^{-1}(\Gamma(t))\mid m_{*}(z,1)=0\}
respectively we see that

P_{\mathcal{K}^{\perp}}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}=0,\quad P_{\mathcal{K}}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}=0,\quad\bar{\varphi}=0,

and as
\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}=P_{\mathcal{K}^{\perp}}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}+P_{\mathcal{K}}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}
we see
\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}
vanishes on
[0,T]
also.
From this one can readily show that
\bar{\mu}=0
on
[0,T]
and hence determine uniqueness of weak solutions.
∎

6.1.1
Proof of Lemma
6.2

Proof.

Firstly we establish the most complicated inequality, (
6.5
).
Testing (
6.2
) with
\mathcal{S}^{\perp}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}
, as defined in Appendix
B
, and rewriting terms in a suitable way we find that

\mathbf{m}_{*}\left(\partial^{\circ}{\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}},\mathcal{S}^{\perp}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}\right)+\hat{\mathbf{a}}(\eta(\varphi^{1})-\eta(\varphi^{2}),\mathbf{u}_{T}^{1},\mathcal{S}^{\perp}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}})+\hat{\mathbf{a}}(\eta(\varphi^{2}),\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}},\mathcal{S}^{\perp}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}})+\mathbf{c}_{1}(\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}},\mathbf{u}_{T}^{1},\mathcal{S}^{\perp}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}})+\mathbf{c}_{1}(\mathbf{u}_{T}^{2},\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}},\mathcal{S}^{\perp}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}})\\
+\mathbf{l}(\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}},\mathcal{S}^{\perp}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}})+\mathbf{d}_{1}(\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}},\mathcal{S}^{\perp}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}})+\mathbf{d}_{2}(\eta(\varphi^{1})-\eta(\varphi^{2}),\mathcal{S}^{\perp}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}})=\varepsilon\mathbf{c}_{3}(\bar{\varphi},\varphi^{1},\mathcal{S}^{\perp}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}})+\varepsilon\mathbf{c}_{3}(\varphi^{2},\bar{\varphi},\mathcal{S}^{\perp}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}).

(6.8)

Firstly, we claim that

\mathbf{m}_{*}\left(\partial^{\circ}{\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}},\mathcal{S}^{\perp}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}\right)=\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dt}\|P_{\mathcal{K}^{\perp}}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}\|_{\perp}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{b}(\mathcal{S}^{\perp}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}},\mathcal{S}^{\perp}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}})-\mathbf{m}(P_{\mathcal{K}^{\perp}}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}HV_{N},\mathcal{S}^{\perp}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}})\\
+\sum_{i=1}^{N_{K}}\mathbf{m}(\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}},\boldsymbol{\kappa}_{i})\mathbf{m}(\mathcal{S}^{\perp}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}},\partial^{\circ}\boldsymbol{\kappa}_{i}),

(6.9)

where we are using the notation from Appendix
B
.
To see this we write

\mathbf{m}_{*}\left(\partial^{\circ}{\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}},\mathcal{S}^{\perp}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}\right)=\frac{d}{dt}\mathbf{m}(\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}},\mathcal{S}^{\perp}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}})-\mathbf{m}\left(\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}},\partial^{\circ}{\mathcal{S}^{\perp}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}}\right)-\mathbf{m}\left(\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}HV_{N},\mathcal{S}^{\perp}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}\right).

Since
\mathcal{S}^{\perp}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}\in\mathcal{K}^{\perp}(t)
we see that
\mathbf{m}(\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}},\mathcal{S}^{\perp}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}})=\mathbf{m}(P_{\mathcal{K}^{\perp}}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}},\mathcal{S}^{\perp}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}})
, and hence by the definition of
\mathcal{S}^{\perp}

\frac{d}{dt}\mathbf{m}(\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}},\mathcal{S}^{\perp}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}})=\frac{d}{dt}\|P_{\mathcal{K}^{\perp}}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}\|_{\perp}^{2},

and so all that remains is to consider
\mathbf{m}\left(\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}},\partial^{\circ}{\mathcal{S}^{\perp}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}}\right)
.
Firstly, we split this term into two parts

\mathbf{m}\left(\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}},\partial^{\circ}{\mathcal{S}^{\perp}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}}\right)=\mathbf{m}\left(P_{\mathcal{K}}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}},\partial^{\circ}{\mathcal{S}^{\perp}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}}\right)+\mathbf{m}\left(P_{\mathcal{K}^{\perp}}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}},\partial^{\circ}{\mathcal{S}^{\perp}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}}\right).

We write this first term as

\mathbf{m}\left(P_{\mathcal{K}}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}},\partial^{\circ}{\mathcal{S}^{\perp}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}}\right)=\frac{d}{dt}\mathbf{m}\left(P_{\mathcal{K}}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}},\mathcal{S}^{\perp}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}\right)-\mathbf{m}\left(P_{\mathcal{K}}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}HV_{N},\mathcal{S}^{\perp}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}\right)-\mathbf{m}_{*}\left(\partial^{\circ}P_{\mathcal{K}}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}},\mathcal{S}^{\perp}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}\right),

where clearly
\mathbf{m}\left(P_{\mathcal{K}}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}},\mathcal{S}^{\perp}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}\right)=0
.
Next we use (
B.5
) to see that

\mathbf{m}_{*}\left(\partial^{\circ}P_{\mathcal{K}}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}},\mathcal{S}^{\perp}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}\right)=\mathbf{m}_{*}\left(\partial^{\circ}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}},P_{\mathcal{K}}\mathcal{S}^{\perp}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}\right)\\
+\sum_{i=1}^{N_{K}}\left[\mathbf{m}(\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}},\partial^{\circ}\boldsymbol{\kappa}_{i})+\mathbf{m}(\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}HV_{N},\boldsymbol{\kappa}_{i})\right]\mathbf{m}(\mathcal{S}^{\perp}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}},\boldsymbol{\kappa}_{i})+\mathbf{m}(\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}},\boldsymbol{\kappa}_{i})\mathbf{m}(\mathcal{S}^{\perp}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}},\partial^{\circ}\boldsymbol{\kappa}_{i}),

which simplifies to

\mathbf{m}_{*}\left(\partial^{\circ}P_{\mathcal{K}}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}},\mathcal{S}^{\perp}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}\right)=\sum_{i=1}^{N_{K}}\mathbf{m}(\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}},\boldsymbol{\kappa}_{i})\mathbf{m}(\mathcal{S}^{\perp}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}},\partial^{\circ}\boldsymbol{\kappa}_{i}).

Next we write

\mathbf{m}\left(P_{\mathcal{K}^{\perp}}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}},\partial^{\circ}{\mathcal{S}^{\perp}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}}\right)=\mathbf{a}(\mathcal{S}^{\perp}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}},\partial^{\circ}\mathcal{S}^{\perp}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}})=\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dt}\|P_{\mathcal{K}^{\perp}}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}\|_{\perp}^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{b}(\mathcal{S}^{\perp}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}},\mathcal{S}^{\perp}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}).

Combining all of this together one obtains (
6.9
).

Next we rewrite the second
\hat{\mathbf{a}}
term in (
6.8
) by using integration by parts.
To do this we note that
\mathcal{S}^{\perp}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}\in L^{2}_{H^{2}}
and we have the bound (
B.4
).
Integration by parts yields

\hat{\mathbf{a}}(\eta(\varphi^{2}),\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}},\mathcal{S}^{\perp}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}})=\int_{\Gamma(t)}\eta(\varphi^{2})\mathbb{E}(\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}):\mathbb{E}(\mathcal{S}^{\perp}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}})=-\int_{\Gamma(t)}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}\cdot\mathbb{P}\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot\left(\eta(\varphi^{2})\mathbb{E}(\mathcal{S}^{\perp}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}})\right)\\
=-\int_{\Gamma(t)}\eta^{\prime}(\varphi^{2})\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}\cdot\mathbb{E}(\mathcal{S}^{\perp}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}})\nabla_{\Gamma}\varphi^{2}+\int_{\Gamma(t)}\eta(\varphi^{2})\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}\cdot P_{\mathcal{K}^{\perp}}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}},

where we have used the fact that
-\mathbb{P}\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot\mathbb{E}(\mathcal{S}^{\perp}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}})=P_{\mathcal{K}^{\perp}}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}
a.e. on
\Gamma(t)
.
Hence one obtains

\hat{\mathbf{a}}(\eta(\varphi^{2}),\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}},\mathcal{S}^{\perp}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}})=-\int_{\Gamma(t)}\eta^{\prime}(\varphi^{2})\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}\cdot\mathbb{E}(\mathcal{S}^{\perp}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}})\nabla_{\Gamma}\varphi^{2}+\int_{\Gamma(t)}\eta(\varphi^{2})P_{\mathcal{K}^{\perp}}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}\cdot P_{\mathcal{K}^{\perp}}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}\\
+\int_{\Gamma(t)}\eta(\varphi^{2})P_{\mathcal{K}}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}\cdot P_{\mathcal{K}^{\perp}}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}.

(6.10)

Next we bound the other
\hat{\mathbf{a}}
term.
To do this we write

\hat{\mathbf{a}}(\eta(\varphi^{1})-\eta(\varphi^{2}),\mathbf{u}_{T}^{1},\mathcal{S}^{\perp}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}})=\int_{\Gamma(t)}\left(\int_{0}^{1}\eta^{\prime}(su^{1}+(1-s)\varphi^{2})\bar{\varphi}\,ds\right)\mathbb{E}(\mathbf{u}_{T}^{1}):\mathbb{E}(\mathcal{S}^{\perp}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}),

and noting the boundedness of
\eta^{\prime}
we obtain the bound

\hat{\mathbf{a}}(\eta(\varphi^{1})-\eta(\varphi^{2}),\mathbf{u}_{T}^{1},\mathcal{S}^{\perp}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}})\leq C\|\bar{\varphi}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Gamma(t))}\|\mathbb{E}(\mathbf{u}_{T}^{1})\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma(t))}\|P_{\mathcal{K}^{\perp}}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}\|_{\perp}.

Now we use Lemma
3.11
, and Poincaré’s inequality, to see that

\|\bar{\varphi}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Gamma(t))}\leq C\|\nabla_{\Gamma}\bar{\varphi}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma(t))}\left(1+\log\left(1+\frac{C\|\bar{\varphi}\|_{H^{2}(\Gamma(t))}}{\|\nabla_{\Gamma}\bar{\varphi}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma(t))}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\right),

where we note from (
6.1
) that
\bar{\varphi}
is sufficiently smooth for this.
Now from the
L^{\infty}_{H^{1}}
bounds for
\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2}
we see that there is a constant,
C_{1}
, such that
\|\nabla_{\Gamma}\bar{\varphi}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma(t))}\leq C_{1}
for almost all
t\in[0,T]
.
Hence one can find a sufficiently large constant
C_{2}
so that

\log\left(1+\frac{C\|\bar{\varphi}\|_{H^{2}(\Gamma(t))}}{\|\nabla_{\Gamma}\bar{\varphi}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma(t))}}\right)\leq\log\left(\frac{C_{2}(\|\nabla_{\Gamma}\bar{\varphi}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma)}+\|\bar{\varphi}\|_{H^{2}(\Gamma(t))})}{\|\nabla_{\Gamma}\bar{\varphi}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma(t))}^{2}}\right).

Moreover for
C_{2}>1
this logarithmic term is positive, and we will ultimately be able to apply Lemma
3.10
.
All in all this gives us the bound

|\hat{\mathbf{a}}(\eta(\varphi^{1})-\eta(\varphi^{2}),\mathbf{u}_{T}^{1},\mathcal{S}^{\perp}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}})|\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{8}\|\nabla_{\Gamma}\bar{\varphi}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma(t))}^{2}+C\|\mathbb{E}(\mathbf{u}_{T}^{1})\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma(t))}^{2}\|P_{\mathcal{K}^{\perp}}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}\|_{\perp}^{2}\\
+C\log\left(\frac{C_{2}(\|\nabla_{\Gamma}\bar{\varphi}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma(t))}+\|\bar{\varphi}\|_{H^{2}(\Gamma(t))})}{\|\nabla_{\Gamma}\bar{\varphi}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma(t))}^{2}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\|\nabla_{\Gamma}\bar{\varphi}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma(t))}\|\mathbb{E}(\mathbf{u}_{T}^{1})\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma(t))}\|P_{\mathcal{K}^{\perp}}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}\|_{\perp},

(6.11)

where we have used Young’s inequality where appropriate.

Now we bound the
\mathbf{c}_{1}
terms.
Firstly we note that

|\mathbf{c}_{1}(\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}},\mathbf{u}_{T}^{1},\mathcal{S}^{\perp}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}})|=|\mathbf{c}_{1}(\mathcal{S}^{\perp}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}},\mathbf{u}_{T}^{1},\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}})|\leq\|\mathcal{S}^{\perp}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}\|_{\mathbf{H}^{1,4}(\Gamma(t))}\|\mathbf{u}_{T}^{1}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{4}(\Gamma(t))}\|\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma(t))}.

We recall the interpolation inequality
7
7

7

See
[
10
]
, and note that a
C^{3}
surface is sufficiently smooth for this to hold.
This can be extended to evolving surfaces with a time independent constant as in
[
45
]
Lemma 3.4.
,

\|\mathcal{S}^{\perp}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}\|_{\mathbf{H}^{1,4}(\Gamma(t))}\leq C\|\mathcal{S}^{\perp}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}\|_{\mathbf{H}^{1}(\Gamma(t))}^{\frac{1}{2}}\|\mathcal{S}^{\perp}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}\|_{\mathbf{H}^{2}(\Gamma(t))}^{\frac{1}{2}},

and use (
B.1
), (
B.4
) to see that

\|\mathcal{S}^{\perp}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}\|_{\mathbf{H}^{1,4}(\Gamma(t))}\leq C\|P_{\mathcal{K}^{\perp}}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}\|_{\perp}^{\frac{1}{2}}\|P_{\mathcal{K}^{\perp}}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma(t))}^{\frac{1}{2}}\leq C\|P_{\mathcal{K}^{\perp}}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}\|_{\perp}^{\frac{1}{2}}\|\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma(t))}^{\frac{1}{2}}.

Hence we observe that

|\mathbf{c}_{1}(\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}},\mathbf{u}_{T}^{1},\mathcal{S}^{\perp}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}})|\leq C\|\mathbf{u}_{T}^{1}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{4}(\Gamma(t))}\|P_{\mathcal{K}^{\perp}}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}\|_{\perp}^{\frac{1}{2}}\|\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma(t))}^{\frac{3}{2}},

and hence Young’s inequality yields

\displaystyle|\mathbf{c}_{1}(\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}},\mathbf{u}_{T}^{1},\mathcal{S}^{\perp}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}})|\leq C\|\mathbf{u}_{T}^{1}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{4}(\Gamma(t))}^{4}\|P_{\mathcal{K}^{\perp}}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}\|_{\perp}^{2}+\frac{\eta_{*}}{14}\|\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma(t))}^{2}.

(6.12)

An identical argument yields

\displaystyle|\mathbf{c}_{1}(\mathbf{u}_{T}^{2},\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}},\mathcal{S}^{\perp}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}})|\leq C\|\mathbf{u}_{T}^{2}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{4}(\Gamma(t))}^{4}\|P_{\mathcal{K}^{\perp}}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}\|_{\perp}^{2}+\frac{\eta_{*}}{14}\|\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma(t))}^{2}.

(6.13)

We now turn to the contributions from the evolution of the surface, that is the terms involving
\mathbf{l},\mathbf{d}_{1},\mathbf{d}_{2}
, which would vanish for a stationary surface.
The simplest of these terms is

\displaystyle\mathbf{l}(\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}},\mathcal{S}^{\perp}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}})\leq C\|\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma(t))}\|\mathcal{S}^{\perp}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma(t))}\leq\frac{\eta_{*}}{14}\|\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma(t))}^{2}+C\|P_{\mathcal{K}^{\perp}}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}\|_{\perp}^{2},

(6.14)

where we have use Young’s inequality and (
B.1
).
Next we look at

\displaystyle\mathbf{d}_{2}(\eta(\varphi^{1})-\eta(\varphi^{2}),\mathcal{S}^{\perp}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}})\leq C\|\bar{\varphi}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma(t))}\|P_{\mathcal{K}^{\perp}}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}\|_{\perp}\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{12}\|\nabla_{\Gamma}\bar{\varphi}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma(t))}^{2}+C\|P_{\mathcal{K}^{\perp}}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}\|_{\perp}^{2},

(6.15)

which follows similarly to the above inequality, but we have also use the Lipschitz continuity of
\eta(\cdot)
, Poincaré’s inequality, and the uniform bounds on
\widetilde{\mathbf{u}_{T}}
.
Finally to bound the
\mathbf{d}_{1}
term we see

\mathbf{d}_{1}(\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}},\mathcal{S}^{\perp}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}})=\mathbf{c}_{1}(\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}},\widetilde{\mathbf{u}_{T}},\mathcal{S}^{\perp}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}})+\mathbf{c}_{1}(\widetilde{\mathbf{u}_{T}},\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}},\mathcal{S}^{\perp}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}})\\
=-\mathbf{c}_{1}(\mathcal{S}^{\perp}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}},\widetilde{\mathbf{u}_{T}},\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}})-\mathbf{c}_{1}(\mathcal{S}^{\perp}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}},\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}},\widetilde{\mathbf{u}_{T}})+2\mathbf{m}(\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}HV_{N},\mathcal{S}^{\perp}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}),

where we have used the antisymmetry of
\mathbf{c}_{1}
(for solenoidal functions), and the extra term comes from the fact that
\nabla_{\Gamma}\cdot\widetilde{\mathbf{u}_{T}}=-HV_{N}
.
From this one readily sees that

|\mathbf{d}_{1}(\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}},\mathcal{S}^{\perp}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}})|\leq C\|P_{\mathcal{K}^{\perp}}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}\|_{\perp}\|\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma(t))}\|\widetilde{\mathbf{u}_{T}}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{\infty}(\Gamma(t))}+C\|\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma(t))}\|P_{\mathcal{K}^{\perp}}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}\|_{\perp},

where we have used (
B.1
).
This clearly yields

\displaystyle|\mathbf{d}_{1}(\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}},\mathcal{S}^{\perp}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}})|\leq C\|\widetilde{\mathbf{u}_{T}}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{\infty}(\Gamma(t))}^{2}\|P_{\mathcal{K}^{\perp}}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}\|_{\perp}^{2}+\frac{\eta_{*}}{14}\|\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma(t))}^{2}.

(6.16)

The last terms for us to bound are the
\mathbf{c}_{3}
contributions, from which one readily sees from Young’s inequality that

\displaystyle\varepsilon|\mathbf{c}_{3}(\bar{\varphi},\varphi^{1},\mathcal{S}^{\perp}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}})|+\varepsilon|\mathbf{c}_{3}(\varphi^{2},\bar{\varphi},\mathcal{S}^{\perp}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}})|\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{12}\|\nabla_{\Gamma}\bar{\varphi}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma(t))}^{2}+C\left(\|\varphi^{1}\|_{H^{1,4}(\Gamma(t))}^{2}+\|\varphi^{2}\|_{H^{1,4}(\Gamma(t))}^{2}\right)\|\mathcal{S}^{\perp}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}\|_{\mathbf{H}^{1,4}(\Gamma(t))}^{2}.

Again using the above interpolation inequality we see that

\|\mathcal{S}^{\perp}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}\|_{\mathbf{H}^{1,4}(\Gamma(t))}^{2}\leq C\|P_{\mathcal{K}^{\perp}}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}\|_{\perp}\|\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma(t))}.

Hence using Young’s inequality we find

\varepsilon|\mathbf{c}_{3}(\bar{\varphi},\varphi^{1},\mathcal{S}^{\perp}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}})|+\varepsilon|\mathbf{c}_{3}(\varphi^{2},\bar{\varphi},\mathcal{S}^{\perp}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}})|\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{12}\|\nabla_{\Gamma}\bar{\varphi}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma(t))}^{2}+C\left(\|\varphi^{1}\|_{H^{1,4}(\Gamma(t))}^{4}+\|\varphi^{2}\|_{H^{1,4}(\Gamma(t))}^{4}\right)\|P_{\mathcal{K}^{\perp}}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}\|_{\perp}^{2}\\
+\frac{\eta_{*}}{14}\|\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma(t))}^{2},

(6.17)

where we note that
\varphi^{i}\in L^{4}_{H^{1,4}}
as shown above.

Now we use (
6.9
)-(
6.17
) in (
6.8
) to see that

\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dt}\|P_{\mathcal{K}^{\perp}}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}\|_{\perp}^{2}+\int_{\Gamma(t)}\eta(\varphi^{2})P_{\mathcal{K}^{\perp}}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}\cdot P_{\mathcal{K}^{\perp}}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}\leq\left|\int_{\Gamma(t)}\eta^{\prime}(\varphi^{2})\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}\cdot\mathbb{E}(\mathcal{S}^{\perp}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}})\nabla_{\Gamma}\varphi^{2}\right|+\left|\int_{\Gamma(t)}\eta(\varphi^{2})P_{\mathcal{K}}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}\cdot P_{\mathcal{K}^{\perp}}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}\right|\\
+\frac{\varepsilon}{4}\|\nabla_{\Gamma}\bar{\varphi}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma(t))}^{2}+C\|\mathbb{E}(\mathbf{u}_{T}^{1})\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma(t))}^{2}\|P_{\mathcal{K}^{\perp}}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}\|_{\perp}^{2}+C\left(\|\mathbf{u}_{T}^{1}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{4}(\Gamma(t))}^{4}+\|\mathbf{u}_{T}^{2}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{4}(\Gamma(t))}^{4}\right)\|P_{\mathcal{K}^{\perp}}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}\|_{\perp}^{2}+\frac{6\eta_{*}}{14}\|\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma(t))}^{2}\\
+\frac{\eta_{*}}{14}\|P_{\mathcal{K}^{\perp}}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma(t))}^{2}+C\left(\|\varphi^{1}\|_{H^{1,4}(\Gamma(t))}^{4}+\|\varphi^{2}\|_{H^{1,4}(\Gamma(t))}^{4}\right)\|P_{\mathcal{K}^{\perp}}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}\|_{\perp}^{2}\\
+C\log\left(\frac{C_{2}(\|\nabla_{\Gamma}\bar{\varphi}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma(t))}+\|\bar{\varphi}\|_{H^{2}(\Gamma(t))})}{\|\nabla_{\Gamma}\bar{\varphi}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma(t))}^{2}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\|\nabla_{\Gamma}\bar{\varphi}\|_{L^{2}(\Gamma(t))}\|\mathbb{E}(\mathbf{u}_{T}^{1})\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma(t))}\|P_{\mathcal{K}^{\perp}}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}\|_{\perp},

where we have also bounded the extra terms in (
6.9
) as

\frac{1}{2}|\mathbf{b}(\mathcal{S}^{\perp}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}},\mathcal{S}^{\perp}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}})|+|\mathbf{m}(P_{\mathcal{K}^{\perp}}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}HV_{N},\mathcal{S}^{\perp}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}})|+\sum_{i=1}^{N_{K}}|\mathbf{m}(\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}},\boldsymbol{\kappa}_{i})\mathbf{m}(\mathcal{S}^{\perp}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}},\partial^{\circ}\boldsymbol{\kappa}_{i})|\leq C\|P_{\mathcal{K}^{\perp}}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}\|_{\perp}^{2}\\
+\frac{\eta_{*}}{14}\|P_{\mathcal{K}^{\perp}}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma(t))}^{2}+\frac{\eta_{*}}{14}\|\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma(t))}^{2},

where we use (
B.1
) and the uniform bounds on
H,V_{N},\boldsymbol{\kappa}_{i},\partial^{\circ}\boldsymbol{\kappa}_{i}
appropriately.
Now recalling that
\eta_{*}\leq\eta(\cdot)\leq\eta^{*}
,
\eta^{\prime}
is bounded, and that by construction

\displaystyle\|\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma(t))}^{2}=\|P_{\mathcal{K}}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma(t))}^{2}+\|P_{\mathcal{K}^{\perp}}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma(t))}^{2},

(6.18)

one can use Young’s inequality to arrive at (
6.5
).

Now we establish (
6.6
).
Testing (
6.2
) with
P_{\mathcal{K}}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}
, we see that the
\hat{\mathbf{a}}
and
\mathbf{d}_{2}
terms vanish and one has

\mathbf{m}_{*}\left(\partial^{\circ}{\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}},P_{\mathcal{K}}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}\right)+\mathbf{c}_{1}(\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}},\mathbf{u}_{T}^{1},P_{\mathcal{K}}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}})+\mathbf{c}_{1}(\mathbf{u}_{T}^{2},\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}},P_{\mathcal{K}}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}})+\mathbf{l}(\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}},P_{\mathcal{K}}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}})+\mathbf{d}_{1}(\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}},P_{\mathcal{K}}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}})\\
=\varepsilon\mathbf{c}_{3}(\bar{\varphi},\varphi^{1},P_{\mathcal{K}}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}})+\varepsilon\mathbf{c}_{3}(\varphi^{2},\bar{\varphi},P_{\mathcal{K}}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}),

(6.19)

where we have rewritten the nonlinear terms in a more appropriate way.
As before we pull out the derivative term so that

\mathbf{m}_{*}\left(\partial^{\circ}{\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}},P_{\mathcal{K}}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}\right)=\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dt}\|P_{\mathcal{K}}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma(t))}^{2}-\mathbf{m}(\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}HV_{N},P_{\mathcal{K}}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}})+\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{m}(P_{\mathcal{K}}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}HV_{N},P_{\mathcal{K}}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}})\\
-\sum_{i=1}^{N_{K}}\mathbf{m}(\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}},\boldsymbol{\kappa}_{i})\mathbf{m}(P_{\mathcal{K}^{\perp}}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}},\partial^{\circ}\boldsymbol{\kappa}_{i}).

(6.20)

To see this, we see that

\mathbf{m}_{*}\left(\partial^{\circ}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}},P_{\mathcal{K}}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}\right)=\frac{d}{dt}\|P_{\mathcal{K}}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma(t))}^{2}-\mathbf{m}(\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}HV_{N},P_{\mathcal{K}}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}})-\mathbf{m}_{*}\left(\partial^{\circ}P_{\mathcal{K}}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}},\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}\right).

Then one writes

\mathbf{m}_{*}\left(\partial^{\circ}P_{\mathcal{K}}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}},\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}\right)=\mathbf{m}_{*}\left(\partial^{\circ}P_{\mathcal{K}}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}},P_{\mathcal{K}}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}\right)+\mathbf{m}_{*}\left(\partial^{\circ}P_{\mathcal{K}}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}},P_{\mathcal{K}^{\perp}}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}\right),

where the transport theorem lets us see that

\mathbf{m}_{*}\left(\partial^{\circ}P_{\mathcal{K}}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}},P_{\mathcal{K}}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}\right)=\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dt}\|P_{\mathcal{K}}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma(t))}^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{m}(P_{\mathcal{K}}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}HV_{N},P_{\mathcal{K}}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}).

The term involving
P_{\mathcal{K}^{\perp}}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}
is then dealt with by using (
B.5
) so that

\displaystyle\mathbf{m}_{*}\left(\partial^{\circ}P_{\mathcal{K}}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}},P_{\mathcal{K}^{\perp}}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}\right)=\sum_{i=1}^{N_{K}}\mathbf{m}(\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}},\boldsymbol{\kappa}_{i})\mathbf{m}(P_{\mathcal{K}^{\perp}}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}},\partial^{\circ}\boldsymbol{\kappa}_{i}),

and combining all this together yields (
6.20
).

Next we consider the
\mathbf{c}_{1}
terms.
We notice that

|\mathbf{c}_{1}(\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}},\mathbf{u}_{T}^{1},P_{\mathcal{K}}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}})|=|\mathbf{c}_{1}(P_{\mathcal{K}}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}},\mathbf{u}_{T}^{1},\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}})|\leq\|P_{\mathcal{K}}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}\|_{\mathbf{H}^{1,4}(\Gamma(t))}\|\mathbf{u}_{T}^{1}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{4}(\Gamma(t))}\|\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma(t))},

and by using (
B.3
) one can deduce

|\mathbf{c}_{1}(\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}},\mathbf{u}_{T}^{1},P_{\mathcal{K}}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}})|\leq C\|P_{\mathcal{K}}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma(t))}\|\mathbf{u}_{T}^{1}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{4}(\Gamma(t))}\|\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma(t))}.

The same calculations for the other
\mathbf{c}_{1}
term and Young’s inequality yields

\displaystyle|\mathbf{c}_{1}(\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}},\mathbf{u}_{T}^{1},P_{\mathcal{K}}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}})|+|\mathbf{c}_{1}(\mathbf{u}_{T}^{2},\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}},P_{\mathcal{K}}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}})|\leq\frac{\eta_{*}}{12}\|\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma(t))}^{2}+C\left(\|\mathbf{u}_{T}^{1}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{4}(\Gamma(t))}^{2}+\|\mathbf{u}_{T}^{2}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{4}(\Gamma(t))}^{2}\right)\|P_{\mathcal{K}}\bar{\mathbf{u}_{T}}\|_{\mathbf{L}^{2}(\Gamma(t))}^{2}.

(6.21)

We then turn to the terms involving
